National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 ii

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Abstract
Sponsored by the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE), the National Carbon Capture Center
(NCCC) is a cornerstone of U.S. innovation in advancing fossil energy technology solutions.
Bridging the gap between laboratory research and large-scale demonstrations, the NCCC
evaluates carbon capture processes from technology developers, focusing on the early-stage
development of the most promising, cost-effective technologies for future commercial
deployment.

Since its creation in 2009, the NCCC has achieved remarkable progress. The center has hosted a
variety of technology developers, both national and international, fostering the
commercialization of new materials and processes for power generation that can meet future
environmental standards while limiting the increased cost of electricity. These developers have
been able to utilize their testing experience at the NCCC to refine and, in many cases, scale up
their technologies. Through the staff’s diligent research and effort, the data generated at the site
has proved to be reliable and accurate despite the challenges posed by novel processes and
operating conditions.

At the end of this contract period, from June 6, 2014, through March 31, 2026, the NCCC had
completed about 170,000 hours of technology testing for carbon innovators. Through the pilot
testing and development of approximately 85 technologies, the center has already reduced the
projected cost of carbon capture by more than 40%, with additional cost savings likely in the
future.

 xii

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

1.0 Executive Summary
Sponsored by the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE), the National Carbon Capture Center
(NCCC) is a cornerstone of U.S. innovation in advancing fossil energy technology solutions.
Bridging the gap between laboratory research and large-scale demonstrations, the NCCC
evaluates carbon capture processes from technology developers, focusing on development of the
most promising, cost-effective technologies for future commercial deployment.

The NCCC provides test facilities and wide-ranging support to researchers developing lower-
cost carbon capture technologies that will enable fossil fuel-based power generation to remain a
key contributor to the energy mix. The facilities accommodate a range of equipment sizes and
operating conditions and provide commercially representative settings that allow results to be
scaled confidently to commercial application, a crucial element in shortening development times.

Project Partnership with DOE
The DOE Office of Fossil Energy (now the Hydrocarbons and Geothermal Energy Office), its
National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL), and Southern Company established the NCCC
in 2009 to become a cornerstone for U.S. leadership in advanced clean coal technology
development. After the successful completion of the first contract period, which comprised
testing and advancement of numerous carbon capture and gasification support technologies, the
DOE renewed its support of the project with a second cooperative agreement through May 2020.
In mid-2017, after completing more than 30 projects in the gasification and pre-combustion
carbon capture areas, the NCCC concluded this work in response to changes in the project scope
to focus more on post-combustion capture for natural gas and coal power generation. During the
contract period, the NCCC further expanded its scope to include CO₂ conversion and removal
technologies.

Reporting Period
This report covers the work performed during the NCCC’s second cooperative agreement with
DOE, DE-FE0022596, which covered the period from June 6, 2014, through March 31, 2026.

Project Mission and Approach
Offering a world-class neutral test facility and a highly specialized staff, the NCCC accelerates
the commercialization of advanced technologies to enable fossil-fueled power plants and
industrial sources to reduce carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions. In undertaking its mission, the
NCCC has been involved in a range of activities to develop the most promising technologies for
future commercial deployment. Researchers at the center have conducted numerous tests for the
advancement of post-combustion carbon capture for use in pulverized coal or natural gas power
plants, gasification processes, and pre-combustion carbon capture, which is suitable for use in
gasification plants. To aid in the selection of technologies to be tested, a comprehensive
inventory of candidate technologies related to carbon capture was created and is periodically
updated with new emerging technologies.

The NCCC location, shown in Figure 1, includes post-combustion carbon capture facilities
located at the Alabama Power E.C. Gaston power plant, and the adjacent site formerly housing
gasification and pre-combustion carbon capture facilities. The post-combustion facilities include

 1

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

test bays for simultaneous lab, bench- and pilot-scale operation of advanced carbon capture
technologies at commercially relevant process conditions. The site also houses two fully
integrated processes for solvent evaluations, the Pilot-Scale Test Unit (PSTU) and Slipstream
Solvent Test Unit (SSTU).

The gasification process featured the Transport Gasifier (now decommissioned) for production
of coal-derived syngas used in testing bench- and small pilot-scale gasification and pre-
combustion carbon capture processes. Technologies tested include syngas utilization processes
such as Fischer-Tropsch synthesis and syngas chemical looping, gas cleaning processes such as
mercury capture, and processes related to carbon capture, including CO₂ solvents, sorbents,
membrane, and water-gas shift and COS hydrolysis catalysts.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

1.1 Post-Combustion Carbon Capture

During the contract period, operations supported more than 90,000 hours of carbon capture
technology testing, which involved advanced solvents, membranes, enzymes, and associated
processes, as listed in Table 1 and described below. Operation of the NCCC’s small pilot-scale
PSTU and bench-scale SSTU encompassed about 40,000 testing hours, supporting the
evaluations of advanced solvents, process technologies, controls, sensing, and emissions studies
through more than 15 technology developer projects. The NCCC hosted more than 20
technology developer test skids in its pilot-, bench-, and lab-scale test areas. In addition to
enabling these technology developer projects, the NCCC conducted testing to evaluate artificial
intelligence (AI)-assisted process control automation and characterize amine emissions.

1.1.1 Process Control Automation with Ai-Ops Technology
The NCCC evaluated AI-based supervisory control using Ai-Ops’ Koios platform to improve
process efficiency, reduce costs, and enhance operational insight by automating real-time control
of key parameters such as steam flow and liquid/gas ratios in a monoethanol amine (MEA)-based
carbon capture system. Funded primarily through state of Alabama and industry sources, the
project involved data transfer, model development, and deployment of AI-driven control
integrated with existing systems, including additional capabilities for foaming prediction and
plugging detection. Commissioning in mid-2025 demonstrated successful integration and
operation over 108 hours, though early performance was impacted by instrumentation issues and
limited model training data, leading to control instability and modest accuracy (~5% deviation
from setpoint). Subsequent adjustments improved stability, and results suggest that with higher
quality training data, the system could achieve significantly tighter control (±1%), highlighting
the potential of AI-assisted automation to advance carbon capture operations.

1.1.2 Aerosol and Amine Emissions Studies
Amine emissions from carbon capture systems, strongly linked to aerosol (SO₃) presence in flue
gas, pose a significant challenge due to their extremely small particle size, which makes them
difficult to capture and leads to solvent losses. To better understand and quantify these
emissions, the NCCC implemented advanced sampling and analysis systems, including an
Electric Low Pressure Impactor (ELPI+), a Phase Doppler Interferometer (PDI), and a Fourier
Transform Infrared Spectrometer (FTIR), at key process locations to characterize particle size
distributions and amine concentrations. Baseline measurements taken before and after
installation of an upstream activated carbon injection baghouse showed dramatic improvements:
MEA emissions dropped from ~100-110 ppm to 5-10 ppm, while aerosol particle counts
decreased by several orders of magnitude and shifted to sizes mainly below 0.1 microns. These
findings confirm that upstream SO₃ removal significantly reduces aerosol formation and
associated amine emissions, highlighting its importance for commercial deployment, though
further work is needed to fully understand impacts on process conditions and overall emissions
behavior.

1.1.3 DOE Carbon Capture Simulation Initiative
Testing was conducted with MEA solvent for two campaigns supporting DOE’s Carbon Capture
Simulation Initiative (CCSI) and its second phase, CCSI2, Carbon Capture Simulation for
Industry Impact. The goal of CCSI was to significantly reduce the time required to develop and

 4

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

scale up new energy technologies. The testing resulted in a toolset for gaining additional data
and insight during carbon capture test campaigns. The CCSI toolset continues to be applied in
collaboration with technology developers and test centers to support broader deployment. For
example, the toolset has been used at Technology Centre Mongstad through collaboration with
the International Test Center Network.

1.1.4 Cansolv DC-103 and DC-201 Solvents
Testing of the Cansolv DC‑103 solvent under coal‑derived flue gas conditions included more
than 300 hours of steady‑state operation to evaluate capture performance, emissions, and
sensitivity to aerosols and acid species. The solvent consistently achieved >90% CO₂ removal in
pilot operation, with amine emissions typically below ~0.2 ppmv under stable conditions.
Process optimization indicated up to ~10% reduction in reboiler duty relative to baseline
operation while maintaining stable degradation behavior and capture performance. The
campaign provided detailed data on aerosol-driven emissions mechanisms and informed design
and operating strategies for reliable large‑scale deployment.

Operation with the Cansolv DC‑201 solvent under simulated natural gas combined-cycle
conditions focused on quantifying energy performance and process flexibility for lower‑CO₂ flue
gas streams. The solvent was designed to meet a 90% CO₂ capture efficiency target with specific
regeneration energy on the order of ~2.6–2.9 GJ/ton CO₂. Testing evaluated performance across
a range of operating conditions, generating data for model verification and process optimization.
Results confirmed the potential for lower steam demand and reduced operating costs while
maintaining high capture efficiency, supporting advancement toward commercial natural gas
combined-cycle (NGCC) applications.

1.1.5 Carbon Clean Solutions Solvent
After completing the first phase of testing of its APBS solvent using the PSTU in early 2014,
Carbon Clean Solutions returned to the NCCC in 2015 for a second phase of testing. The
evaluation included parametric optimization of liquid‑to‑gas ratio, stripper pressure, and
intercooling, with results demonstrating stable operation and high CO₂ capture while generating
detailed data on energy balance, CO₂ purity, and solvent behavior. The test campaign also
quantified amine emissions and degradation products. Based on test results, Carbon Clean
Solutions projected a minimum regeneration energy value of 1,290 Btu/lb (3.0 GJ/ton CO₂) or
less to achieve 90% CO₂ capture using a liquid/gas (L/G) ratio of 0.76 and stripper pressure of
14 psig. The solvent is currently being employed for CO₂ capture at commercial facilities in
India and Europe.

1.1.6 ION Clean Energy Solvents
ION Engineering conducted over 1,100 hours of pilot testing in 2015 to evaluate its advanced
solvent, ICE-21, against DOE performance targets, focusing on CO₂ capture efficiency and
solvent stability. Testing showed CO₂ capture rates exceeding 95% across multiple operating
conditions, with stable process and water balance maintained throughout the campaign. Energy
use was higher than modeled expectations under the selected test conditions, indicating some
operational inefficiencies. Analysis of solvent condition showed limited degradation and low
levels of heat-stable salts over the test campaign, with no significant impact on solvent

 5

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

performance. Corrosion testing and metal ion analysis also indicated lower material impact
compared to baseline MEA data.

The 2021 ION Clean Energy test campaign at the NCCC evaluated the performance of the
ICE‑31 solvent over more than 4,000 hours of pilot‑scale operation with natural gas and
coal‑derived flue gases. The results demonstrated consistently high CO₂ capture efficiencies of
at least 95%, with the ability to reach approximately 98% capture under NGCC conditions with
only modest increases in energy demand. Specific reboiler duty values were observed in the
range of about 2.5–2.7 GJ/tonne CO₂ under optimized conditions, with lower energy
requirements achieved when using heat‑integrated stripping approaches such as the Advanced
Flash Stripper. The campaign also showed strong agreement between pilot data and ION’s
process model, supporting its use for scale‑up, and included a 1,500‑hour steady‑state run that
demonstrated stable operation, minimal solvent degradation, and low emissions without the need
for solvent replacement.

1.1.7 GE Global Aminosilicone Solvent and Desorber
GE Global demonstrated its GAP‑1 aminosilicone solvent using the PSTU, showing measurable
improvements in CO₂ capture performance, energy efficiency, and projected cost relative to
conventional MEA systems. Testing achieved up to 95% CO₂ capture under optimized
conditions, with operating data confirming that solvent performance could be tuned through
water content and process configuration. While early operation of the CSTR desorber identified
challenges such as foaming and solvent losses, these were mitigated through operational
adjustments; however, the conventional steam stripping column ultimately provided more stable
operation, higher desorption efficiency, and lower solvent make-up requirements. Under
optimized conditions, the solvent delivered a 25% increase in working capacity, ~10% reduction
in steam duty, and lower desorption temperatures compared to MEA, with minimal short-term
degradation rates. Techno-economic analysis based on pilot results projected a first-year capture
cost of approximately $48/ton CO₂ (about 20% lower than MEA) primarily driven by reduced
capital costs.

1.1.8 University of Texas at Austin (UT-Austin) PZAS Process
UT-Austin conducted three major test campaigns using the PSTU to evaluate the Piperazine
Advanced Stripper (PZAS) process, which features the advanced flash stripper for solvent
regeneration and aqueous piperazine solvent. The campaigns focused on process performance,
solvent stability, and system behavior under long-duration commercially representative operating
conditions, providing pilot-scale data to support evaluation of scale-up considerations.

The first campaign, the result of collaboration among UT-Austin, Trimeric Corporation, and the
URS Group, comprised more than 2,100 hours of operation using coal-derived flue gas in 2018
with generally stable performance while achieving heat duties of about 1.9–2.3 GJ/tonne CO₂ at
90% capture. This was lower than the corresponding operation with the PSTU’s original solvent
regenerator, a simple stripper, which ranged from 3.4 to 4.0 GJ/tonne under similar conditions.
The system also demonstrated the ability to reach up to 99% CO₂ removal with a modest
increase in energy requirement. Measured solvent degradation remained relatively low, and
emissions were maintained below 1 ppm under typical operating conditions, although higher
sulfur trioxide (SO₃) levels required additional operational adjustments to control aerosol-related

 6

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

emissions. Instances of solids formation and corrosion were observed but were limited and
dependent on operating conditions and equipment location. The results provide comparative
performance data indicating improved energy efficiency relative to conventional stripping while
also identifying operational factors and material considerations relevant to scale-up.

In 2019, UT-Austin, with funding from the CO₂ Capture Project Phase 4 (CCP4, an industry
consortium advancing large-scale carbon capture technologies), conducted the second major test
campaign with the PZAS process. This campaign focused on performance metrics and
operational observations under NGCC conditions. Heat duty values ranged from approximately
2.18 to 2.37 GJ/tonne CO₂, depending on solvent loading and correction methods, with higher
CO₂ removal levels under NGCC conditions requiring increased energy input compared to lower
removal targets. The absorber achieved 89–95% CO₂ removal, with performance dependent on
rich loading and intercooling conditions, and analysis suggested that comparable performance
could be maintained with reduced packing height. Solvent oxidation rates increased relative to
earlier coal-based testing due to higher oxygen concentrations, leading to greater ammonia
production and degradation, while carbon bed treatment influenced both oxidation behavior and
impurity levels. Corrosion testing showed that material performance varied significantly, with
316L stainless steel and carbon steel exhibiting elevated corrosion rates under high-temperature
conditions, while 304 and 2205 stainless steels performed more favorably. Amine emissions
remained below 0.3 ppm for most of the campaign but increased under specific operating
changes associated with hotter flue gas conditions. Overall, the results characterized the
relationships between operating conditions, energy use, solvent stability, and material durability
in lower-CO₂ flue gas environments.

The third test campaign was conducted in 2023 to study solvent oxidation and evaluate
mitigation strategies under extended pilot-scale operation. The PZAS process ran for
approximately 5,900 hours, demonstrating stable performance and sustained CO₂ capture of
~95–98%, with a net heat duty of about 2.43 GJ/tonne CO₂ under NGCC-like conditions. The
results showed that solvent degradation driven primarily by nitrogen dioxide (NO₂) and
dissolved oxygen remains a key operational challenge, with a distinct tipping point observed
after prolonged operation leading to rapid increases in ammonia emissions and degradation
byproducts. Among mitigation strategies tested, thermal reclaiming was the most effective at
restoring solvent condition and reducing emissions by removing degradation products and
dissolved metals, while NO₂ control and nitrogen sparging provided measurable but incremental
reductions in oxidation rates. Emissions control testing showed that acid wash systems can
significantly reduce ammonia emissions to near-background levels.

1.1.9 Susteon Ionic Liquid-Catalyzed MEA Solvent
Susteon conducted pilot-scale testing of an ionic liquid-catalyzed MEA solvent using the PSTU
in 2022, completing a series of parametric tests comparing baseline 30 wt% MEA performance
with and without the catalyst under both coal- and NGCC-like flue gas conditions. The results
showed that the catalyzed system maintained high CO₂ capture performance comparable to
conventional MEA while influencing absorption and desorption kinetics. Normalized
comparisons of selected runs at common amine concentrations (23–31 wt.%) indicated that the
solvent regeneration duty for the catalyzed system was approximately 11–12% lower than that of
baseline MEA under comparable NGCC operating conditions, with the magnitude of

 7

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

improvement dependent on operating conditions and process integration. The testing also
provided pilot-scale data for model validation and performance normalization, supporting
evaluation of catalyst-enhanced solvents as a potential process improvement approach while
identifying the need for further optimization and assessment of system performance.

1.1.10 ExxonMobil/Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (MHI) Solvents
ExxonMobil, in collaboration with MHI, conducted testing of two amine-based solvents in 2024,
the commercial KS-21™ and a novel Solvent B in the PSTU to collect data on emissions and
energy consumption. The KS-21 solvent was tested for 590 hours, and Solvent B was tested for
850 hours. Testing included both parametric operation to assess sensitivity to process variables
and extended degradation testing to evaluate solvent stability over time. Solvent B demonstrated
less performance variability in response to changes in the liquid-to-gas (L/G) ratio or flue gas
turndown than KS-21, presumably due to Solvent B’s improved reaction kinetics and absorption
efficiency. Solvent B also showed improved trends in degradation compared to KS-21.

Building on the insights gained from these initial campaigns, the ExxonMobil/MHI team
returned to the NCCC in 2025 to test an additional solvent formulation using the PSTU,
achieving approximately 2,100 hours of operation under natural gas-derived flue gas conditions
(4–8% CO₂). The results demonstrated that the solvent achieved target CO₂ capture performance
at reduced L/G ratios, indicating improved absorption efficiency and reaction kinetics, while
regeneration performance showed a predictable dependence on steam input and CO₂
concentration. Extended operation confirmed stable system performance with no observable
degradation in capture capability, and degradation testing indicated lower overall solvent
degradation rates, strongly influenced by operating conditions such as temperature, pressure, and
the presence of oxidizing species (oxygen and NO₂). These findings provide pilot-scale
validation of improved process efficiency and solvent durability and support continued
development of advanced solvent formulations for post-combustion CO₂ capture.

1.1.11 EPRI/Pacific Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL)/RTI International Water-
 Lean Solvent
EPRI, Pacific Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL), RTI International, and their project
collaborators developed an engineering-scale test of EEMPA (N-(2-ethoxyethyl)-3-
morpholinopropan-1-amine) solvent using the PSTU. The primary objective of this test was to
collect long-term data operating EEMPA with both coal- and natural gas-representative flue
gases at the 0.5 MWe-equivalent scale. This project represented the largest-scale test of EEMPA
as a water-lean solvent, accumulating over 2,300 hours of operation. Water content was
effectively controlled across a wide range through adjustment of inlet gas temperature and
regenerator pressure, with stable operation maintained even at elevated water levels despite
increased viscosity. Heat exchanger performance was identified as a critical factor, as small
temperature deviations significantly impacted solvent loading capacity and overall energy
efficiency. Higher-than-expected solvent degradation and declining solvent concentration
highlighted the importance of solvent purity and tight control of regeneration temperatures.
Additional challenges included potential aerosol formation, seal compatibility issues in pumps,
and the need for improved understanding of degradation mechanisms and materials performance
under realistic operating conditions.

 8

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

1.1.12 University of Edinburgh Solvent Analyzer
The University of Edinburgh developed and tested a real-time solvent analysis device capable of
determining solvent concentration and CO₂ loading within approximately 10 seconds,
representing a substantial improvement over conventional methods that can take up to 30
minutes. The instrument uses inferential analysis to provide continuous, online measurements
and offers advantages such as simple calibration, lower capital and maintenance costs, and
compatibility with automated control systems. During field validation, the analyzer produced
results consistent with standard laboratory titration methods, demonstrating strong accuracy and
reliability. Subsequent extended testing at a commercial facility confirmed its robustness, with
the device successfully tracking changes in operating conditions and providing actionable data to
improve process control without introducing significant measurement uncertainty.

1.1.13 Cansolv Aerosol Mitigation Processes
This Cansolv project evaluated technologies to mitigate amine losses by targeting the formation
and removal of fine aerosol particles that are difficult to capture and can be emitted from the
carbon capture system. Two approaches were tested in the SSTU: a Thermal Swing Adsorber
(TSA) to remove aerosol precursors such as SO₂ and SO₃ from the incoming gas, and a
Brownian Demister Unit (BDU) to remove aerosols from the treated gas stream. Testing with
MEA showed that operation of the TSA had minimal impact on overall aerosol number and size
distribution under the conditions evaluated, although changes in operating conditions influenced
the formation of very fine particles (<0.03 µm). Evaluation of the BDU was inconclusive due to
measurement issues, including liquid accumulation and unstable readings, preventing reliable
assessment of its performance. Results indicated that aerosol behavior is highly sensitive to
operating conditions and that further testing is required to determine the effectiveness of these
mitigation technologies.

1.1.14 RTI International Solvent Non-Aqueous Solvent Process
RTI International conducted testing of its non‑aqueous solvent CO₂ capture process using the
SSTU in 2018, completing approximately 580 hours of operation on coal-derived flue gas. The
testing focused on solvent stability, emissions, and operability under realistic conditions,
including parametric runs and periods with SO₃ injection to evaluate aerosol effects. Results
showed that the system could be operated continuously over extended periods, while also
highlighting the influence of aerosols on solvent emissions, the need to manage water
accumulation, and the sensitivity of performance and emissions to operating conditions. Overall,
the campaign provided data on solvent behavior and system operation in real flue gas that are
relevant to assessing scale‑up considerations.

1.1.15 NETL Fiber Optic-Based Sensors
NETL is evaluating fiber‑optic sensing technologies at the NCCC to support real‑time
monitoring of carbon capture solvent degradation under representative operating conditions.
These systems use advanced material‑coated optical fibers to continuously detect process
conditions along the sensing line; the sensors are capable of monitoring both liquids, such as
identifying solvent degradation, and gases, including measuring CO₂ concentrations in flue gas.
Compared with conventional sensors, they enable wide‑area, high‑resolution monitoring within a
single installation, supporting monitoring, reporting, and verification needs for carbon

 9

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

management. NETL is assessing the technology using MEA solvent. Testing began in the third
quarter of 2025 and will continue through June 2026.

1.1.16 Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory (LLNL) 3D-Printed Structured Column
 Packing
LLNL has developed advanced structured packings based on additively manufactured
geometries. These 3D-printed packings improve mass transfer rate with minimum tradeoffs on
pressure drop and maximum liquid loading. LLNL has demonstrated the performance of these
packings in the lab and has scaled them up to further validate their performance in the SSTU
using MEA solvent. Testing, which is being conducted in parallel with NETL’s sensor
technology campaign, began in the fourth quarter of 2025 and will continue through July 2026.

1.1.17 Membrane Technology and Research (MTR) 1-Ton/Day (TPD) Membrane System
MTR designed, built, and operated a 1‑ton/day CO₂ membrane system at the NCCC to evaluate
its Polaris™ polymeric membrane technology for post‑combustion carbon capture from
coal‑derived flue gas. The system used a two‑stage process, with cross‑flow vacuum modules
for initial CO₂ enrichment followed by sweep‑gas modules to achieve overall capture targets
exceeding 90%. Field testing in 2014 and 2015 demonstrated stable, long‑duration operation
under real plant conditions, including shutdowns, restarts, and variations in temperature and flow
rate. The system consistently enriched CO₂ from ~12% in the feed to 50-70% in the first stage
and reduced treated flue gas CO₂ to roughly 2-5%, with performance influenced primarily by
feed temperature and flow conditions. Overall, the campaign accumulated more than 11,000
operating hours and provided key data to validate membrane durability and guide the design of
larger‑scale (20‑TPD) membrane systems.

1.1.18 MTR 20-TPD Membrane System
MTR installed and operated the scaled‑up 20‑ton/day CO₂ membrane system at the NCCC to
validate larger‑scale performance of the Polaris membrane technology. The system employed a
two‑stage configuration similar to the 1‑TPD unit, with the addition of a high‑efficiency
plate‑and‑frame sweep module in the second stage that replaced earlier spiral‑wound modules to
improve performance. Testing across 2015 campaigns demonstrated stable operation in both
winter and summer conditions, achieving CO₂ capture rates typically around 85–90% (and
exceeding 90% under certain conditions) while enriching CO₂ from ~11–12% in the feed to
~45% in the permeate and reducing treated flue gas to ~2% CO₂. The plate‑and‑frame design
significantly reduced pressure drop—by roughly a factor of four compared to spiral modules—
offering meaningful energy savings at commercial scale. Overall, the system accumulated over
1,200 hours of operation on flue gas, confirming scalability, durability, and agreement with
design predictions, and providing key data for further technology advancement and subsequent
pilot demonstrations.

1.1.19 Linde-BASF Solvent Process
Linde and BASF jointly developed and demonstrated an advanced solvent‑based
post‑combustion CO₂ capture process at the NCCC, integrating BASF’s OASE® blue amine
solvent with Linde’s process design and engineering innovations. The 1.5‑MWe pilot plant was
operated from 2015 through 2016, including extensive parametric testing and a 1,500‑hour

 10

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

continuous campaign, accumulating over 6,700 total operating hours and more than 4,000 hours
on coal‑derived flue gas. Testing validated key performance targets, including CO₂ capture rates
above 90%, high product purity (>99.9%), and reduced regeneration energy as low as
~2.7 GJ/tonne CO₂, significantly lower than conventional MEA systems. The campaign also
demonstrated stable long‑term solvent performance with minimal degradation, effective
emissions control, and favorable operability under varying flue gas conditions, supporting
scale‑up of the technology for commercial applications.

1.1.20 GTI Energy Hollow-Fiber Membrane Contactor
GTI Energy conducted testing at the NCCC to evaluate a 0.5‑MWe‑equivalent hollow-fiber
membrane contactor (HFMC) system, scaled up from prior bench‑scale lab work to a pilot unit
with 28 commercial modules, each about 8 inches in diameter and 5 feet long. The technology
uses polyether ether ketone to form super‑hydrophobic porous hollow fibers that enable flue gas
to pass inside the fibers while a reactive amine solvent flows outside, combining membrane
diffusion and absorption to achieve high mass‑transfer rates in a compact design. The campaign
included extended testing over multiple operating periods from 2017 through 2022, for a total of
more than 3,000 hours. Initial results with a subset of modules achieved about 90% CO₂ capture
and >98% purity on coal‑fired flue gas. However, when operated at full scale for longer
durations, performance declined due to mechanical integrity issues such as tubesheet leakage and
possible fiber bonding defects, which reduced capture efficiency over time. The project
concluded that additional membrane fabrication and module hardware development are required
before advancing to larger demonstration‑scale systems.

1.1.21 TDA Research Alkalized Alumina Sorbent System
TDA Research, Inc. conducted pilot-scale testing of an alkalized alumina sorbent system at the
NCCC using a 0.5 MWe slipstream unit, accumulating nearly 3,000 hours of operation, and
demonstrated that the technology can achieve 90% CO₂ capture with 95% product purity on both
coal-derived and simulated natural gas flue gas streams. The multi-bed, near-isothermal process
enabled efficient sorbent utilization and relatively low regeneration energy, supporting a
projected capture cost of about $34.9 per tonne of CO₂, below DOE cost targets and lower than
baseline amine systems. Testing also confirmed the benefits of process features such as purge
and steam management steps in improving efficiency. However, pilot operation identified key
challenges related to sorbent manufacturing quality and long-term stability, including capacity
loss associated with material defects and moisture sensitivity, which required sorbent
reprocessing and replacement during the test program. The results demonstrated the technical
viability and potential cost advantage of the sorbent-based approach while highlighting the need
for improved sorbent durability for commercial deployment.

1.1.22 Air Liquide Hollow-Fiber Cold Membrane
Air Liquide’s hollow-fiber cold membrane technology was tested at the NCCC over multiple
campaigns spanning approximately 2015 through 2019, including both earlier PI‑1 membrane
validation and later next‑generation PI‑2 scale-up efforts at the 0.3-MWe field test unit. Across
this period, the project accumulated about 5,800 hours of operation on real coal-derived flue gas,
providing a robust dataset for performance and durability evaluation. The testing demonstrated
stable long-term membrane operation, including extended steady-state runs exceeding several

 11

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

hundred hours, while consistently achieving around 90% CO₂ recovery with permeate CO₂
concentrations near or above 60% for larger bundles. Scale-up to 6-inch modules met or
exceeded throughput targets, confirming the viability of commercial scale bundle fabrication and
operation. Overall, the results showed that sub-ambient membrane operation can reliably pre-
concentrate CO₂ at high recovery, while the improved PI‑2 material delivers higher productivity,
supporting reduced system size and contributing to projected capture costs in line with program
targets.

1.1.23 Carbon America FrostCC Cryogenic Process
Carbon America tested the FrostCC cryogenic carbon capture system at the NCCC in 2024 using
an engineering-scale pilot that progressed from phased subsystem validation to fully integrated
continuous operation, ultimately accumulating over 1,000 hours of flue gas testing. The testing
demonstrated stable, long-duration operation of the complete system, including compression,
expansion, heat exchange, and cyclic frosting/defrosting of CO₂, confirming the viability of the
self-refrigerating cryogenic process. The system achieved very high performance, with CO₂
capture efficiencies up to 99% and product purity near 99.97%, while simultaneously removing
co-pollutants such as NOₓ and SO₂ to very low levels. Results also showed strong capture
productivity and close agreement between measured data and model predictions.

1.1.24 SRI International Mixed-Salt Process
The NCCC hosted engineering‑scale testing of SRI International’s Mixed‑Salt Process (MSP), an
advanced post‑combustion carbon capture technology that uses a dual‑salt solvent system of
ammonium and potassium carbonate to efficiently absorb and regenerate CO₂ from flue gas
streams while minimizing energy use and emissions. Testing of the 0.5‑MWe pilot system began
in 2025 and continued through a broader program expected to conclude in the second quarter of
2026, generating operational and techno‑economic data under real‑world conditions to support
scale‑up and commercialization. Baker Hughes, which holds the exclusive license for MSP
technology, participated as a key industry partner and conducted emissions‑related evaluation
activities as part of the program, supporting verification of the process’s low‑emissions
performance.

1.1.25 KC8 Capture Technologies UNO MK 3 Solvent Process
KC8 Capture Technologies began pilot-scale testing of its UNO MK3 solvent at the NCCC in
2025, with the campaign still underway and expected to continue through the end of 2026. The
UNO MK3 system is an advanced post-combustion carbon capture technology based on a
precipitating (phase-change) solvent that forms a solid phase when absorbing CO₂, enabling
higher loading capacity and potentially reducing the energy required for solvent regeneration
compared to conventional amine systems. This design aims to improve overall efficiency, lower
operating costs, and reduce equipment size while maintaining stability under real flue gas
conditions, including exposure to common contaminants. The ongoing NCCC testing is intended
to demonstrate long-term operability, solvent durability, and practical performance at scale.

1.1.26 Trimeric/UT-Austin Nitrogen Dioxide (NO₂) Scrubbing
Trimeric and UT-Austin completed testing of a chemical process for removal of NO₂ upstream
of amine-based solvent systems in order to minimize solvent losses. The process involves the

 12

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

addition of the low-cost chemical thiosulfate to aqueous sodium hydroxide in an SO₂ pre-
scrubber. Testing showed that the NO₂ removal rate strongly correlated with the sulfite
concentration, which was controlled by the amount of thiosulfate added. Greater than 95%
removal of NO₂ was achieved.

1.1.27 SRI International Sorbent
SRI International’s 2014 testing at the NCCC evaluated a 40‑kWe bench-scale system using
circulating carbon microbead sorbents. The campaign ran from late 2013 through mid‑2014 and
accumulated approximately 250 hours of operation, providing initial validation under real flue
gas conditions. The system demonstrated continuous operation but achieved about 67% CO₂
capture efficiency and ~93% product purity, below design targets due mainly to elevated
adsorber temperatures, insufficient gas residence time, and heat transfer limitations. Testing also
revealed important mechanical and process challenges—such as sorbent flow instability,
pressure control issues, and higher-than-expected steam demand from heat losses—while
confirming that these shortcomings were largely engineering-related rather than fundamental
sorbent limitations. Overall, the results provided critical scale-up insights and supported
development of a more efficient solid sorbent CO₂ capture system.

1.1.28 DOE Sorbents
The DOE Carbon Capture Unit was developed to evaluate CO₂ capture using amine-
functionalized solid sorbents, specifically polyethylenimine supported on silica, in a fluidized-
bed system designed for both absorption and regeneration. After initial multi-year operation at
NETL in both circulating and batch modes under varying conditions, the unit was tested at the
NCCC in 2014 with a focus on trace metal accumulation, particularly selenium, on the sorbent.
During early 2014, the unit operated for about 44 hours in circulating mode, where CO₂ capture
efficiency declined over time due to incomplete sorbent regeneration. Subsequent testing from
June through August was conducted in batch mode without regeneration, accumulating over
346 hours under simulated natural gas conditions and 282 hours under coal-fired conditions.
Post-testing thermo-gravimetric analysis showed no permanent loss in CO₂ capture capacity,
while chemical analyses indicated trace element concentrations well below hazardous waste
limits.

1.1.29 Carbon Capture Scientific Gas Pressurized Stripping
Carbon Capture Scientific, LLC, and partners tested a bench‑scale gas pressurized stripping
(GPS) CO₂ capture process at the NCCC, completing parametric and long‑term testing in 2015
with a total of 1,145 hours of flue gas operation. The GPS system uses an amine-based blended
solvent and uniquely releases CO₂ at elevated pressure and high purity in the stripper, reducing
downstream compression needs. Testing demonstrated the impacts of operating conditions such
as flue gas flow rate, gas-to-liquid ratio, stripper pressure, and nitrogen sweep on CO₂ removal
and purity, and confirmed the ability to achieve about 90% CO₂ capture with high-purity product
during extended operation from May through August 2015. Results also showed relatively low
energy consumption compared to conventional MEA systems, supporting the potential of the
process for improved efficiency and further optimization.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

1.1.30 Ohio State University (OSU) Membranes
OSU conducted three test campaigns at the NCCC for membrane development. The 2015 OSU
membrane test campaign at the NCCC evaluated spiral‑wound inorganic/polymer composite
membrane modules under coal‑derived flue gas conditions. The study tested three modules for
approximately 96 to 208 hours each, demonstrating CO₂ permeance around 800 gas permeation
units (GPU) and CO₂/N₂ selectivity ranging from about 150 to 270, results that were consistent
with prior laboratory testing using simulated gas. Overall, the membranes showed stable
permeance and reasonable short‑term durability in the presence of flue gas impurities such as O₂,
SO₂, and NOₓ, though performance degradation in selectivity was observed following shutdown
and restart events due primarily to module fabrication issues such as sealing, glue curing, and
mechanical deformation rather than intrinsic membrane material limitations.

The 2018 OSU membrane test campaign represented a significant advancement over earlier field
trials by demonstrating extended-duration operation, improved membrane module design, and
enhanced CO₂ capture performance under realistic flue gas conditions. The study tested three
modules, including a principal module operated for approximately 500 hours, demonstrating
stable performance despite operational disturbances such as flue gas shutdowns and flow
fluctuations, with recovery following these events. Under optimized conditions (67 °C and 4 atm
feed pressure), the membranes achieved CO₂ permeance of about 1450 GPU and CO₂/N₂
selectivity near 185, along with CO₂ capture rates exceeding 40% and permeate purities of
approximately 95% or greater. The results were consistent with lab testing and confirmed both
performance reproducibility and resistance to degradation from typical flue gas impurities.

The OSU 2022 membrane test campaign at the NCCC focused on demonstrating an integrated,
multi-stage membrane system rather than individual modules, representing a key step toward
practical deployment of membrane-based carbon capture. The testing used advanced facilitated
transport membranes incorporated into a bench-scale skid and evaluated performance with both
simulated and real flue gas under a range of operating conditions. The system achieved high
CO₂ capture efficiencies on the order of roughly 60–90% with product purities exceeding 95%,
while maintaining stable operation and consistent performance across extended testing periods.
The results confirmed successful scale-up of membrane materials and modules, demonstrated the
effectiveness of multi-stage configurations for deep CO₂ capture, and showed strong tolerance to
flue gas impurities.

1.1.31 NETL Membrane Materials
NETL has conducted multiple bench‑scale membrane test campaigns at the NCCC since 2015,
achieving over 10,000 hours of testing while systematically evaluating a range of polymeric,
composite, and mixed‑matrix membranes under real coal‑derived flue gas conditions. Using
controlled permeation systems fed by slipstream gas, these campaigns have generated consistent
findings that membrane performance under realistic conditions is lower and more complex than
laboratory predictions due to the effects of water vapor, contaminants, and multicomponent gas
interactions. Across successive campaigns, bench‑scale testing has provided critical data on CO₂
permeance, selectivity, and short‑term stability, enabling comparison of materials and
identification of degradation mechanisms while supporting down‑selection and model validation.
The results have demonstrated the importance of field‑relevant testing, showing that even
promising membranes require further optimization to maintain performance in the presence of

 14

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

moisture and impurities, and establishing NCCC bench‑scale testing as a key step in advancing
membrane technologies toward larger‑scale validation.

1.1.32 Akermin Enzymes
In 2016, Akermin conducted a bench‑scale test campaign at the NCCC to scale up its
enzyme‑enhanced solvent system, which uses carbonic anhydrase immobilized in polymeric
micro-particles to accelerate CO₂ absorption. Initial testing showed a rapid improvement in
capture efficiency—from about 48% without catalyst to as high as 93% after biocatalyst
addition—but performance was not sustained due to loss and accumulation of particles within
the system. As particle concentration declined, CO₂ capture dropped, and operational issues
such as uncontrolled spillover and poor absorber efficiency led to instability. Over about 20 days
of operation, the system achieved an average capture of roughly 70%, with only brief periods
near the 90% target. Ultimately, challenges with particle management, solvent conditions, and
equipment performance prevented stable operation, indicating the need for significant design
improvements.

1.1.33 Precision Combustion Inc. (PCI) Microlith Sorbent
The PCI test campaign evaluated a structured sorbent carbon capture system using a Microlith
mesh coated with a nanosorbent across three bench-scale campaigns in 2020, 2022, and 2023.
Testing progressed from initial shakedown with a first-generation system on coal-derived flue
gas to extended performance validation of an optimized second-generation design on natural gas
flue gas, demonstrating stable cyclic adsorption-desorption operation, rapid CO₂ uptake and
regeneration, low pressure drop, and improved mass and heat transfer characteristics. Results
showed consistent performance over multiple cycles, enhanced sorbent utilization, and clear
gains in efficiency and operability between system generations, confirming the technology’s
ability to achieve high-throughput carbon capture with reduced energy demand.

1.1.34 GTI Energy ROTA-CAP Rotating Packed-Bed Solvent Process
GTI tested the ROTA‑CAP process at the NCCC over multiple campaigns conducted between
2021 and 2023, accumulating more than 1,600 total operating hours. The integrated rotating
packed-bed absorber and regenerator system demonstrated stable, continuous operation at
capacities up to about 0.5 tonne/day of CO₂ capture, achieving greater than 95% CO₂ removal
efficiency and over 95% product purity across a wide range of inlet CO₂ concentrations (roughly
4-22%). Long-duration campaigns operated 24/7 for several weeks at a time confirmed
consistent performance, effective solvent regeneration, and reliable handling of real flue gas
impurities, while also validating the intensified mass transfer benefits of the rotating packed-bed
design and supporting techno-economic conclusions indicating reduced cost relative to
conventional solvent systems.

1.1.35 GTI Energy Graphene Oxide-Based Membrane
GTI Energy’s graphene oxide (GO) membrane test campaign, conducted in 2023 and 2024,
evaluated a bench-scale membrane-based CO₂ capture process that integrates high selectivity
(GO‑1) and high-flux (GO‑2) hollow-fiber membranes into a multi-stage configuration. Testing
demonstrated successful scale-up of the membranes to module sizes up to ~1,000 cm² while
maintaining strong performance, including CO₂ permeance on the order of 1,000-2,500 GPU and

 15

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

selectivity ranging from ≥20 to >200 depending on membrane type. Integrated system testing
achieved approximately 70-90% CO₂ removal with product purity of at least 95% during steady-
state operation, while also showing stable performance across repeated startup/shutdown cycles
and under exposure to simulated flue gas contaminants at typical operating temperatures. The
results confirmed that the GO-based membrane system can deliver high separation efficiency in
a compact, modular process, with demonstrated durability and strong potential for reducing
capture cost relative to conventional solvent systems.

1.1.36 CORMETECH Sorbent
CORMETECH is conducting a bench-scale test campaign at the NCCC to advance the
development and optimization of a monolithic amine contactor system for carbon capture applied
to NGCC units. Test campaign objectives include studying the impacts of nitrogen oxides and
sulfur dioxide on sorbent performance, demonstrating a minimum of one month continuous,
steady-state operation achieving >95% carbon capture efficiency and >95% CO₂ purity, and
evaluating the impact of dynamic operation on system performance. Following initial parametric
testing operation in late 2024, CORMETECH performed additional flue gas testing in 2025. The
team has made modifications and will resume testing in the third quarter of 2026 with new
sorbents for improved performance.

1.1.37 Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute (RPI)/State University of New York at Buffalo
 (SUNY Buffalo) Sorbents
RPI and SUNY Buffalo (University at Buffalo, The State University of New York) are
developing a transformational molecular layer deposition tailor-made, size-sieving sorbent and
using a pressure swing adsorption process developed by University of South Carolina for CO₂
capture. The sorbent is designed to have high CO₂ adsorption capacity and excellent stability in
the presence of water vapor.

RPI and SUNY Buffalo, in collaboration with the University of South Carolina and GTI Energy,
developed and tested a molecular layer deposition (MLD)–modified Ti‑13X sorbent integrated
with a proprietary pressure swing adsorption (PSA) process for CO₂ capture from flue gas. The
tailored sorbent demonstrated improved working capacity, enhanced CO₂/N₂ selectivity, reduced
heat of adsorption, and strong tolerance to water vapor and contaminants compared to
conventional 13X. Bench‑scale testing at the NCCC using a three‑bed PSA system showed that,
under simulated coal flue gas conditions, the technology achieved over 92% CO₂ capture
efficiency with greater than 96% purity and stable operation over 1,000 hours, with minimal
sensitivity to water content. Performance declined under lower CO₂ concentrations
representative of NGCC conditions but remained strong for higher‑concentration industrial
streams, achieving over 96% purity and 70% capture efficiency with stable operation over
extended testing. Across more than eight months of testing with multiple flue gas compositions,
the Ti‑13X sorbent maintained durability without requiring any additional regeneration beyond
standard PSA cycling. highlighting its potential for reliable, lower‑cost CO₂ capture,
particularly for coal and industrial applications, and supporting future scale‑up efforts.

 16

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

1.2 Carbon Conversion and Removal

The NCCC hosted one direct air capture (DAC) project and three carbon conversion projects,
which are listed in Table 2 and summarized below.

1.2.1 Southern States Energy Board (SSEB)/Aircapture Sorbent-Based DAC Process
The SSEB/Aircapture DAC test campaign at the NCCC was conducted as part of the DAC
RECO2UP project over a multi‑year period spanning 2020 through July 2024, with field
operations concentrated in the later project phases following system construction. The testing
involved five distinct campaigns totaling more than 3,300 hours of operation, providing an
extended dataset under commercially relevant conditions. The program evaluated a solid‑amine
adsorption-desorption system integrated with energy recovery, demonstrating the production of
high‑purity carbon dioxide streams and generating operational, economic, and lifecycle data to
support scale‑up. Overall, the testing confirmed sustained system performance in an industrial
environment while identifying pathways for improved efficiency, cost reduction, and commercial
deployment of DAC technology.

1.2.2 Southern Research Ethane-to-Ethylene Process
Southern Research completed a field-scale demonstration of a catalytic process converting
ethane and CO₂ derived from flue gas into ethylene at the NCCC in 2021. The test campaign
included integration of a skid-mounted reactor system with NCCC infrastructure, enabling
extended testing with both concentrated captured CO₂ and direct flue gas streams. Field
operation accumulated over 500 hours of run time using captured CO₂, demonstrating stable
catalyst performance, sustained ethylene production, and resilience to operational interruptions
under realistic conditions. A shorter operational duration, approximately 100 hours, was
achieved with flue gas. Results showed that process performance was strongest with high-purity
CO₂ streams, while oxygen and moisture present in untreated flue gas reduced conversion
efficiency and product selectivity. Overall, the test campaign confirmed the technical feasibility
of the process at pilot scale and highlighted both the operational stability of the catalyst system
and the importance of feed gas quality for achieving favorable performance and economics.

1.2.3 University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) Reversa™
UCLA conducted two major test campaigns at NCCC, first in 2021 to demonstrate initial
feasibility of the Reversa carbonation curing process using flue gas, and again in 2024 to build
on those results by expanding product types and further optimizing process performance. The

 17

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

second campaign leveraged lessons learned from the 2021 demonstration to advance scalability,
efficiency, and commercial readiness of the technology.

The 2021 test campaign demonstrated the carbonation curing process in which concrete masonry
units were produced using calcium-rich materials and exposed to CO₂ from flue gas during
curing, resulting in the formation of carbonate binding phases. Over a multi-week campaign,
more than 5,000 concrete blocks were manufactured using conventional production methods
adapted for CO₂ curing, with measured properties indicating comparable strength and durability
to standard products while incorporating CO₂ into the material matrix.

The 2024 test campaign demonstrated sustained, continuous production of multiple carbonated
concrete products under realistic flue gas conditions. Over a multi-week period, the system
successfully produced concrete masonry units, segmental retaining wall blocks, and manhole
structures at multi-ton scale while maintaining consistent CO₂ uptake and stable process
performance. The campaign showed that the process can accommodate different product
geometries without loss of efficiency or material quality, achieving uniform carbonation and
meeting standard performance requirements across all product types. Continuous operation with
real plant flue gas further confirmed system robustness, indicating that the process can maintain
throughput, consistency, and reliability over extended durations and under variable operating

1.2.4 Helios-NRG Algae Production
Helios-NRG completed a three-month test campaign with its novel algae process for carbon
capture and utilization. Operation took place in summer 2022, primarily from mid-June through
the end of August. During this period, the multi-stage continuous algae system processed flue
gas from Plant Gaston Unit 5 for more than 900 total hours, including a continuous run
exceeding 100 hours on flue gas derived from coal only. The test campaign evaluated system
performance under outdoor conditions, with monitoring of environmental and process
parameters such as solar intensity, temperature, gas composition, and biomass productivity. The
system achieved a CO₂ capture efficiency of 87.2 percent and an average algae productivity of
30.7 g/m²/day, exceeding project targets of 80 percent capture efficiency and 25 g/m²/day
productivity. The campaign demonstrated sustained operation, stable performance, and effective
CO₂ capture under representative power plant conditions.

1.3 Gasification and Pre-Combustion Carbon Capture

The gasification process operated in five different test runs for a total of 4,000 hours.
Gasification operation supported more than 17,000 hours of testing of gasification-related and
pre-combustion CO₂ capture developer technologies, as listed in Table 3. The gasifier fuels
included Mississippi lignite, Powder River Basin (PRB) subbituminous coal, and biomass.
Accomplishments of the gasification and pre-combustion projects are given below.

1.3.1 Southern Research Fischer-Tropsch (F-T) Catalyst and High-Temperature
 Reformer
The Southern Research F-T catalyst process for converting syngas into liquid fuel eliminates the
conventional product upgrading and refining steps and enhances the ability of coal-to-liquids and
coal/biomass-to-liquids processes to compete with petroleum-based processes. Following initial
testing at the NCCC, Southern Research incorporated a 2‑to‑1 scale-up of the F-T reactor and
began parallel tests of a high-temperature steam reformer.

The reformer, operating upstream of the F-T catalyst in a commercial process for syngas pre-
conditioning, showed 90% methane conversion and greater than 95% conversion of tars, with the
exit gas containing the desired 2-to-1 hydrogen-to-carbon monoxide (CO) ratio. The F-T
catalyst exceeded project goals, achieving 75% selectivity for jet-fuel-range hydrocarbons, and
performing equally well with 100% coal feed in the NCCC gasifier and with 20% biomass co-
feed. A techno-economic analysis indicated significant capital cost and product cost reductions
with the technology, but competition with petroleum-based liquids will require improvements in
other major coal- and coal/biomass-to-liquids plant unit operations.

1.3.2 Johnson Matthey Mercury Sorbent
A high-temperature palladium-based mercury sorbent from Johnson Matthey was tested as a
method to remove mercury while retaining the high efficiency of warm gas conditions. Two
levels of palladium loading were evaluated, and the sorbent achieved greater than 99% capture of
mercury, selenium, and arsenic in both sweet and sour syngas conditions.

 19

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

1.3.3 MHI Water-Gas Shift and Carbonyl Sulfide Hydrolysis Catalysts
More than 7,500 hours of testing was achieved with a developmental catalyst from MHI to
evaluate long-term stability, and results showed no significant degradation of the catalyst.
Conversion of carbon monoxide (CO) was typically around 70% with syngas produced from
PRB coal. Higher conversions of up to 90% were achieved while the gasifier operated with
lignite, as the higher moisture fuel increased the syngas steam-to-CO ratio.

Multiple tests were conducted with MHI’s carbonyl sulfide (COS) hydrolysis catalyst to
facilitate sulfur removal from syngas. The catalyst, which features a honeycomb configuration,
high performance at around 570°F, and high durability in the presence of halogens, was tested
for a total of 4,000 hours, demonstrating long-term stability. The catalyst achieved 80%
conversion.

1.3.4 Emerson Rosemount Sapphire Thermowells
Multiple tests were conducted with Emerson’s sapphire thermowells in gasifier service. The
thermowells operated reliably in the highly erosive and corrosive environment. Temperature
readings with the sapphire thermowell units tracked well with reference thermocouples, but were
slightly lower, with the differences at about 3-5% relative to the reference thermocouples.

1.3.5 OSU Syngas Chemical Looping (SCL)
OSU developed a high-pressure syngas-to-hydrogen chemical looping process for effectively
converting carbon-based fuels to electricity, hydrogen, and/or liquid fuels while simultaneously
capturing all the carbon emissions. Employing counter-current moving beds and iron-based
composite oxygen carriers under reduction-oxidation conditions, SCL was tested at the NCCC at
a 250-kilowatt (kW) pilot-scale. The testing demonstrated greater than 98% conversion of
syngas components (CO, methane, and hydrogen) in the reducer reactor as well as hydrogen
production from the oxidizer reactor during steam injection. The steam injection experiment
confirmed that the SCL system is capable of converting syngas and steam into pure hydrogen.

1.3.6 TDA Research Ammonia Sorbent
TDA Research conducted initial testing of a sorbent-based gas cleanup technology to remove
ammonia and hydrogen cyanide as well as mercury and other trace metals from syngas at high
temperatures in a single process step. The sorbent operates in a regenerable manner to remove
ammonia and mercury, while irreversibly absorbing hydrogen cyanide and other contaminants.
TDA completed 50 hours of testing and demonstrated greater than 99% ammonia removal.

1.3.7 Air Products Pressure Swing Adsorption (PSA)
Air Products designed an alternative to acid gas removal with physical solvents that consists of
two process blocks: PSA that separates CO₂ and H₂S from the desired products, and a tailgas
disposition block that separates the sulfur-containing compounds and purifies the CO₂ to a
sequestration-grade product. During testing, the PSA system separated more than 95% of the
CO₂ and 99.7% of the H₂S in the feed gas, and post-test evaluations indicated that the adsorbent
material maintained its capacity.

 20

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

1.3.8 Membrane Technology and Research Proteus™ Hydrogen Membranes
MTR conducted testing of the Proteus hydrogen membranes during each of the gasification runs,
building on previous testing, which began in 2009. The operation employed 4-inch, semi-
commercial membrane modules and 2.5-inch membrane stamps. Over the duration of testing
semi-commercial modules at NCCC, iterative improvements in module design and module
components selection led to consistent five-fold hydrogen enrichment (from about 13% in the
feed gas to more than 70% in the module permeate). The membrane stamp testing involved
Proteus membranes of second-generation (Gen-2) material, which MTR developed specifically
for operation up to 200°C (392°F). During the testing, the Gen-2 membrane stamps with higher
selectivity were stable up to 190°C (375°F) and demonstrated improved hydrogen enrichment
(from 12% in the feed syngas to 85% in the permeate) compared to earlier membrane versions.

1.3.9 Media & Process Technology (MPT) Hydrogen Membranes
MPT continued work at the site to develop a dual-stage membrane process featuring the Carbon
Molecular Sieve (CMS) for bulk hydrogen recovery from syngas and a palladium-based
membrane for separation of residual hydrogen. For the most recent testing, MPT scaled up the
palladium membrane testing from single tubes to a multi-tube bundle and operated the bundle
independently to assess performance and regeneration characteristics. Although hydrogen
permeance values declined during operation due to the effects of tar, MPT demonstrated that the
permeance could be restored in the lab with air treatment at raised temperatures.

1.3.10 Worcester Polytechnic Institute (WPI) Hydrogen Membranes
WPI’s previous membrane testing of palladium-based membranes at the site led to a seven-fold
scale-up, an intermediate design for further planned scale-up. The scaled-up module
demonstrated material robustness and steady permeance values, with hydrogen product purity as
high as 99.9%. With only 1% deviation in thickness among the tubes, the consistent fabrication
of the membranes demonstrated the replicability of the technology.

1.3.11 SRI Hydrogen Membrane
SRI conducted the first syngas testing of a hydrogen membrane fabricated with spun hollow
fibers of the temperature- and chemical-resistant polymer polybenzimidazole (PBI). Testing
confirmed that greater than 90% recovery of CO₂ is possible at temperatures above 375°F

1.3.12 TDA Research CO₂ Sorbent Systems
TDA Research’s testing of a solid CO₂ sorbent at the NCCC consistently demonstrated the
capability to remove more than 90% CO₂. TDA tested a combined water-gas shift (WGS)/CO₂
sorbent system with an innovative heat management component, eventually scaling up the
process from 5 lb/hr to 50-lb/hr. TDA demonstrated that reactor bed temperatures were precisely
controlled by direct water injection, and that shift conversions were higher with water injection
than with steam addition at comparable H₂O-to-CO ratios. When parameters were adjusted to
achieve 90% CO conversion in the WGS stage, the overall carbon capture rate was greater than
95%.

 21

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

TDA also tested a small pilot-scale 0.1-MW CO₂ sorbent (without WGS) process. The robust
control system allowed for wide range of testing, which permitted fine tuning of the performance
for each of the cycle sequences. By optimizing the process cycle to allow faster cycling, TDA
demonstrated greater than 99% CO₂ removal efficiency and a reduction in bed size by half.
After completing tests at the NCCC, TDA operated the 0.1-WM system at China’s Sinopec
facility.

1.3.13 Carbon Clean Solutions CO₂ Solvent
Testing of Carbon Clean Solutions’ CO₂ solvent showed it compared favorably with the
commercially used solvents methyl diethanolamine (MDEA) and diethylene glycol n‑propyl
ether (DEPG), achieving significantly higher CO₂ loading. Using the vapor-liquid equilibrium
test data, Carbon Clean Solutions estimated that the heat of reaction for the Carbon Clean
Solutions solvent was 39.2 kilojoule per mole CO₂, 20% lower than that of MDEA.

1.3.14 SRI Solvent and Bechtel Pressure Swing Claus Processes
SRI’s ammonium carbonate-ammonium bicarbonate (AC-ABC) solvent process features high
capacity for CO₂ and H₂S, high regeneration pressure (which lessens downstream compression
requirements), and thermal stability of the regenerated solvent solution. Operation of SRI’s
pilot-scale system demonstrated greater than 99% capture of CO₂ and hydrogen sulfide (H₂S)
and regeneration of high-purity CO₂ and H₂S at pressure. The downstream Claus system showed
a conversion of more than 99.5% of H₂S gas to elemental sulfur, with H₂S in the product gas at
ppm levels.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

2.0 Post-Combustion Carbon Capture Test Campaigns
