2.1 Process Control Automation with Ai-Ops Technology

Government entities and industry wish to investigate the benefits of implementing AI-based
supervisory control. Implementing AI at a test center like NCCC presents a transformative
opportunity to enhance operational efficiency, reduce costs, and gain new insights. The
integration of AI can significantly reduce operational labor costs by automating routine and
complex tasks that would otherwise require extensive human intervention. AI systems can
monitor and adjust process parameters in real-time, ensuring optimal performance without the
need for constant manual oversight. This automation allows the workforce to focus on higher-
value activities, thereby increasing overall productivity and reducing labor costs associated with
manual process control.

 23

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Specifically, the implementation of AI within the process controls at NCCC offers numerous
benefits, including reduced operational labor costs, decreased system downtime, lower
maintenance costs, less time devoted to process optimization, and the discovery of new insights.
These advantages not only enhance the overall efficiency and cost-effectiveness of the carbon
capture system but also position the test center at the forefront of technological innovation in
environmental sustainability. Therefore, Ai-Ops was selected as the technology vendor due to
the company’s proximity to the site (based in Mobile, AL), resource flexibility, cost-
effectiveness, and their platform’s limited cybersecurity restrictions (does not require outside
connection).

Ai-Ops uses the Koios engineering software platform (annual subscription model) that serves at
the deployment zone for pretrained AI models. The goal was to implement this software-based
control scheme on the PSTU, running MEA as the capture solvent to control liquid/gas (L/G)
ratios and steam input to maximize capture efficiency while minimizing energy consumption.
Results were to be set against previous baseline data to compare accuracy and optimization speed
evaluated within static and dynamic conditions. Additional foaming prediction and plugging
detection algorithms were also proposed.

It was not anticipated that DOE funds were needed/utilized for this project. Total funds of
$80,000 were made available by the Economic Development Partnership of Alabama using the
Innovate Alabama Techstars EnergyTech funding for 2023. If additional funds were required,
$60,000 was set aside by Southern Company Services.

The work was divided into two separate milestones. First was the transfer of historical and other
applicable data to Ai-Ops for model development. The second was the deployment of a Koios
platform for PSTU optimization via control of the steam rates and liquid/gas ratios. The
components of the Ai-Ops scope of work are described below.

Software: Establishment of an annual Koios software licensing and software support including
unlimited tags, devices, and models (valued at $7,499/year)

Front-End Design: Data exports and preliminary control strategies focused on steam flow
control, lean solvent flow control (at set gas rates), and potentially a column foaming prediction
and/or a line plugging detection algorithm

Detailed Design:
 • Build four concurrent models from the imported data
 • Finalize control narratives
 • Provide assistance with:
  Koios hardware/VM specification
  System architecture details and drawing updates
  Koios installation
  Programmable logic controller and add-on instructions for integration
  Graphical interface updates for up to six graphics and faceplates

 24

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

  P&ID edits
  Operating procedure changes and updates
Site Acceptance:
 • Two engineers for four days at eight hours per day
 • Assistance with final Koios integration and programmable logic controller edits
 • Assistance with execution and final cut-over/loop-sale of four models
 • Provide turnover documents for the following:
  Controls strategy and narrative
  Model files, validations, and documentation
  Model building code-base
  System architecture drawings
  P&ID edits
In May 2025, the hardware that hosts the AI-based software and manages the control system was
received. The initial training of the learning methods was performed offline using historical run
data, and the newly created AI-derived model was easily imported into the control systems.
Ai-Ops representatives came on-site on June 23 to set up the new AI-assisted control server.
New components for the PSTU cross-flow heat exchanger were installed in early July. The
interface screen for the controller was designed to be straightforward, with the operator entering
the desired capture rate and then turning on the AI controller (see Figure 3.)

 Figure 3. HMI Faceplate for AI-Assisted Control Scheme

After completing water commissioning, MEA was loaded on July 22, and flue gas started on
July 23. The AI-assisted control scheme for steam control was first turned on July 24 around
10 a.m. Figure 4 shows the system behavior at that time.

 25

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 4. Process Trends During First Attempt at Steam Flow Control

The blue line represents the CO₂ capture rate, and the orange line represents the steam flow
measurement. The controller struggled to remain stable as the steam flow measurement was not
reading properly. It was determined that the steam transmitter (for FI20250B – low flow range
meter) was improperly installed (condensate legs were not long enough vertically) and needed to
be relocated to the floor beneath. Figure 5 shows the initial results obtained when testing was
restarted on July 31 following the relocation of the transmitter.

 Figure 5. PSTU Process Trends During Restart after Reinstallation of Steam Flow Meter

It was observed that the controller was overly aggressive in its response, presumably due to poor
training data, as the input data for the model was collected before the flow meter transmitter was
relocated. Regardless, a change to the controller output frequency was made such that it would
make a steam flow change every 15 minutes instead of every two minutes. This smoothed out

 26

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

the controller’s behavior significantly. Figure 6 shows the system response to CO₂ capture rate
setpoint changes.

 Figure 6. Capture Setpoint Changes and System Behavior

Most of the condition changes occurred over a weekend with limited support, so no adjustments
were made to the controller except for the capture rate setpoint. The controller appeared to
behave somewhat precisely, but not accurately, almost consistently giving a deviation of about
5% from the setpoint. It was concluded that much of this was likely due to the poor training
data. With an improved flow meter installation to train a new model, it is believed that the target
value could be achieved within ±1% tolerance. Unfortunately, additional time was not available
to further investigate this phenomenon. Testing concluded on August 4 to allow sufficient time
to prepare the unit for the next technology developer test campaign.

The PSTU operated for 108 hours on AI-assisted control. During the test period, NETL
conducted an emissions measurement campaign using the NCCC’s PTR-TOF mass
spectrometer, an FTIR spectrometer, and bag sampling of the clean flue gas and the CO₂ product.

2.2 Aerosols and Amine Emissions Studies

Amine emissions from carbon capture systems in the form of aerosols leaving the absorber are a
common problem with commercial solvent processes where SO₃ is present in the flue gas. The
formation of aerosols has been found to correlate with the concentration of SO₃ present in the
flue gas. The amine leaving the system is difficult to capture due to the small particle size (the
vast majority are less than one micron in diameter), and traditional systems do not have an
efficient way to remove them from the exiting treated flue gas. Solvent losses from this effect
can be significant and must be addressed if a commercial system is to be deployed. An
understanding of upstream equipment effects and internal process changes are critical to
determining all the mechanisms at work which create aerosols.

 27

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Therefore, a project was initiated to design and construct a sample extraction system for
analytical equipment including the ELPI+ device to measure the particle size distribution (PSD)
and particle number count in real time. The selected sampling locations included the PSTU inlet
(untreated flue gas), the SSTU inlet (following SO₂ removal and cooling within the PSTU
process units), and the SSTU wash tower outlet.

The primary objective in 2015 was to establish baseline data that could be compared against
results obtained in 2016, when a new activated carbon injection baghouse was brought online at
Gaston Unit 5 (between the Unit 5 flue gas desulfurizer and the selective catalytic reduction
process), upstream of the NCCC test site. The baghouse was installed to reduce heavy metals
emissions but has demonstrated the additional effect of removing most of the SO₃ present and
therefore dramatically decreasing amine emissions.

Figure 7 presents the MEA emissions at the SSTU water wash outlet before (12/12/2015) and
after (10/10/16) the baghouse installation as measured by the ELPI+. MEA emissions from the
SSTU were between 100 and 110 ppm prior to the installation of the baghouse. These emissions
rates were reduced to between 5 and 10 ppm after the baghouse startup. Similar operating
conditions were maintained in the SSTU during both operating durations, indicating that the
baghouse flue gas treatment step is responsible for a significant reduction in the amine emissions
rate.

 Figure 7. MEA Emissions at SSTU Outlet Before and After Baghouse Installation

The PDI was successfully utilized for aerosol characterization during the December 2015
sampling campaign. Aerosol drops as small as 0.1 microns were detected, with concentrations
up to 10E7 per cm3.

PDI sampling was again performed on the SSTU during the October 2016 campaign. During
this sample run, no aerosols were observed despite continuous attempts to measure aerosols.

 28

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

As demonstrated by the figures, the baghouse operation resulted in a significant drop in particle
count. Additionally, more than 97% of the cumulative aerosol number was below 0.1 microns
after the baghouse installation. Further analysis will be required to determine the effects on net
amine emissions rate and the effects of changes in internal process conditions. Regardless, initial
indications are that amine emissions may be significantly reduced by the inclusion of an
activated carbon injection baghouse on coal-derived flue gas streams where SO₃ is present.

For the MEA baseline test with the SSTU in late 2016, initial data from the run confirmed a
significant drop in aerosol count after the baghouse began operating. The ELPI+ data showed a
shift in PSD and particle count, but the PDI was unable to provide any indication of aerosols
present because the particle sizes were below the detectable range of the instrument. Figure 8
provides two screenshots from the PDI from before and after the baghouse installation.

 Before Baghouse Installation After Baghouse Installation
 Figure 8. Screenshots of PDI Data before and after Baghouse Operation

Data from the ELPI+, presented in Figure 9, showed that the majority of particles were below
0.1 microns in diameter, which is below the detection limit of the PDI. The x-axis of the figure
shows particle diameters in microns, and the y-axis shows the particle count. In addition to the
drastic shift to smaller particle sizes observed, the total particle count dropped by three to five
orders of magnitude compared to previous operation without the upstream baghouse.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

actual concentration of MEA present. Figure 10 provides results of the FTIR measurements
taken prior to the baghouse installation (12/12/2015) and after the baghouse installation
(10/10/2016). The data demonstrated that amine concentrations were significantly reduced after
the baghouse installation.

2.3 DOE Carbon Capture Simulation Initiative

MEA testing was performed in the PSTU to support DOE’s CCSI. The first testing occurred in
2014, with the data generated to be used to validate models of comparable carbon capture
systems. Dynamic and parametric testing was included as part of the model validation.
Dynamic testing focused on modeling the system response to operator or plant changes, and
parametric testing validated the model output for steady-state periods. Testing with MEA
solvent was conducted in 2017 to provide data for further model validation for the second phase
of the CCSI, the CCSI2.

The dynamic testing included varying flue gas, solvent, and steam flow rates. While the system
was in steady-state operation, one of these adjustable parameters was increased and then
decreased in a step-wise pattern, and samples of both rich and lean solvent were collected every
five to 15 minutes, depending on the parameter. The objective was to introduce the next change
before the system could equilibrate from the previous change. Following the dynamic testing,
parametric testing was completed, mainly comprising conditions utilizing only one absorber bed.
Data were taken from 17 steady-state periods for comparison with the predicted results from
process simulation software for two cases involving the absorber and regenerator. A total of 408
hours on flue gas were accumulated for these tests.

Comparisons of the steady-state data to DOE’s deterministic model predictions were made
separately for the absorber model and the regenerator model. A total of 17 cases were simulated,
and a sample of six of these is presented. For the absorber model, the major input of interest was
the liquid-to-gas mass ratio. For the regenerator model, the major inputs of interest were the rich
solvent flow and the reboiler duty. These inputs are given in Table 4.

 31

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Table 4. Inputs for Steady-State Absorber and Regenerator Simulations with MEA
 L/G Mass Rich Solvent Flow Reboiler Duty

Comparisons of model predictions and NCCC operational data for the absorber are plotted in
Figure 11. The model predictions matched the percent CO₂ capture operational data well when
the CO₂ capture rate was high. The model generally under-predicted the temperature of the rich
solvent stream in the absorber outlet. This discrepancy may be due to the model’s inaccurate
characterization of the heat of absorption of the MEA-H₂O-CO₂ system.

 Figure 11. Comparison of Model and NCCC Data for Absorber Operation with MEA

For the stripper model, the output variables of interest are the CO₂ loading and temperature of the
outlet lean solvent. The comparisons are given in Figure 12. The model generally over-
predicted both the loading and temperature of the lean solvent stream. These discrepancies may
be reconciled with future modeling of the thermodynamics of the system and uncertainty
quantification of the property models, hydraulic models, and mass transfer models.

 32

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 12. Comparison of Model and NCCC Data for Regenerator Operation with MEA

2.4 DOE Carbon Capture Simulation for Industry Impact

The CCSI2 test campaign leveraged the existing model of the PSTU for an MEA system through
a Bayesian experimental design. With this approach, the uncertainty in the process model
prediction was estimated prior to test planning, then operating conditions were selected, and
finally, the results were used to update the model parameters to reduce that uncertainty. The four
major input variables and their ranges were:

 • Lean solvent flowrate: 3,000 to 13,000 kg/hr
 • Flue gas flowrate: 1,000 to 3,000 kg/hr
 • Lean solvent loading: 0.1 to 0.35 mol CO₂/mol MEA
 • Flue gas CO₂ weight fraction: 0.125 to 0.175
These ranges were selected to provide realistic conditions and a CO₂ capture rate ranging from
50 to 95%. The MEA concentration was maintained at 30 wt% throughout the test campaign.
The first 20 conditions were selected for 3-bed absorber operation with intercooling. The four
input variables were modeled to calculate the expected CO₂ capture rate. Experimental data
from the campaign was then used to update the process model parameters through an iterative
process in the Bayesian inference methodology. These updated parameters were then fed into
the Bayesian inference methodology to predict new model uncertainty values.

As shown in Figure 13, the first 20 test conditions resulted in a decrease in uncertainty.
Confidence interval widths, as calculated by the surrogate absorber model, are shown for (A) a
grid of points spread throughout input space and for (B) points for which experimental data were
collected.

The effectiveness of the design of experiment methodology was clearly demonstrated for steady-
state operations. Future projects will focus on developing a more efficient and computationally
streamlined execution of the iterative Bayesian experimental design process. In addition to the
steady-state work, two dynamic experimental design methodologies were successfully
implemented, pseudo-random binary sequence and Schroeder-phased input.

The CCSI toolset continues to be applied in collaboration with technology developers and test
centers to increase deployment. For example, the toolset has been used at Technology Centre
Mongstad through collaboration with the International Test Center Network.

2.5 Cansolv Technologies DC-103 Solvent

In 2014, Cansolv Technologies conducted an extended series of solvent tests using the PSTU.
Testing included two major campaigns: a 325‑hour run with the DC‑103 solvent beginning in
June, followed by a 471‑hour campaign with the next‑generation DC‑201 solvent through
mid‑August, providing a combined total of nearly 800 hours of pilot‑scale operation under steady
and varied conditions. These campaigns built upon prior testing of DC‑201 and expanded the

 35

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

dataset to include both coal‑derived flue gas conditions and simulated NGCC conditions. The
2014 campaigns also incorporated measurements of amine emissions using ELPI+ aerosol
instrumentation and operation under hot climate temperatures to evaluate solvent behavior under
more demanding environmental conditions.

During the first portion of the Cansolv DC-103 test campaign, PSTU operating conditions were
optimized for the solvent. Table 5 summarizes the optimal process conditions established. After
achieving steady-state operation at these conditions, the ELPI+ instrument was used to measure
the concentration and droplet size distribution of aerosols present in the CO₂-depleted flue gas
leaving the wash tower. Samples were taken before and after the installation of a new high
efficiency demister in the wash tower to assess the effect on solvent emissions.

side and stripper side, respectively. Optimal steady-state operation was achieved at around 50
hours of operation. At this point, the configuration with one intercooler in the bottom section of
the absorber (the packing sections) was optimized for energy consumption. The average values
for the absorber side CO₂ removal were about 4.7% higher, and for the absorber side stripping
factor, about 4.7% lower than for the stripper side.

Figure 15 and Figure 16 demonstrate the impact of power plant load on CO₂ removal efficiency.
As the power plant load increased, and thus the inlet CO₂ concentration increased (from 10.8 to
11.8 vol% CO₂ dry), the CO₂ removal rate decreased by about 5%. However, since the total
amount of CO₂ captured remained constant, the stripping factor was also unchanged. This
reduction of CO₂ removal rate at higher inlet CO₂ concentration indicated that the liquid flow
rate was at near maximum loading.

Also shown in the figures is the impact of operating the bottom intercooler on overall process
performance. For normal plant load operations, when the bottom intercooler was removed from
service, the CO₂ removal rate decreased by approximately 5%, and the stripping factor increased
by the same percentage. One test with no intercoolers was performed with the lean amine

 37

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

temperature increased from 45 to 60°C (113 to 140°F), and the results demonstrated no
significant difference in CO₂ removal rate or stripping factor.

Aerosol and Particulate Measurements with ELPI+ Instrument
The ELPI+ measurements provided results for aerosol and particulate matter concentrations and
size distributions at three locations:

 • Inlet flue gas to the absorber
 • Outlet flue gas from the water wash downstream of the original demister
 • Outlet flue gas from the water wash downstream of the high efficiency demister

The measurements were taken to characterize the properties of the flue gas used at the NCCC,
that is, flue gas from a typical coal fired power plant with a wet stack arrangement. In the wet
stack arrangement, the flue gas is cooled directly with water from the pre-scrubber upstream of
the CO₂ absorber. The measurements were also used to assess the flue gas after contact with the
DC-103 solvent. This was done with the two different demisters to evaluate the impact of
standard and high efficiency demisters on droplet capture rate and amine emissions.

Figure 17 provides the ELPI+ measurements of aerosol and particulate concentration and size
distributions for the flue gas used at the NCCC. The results showed no significant difference in
aerosol or particulate concentration or sizes for the three different process locations sampled.
The total number of particles was on the order of 10 to 20 million per cubic centimeter, with the
majority between 0.1 and 0.3 microns. There was no significant difference in the results with the
original, standard demister and the high efficiency demister. Due to the small particle size,
neither type of demister would be expected to have significant aerosol or particulate capture.

 Figure 17. ELPI+ Measurements of Aerosol and Particulate Concentrations during DC-103 Campaign

Laborelec has taken ELPI+ measurements at different coal fired power plants throughout
Europe, with results showing that the majority of aerosols and particulate were smaller than
0.3 microns. While operating with the DC-103 solvent, there was no change in particle size at

 38

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

the outlet of the absorber because of the solvent’s low volatility. However, with a higher
volatility solvent such as MEA, the measured size at the absorber outlet would be significantly
larger.

2.6 Cansolv Technologies DC-201 Solvent

The DC-201 test required operation under diluted flue gas conditions to simulate the flue gas
from a natural gas-fired power plant, to mimic the expected near-term commercial application.
To evaluate the effects of solvent degradation products, the testing utilized solvent that had
operated in previous testing in the PSTU.

A major test objective was to operate under hot climate conditions at 90% CO₂ capture to assess
energy consumption and degradation rates. Under the hot climate conditions, the flue gas
temperature was 45 to 50°C (113 to 122°F) and the lean solvent temperature was 54°C (129°F),
compared to 35 to 40°C (95 to 104°F) flue gas temperatures and 40°C (104°F) lean solvent
temperature under previous, cooler conditions. Figure 18 plots the stripping factor versus liquid-
to-gas ratio for the two climate conditions. The optimized energy consumption under hot climate
conditions was about 6% higher than under the cooler conditions. This was expected since the
equilibrium rich CO₂ loading is lower at higher absorber temperature, and hence a higher
circulation rate and steam rate is required per unit mass of CO₂ captured.

With the relatively short test duration, a clear trend of degradation rate was not apparent.
Although a strontium tracer was added to the solvent to identify any physical entrainment of
non-volatile species in the exiting flue gas, it proved ineffective due to solubility problems.

Another test objective was to measure amine emissions from a fresh amine solution. Table 6
gives the results of these measurements. As expected, the contaminants present in the flue gas
from the coal-fired plant caused higher emissions. The fresh solvent solution emitted less than
used solvent solutions in which degradation product had accumulated.

2.7 Carbon Clean Solutions Solvent

Carbon Clean Solutions tested its APBS solvent for two phases of CO₂ capture testing in the
PSTU. The first test campaign was conducted in 2014 during the previous contract period using
natural gas simulated flue gas and typical coal flue gas. During this contract period, testing was
conducted in early 2015 using only typical coal flue gas.

Regeneration Energy
For operation using natural gas flue gas, the effect of L/G ratio on regeneration efficiency is
shown in Figure 19 with the stripper pressure held constant at 10 psig. The regeneration energy
reached a minimum at an L/G ratio of 0.75 w/w (6,000 lb/hr liquid flow for 8,000 lb/hr of gas
flow).

 Figure 19. Effect of L/G ratio on Regeneration Energy with Simulated Natural Gas Flue Gas

Based on Carbon Clean Solutions’ process simulation conducted with g-PROM software, with
an optimal design of lean/rich heat exchanger and Carbon Clean Solutions’ patented stripper
configuration, a minimum regeneration energy of 1,200 Btu/lb CO₂ (2.8 GJ/ton CO₂) was
predicted for the following conditions:

 • 90% CO₂ removal from flue gas with 4.3 vol% CO₂ and 16 vol% O2
 • Gas velocity of 9 ft/sec
      •    L/G ratio of 0.76

 40

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 • Stripper pressure of 14 psig
For operation with typical coal flue gas, the run time was insufficient to collect enough data to
plot a graph similar to that of Figure 19. However, it was determined that the best conditions
comprised an L/G ratio of 1.6 and a stripper pressure of 19.8 psig. For a CO₂ capture efficiency
of 92.1 percent, the steam-to-CO₂ ratio was 1.34, giving a regeneration energy of 1,234 Btu/lb
CO₂ or 2.87 GJ/ton CO₂. If the liquid flow was reduced further (for example, to 7,000 lb/hr) at a
stripper pressure of 20 psig, a lower value of regeneration energy (about 1,170 Btu/lb or
2.7 GJ/ton CO₂) would be expected.

Based on Carbon Clean Solutions’ process simulation conducted with g-PROM software, with
an optimal design of lean/rich heat exchanger and Carbon Clean Solutions’ patented stripper
configuration, a minimum regeneration energy of 1,075 Btu/lb CO₂ (or 2.5 GJ/ton CO₂) could be
achieved for:

 • 90% CO₂ capture from flue gas containing 11.4 vol% CO₂
 • Gas velocity of 5.5 ft/sec
 • L/G ratio of 1.4
 • Stripper pressure of 20 psig
Stripper Pressure Effects
The effect of the stripper pressure on regeneration efficiency for simulated natural gas flue gas is
shown in Figure 20 for a constant L/G ratio of 0.75. The regeneration energy reached a
minimum at a stripper pressure of about 15 psig. Not enough data were available to determine
the effect of stripper pressure while operating with typical coal flue gas.

  Figure 20. Effect of Stripper Pressure on Regeneration Energy with Natural Gas Simulated Flue Gas

Further testing is needed to obtain the true global minimum value of regeneration energy.
However, from the data obtained and presented in the preceding figures, Carbon Clean Solutions

 41

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

expects a global minimum value below 1,290 Btu/lb (3.0 GJ/ton CO₂) to achieve 90% CO₂
capture using an L/G ratio of 0.76 and stripper pressure of 14 psig.

Additional Findings with Simulated Natural Gas Flue Gas Operation
One run conducted with intercooling during the simulated natural gas flue gas operation, showed
that the regeneration energy reduced only slightly (less than 0.3%) to 1,434.4 Btu/lb CO₂,
suggesting that intercooling was not effective in reducing the regeneration energy for 4 vol%
CO₂ flue gas.

One run was conducted using two packed beds in the absorber, instead of the three packed beds
used in all other runs, and the data demonstrated the solvent’s capability of removing 90% CO₂
with two packed beds with about 5% more regeneration energy as compared to that required with
three beds even with 4.3 vol% CO₂ in the inlet flue gas.

The projected regeneration energy for 90% CO₂ capture (1,290 Btu/lb CO₂ or 3.0 GJ/ton CO₂) is
35 to 40% lower than the values reported for MEA for gas-fired boiler flue gas.

Samples were taken for fresh solvent at the beginning of the test runs and from spent solvent at
the end of the test runs, and the metals concentrations were comparted to those obtained during
previous NCCC testing with MEA. The level of chromium for MEA was more than 22 times
that in APBS solvent after two months of testing, indicating that the APBS solvent was much
less corrosive than MEA solvent.

Samples of the CO₂ product stream consistently showed that the CO₂ content was higher than 97
vol%. The water content was typically 2.5 vol% and nitrogen was typically found to be at a
concentration of 210 ppm.

Table 7 provides results of the analysis of amines and degradation products in the water wash
outlet gas for samples taken during typical flue gas operation.

 Table 7. Degradation Product Concentration During Carbon Clean Solutions Solvent Operation

 Sum of amines, ppmw 5.0
 Sum of aldehydes, ppmw 1.5
 Ammonia, ppmw 10.3
 Total hydrocarbons, ppmw 2.7

In addition to the successful campaign at NCCC, testing of Carbon Clean Solutions’ solvent has
been conducted at larger scales in other test facilities. The solvent is currently being employed
for CO₂ capture at several commercial facilities, including an alkali chemicals and fertilizers
plant with a coal-fired boiler in India (174 TPD CO₂ capture); a coal-fired combined heat and
power plant in eastern Europe (50 TPD CO₂ capture); and a chemical plant with a coal-fired
boiler in India (168 TPD CO₂ capture).
