National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

2.8 ION Clean Energy ICE-21 Solvent

ION Clean Energy completed a test campaign as part of the development of a proprietary
advanced solvent, ICE-21 which consistently demonstrated significant advantages over
traditional MEA in ION’s previous lab and pilot testing. The ION solvent was tested in the
PSTU in the summer of 2015 for 1,116 hours of operation. To meet the overall project
objectives of DOE’s goal for second-generation solvents, ION focused on two major project
target achievements during pilot-scale testing at the PSTU—confirming solvent performance and
determining solvent lifetime.

The temporary installation of a new process water chiller and associated piping was completed
prior to testing at NCCC. This enabled additional flue gas cooling at the direct contact cooler
unit operation and was required to achieve ION’s temperature target for the flue gas entering the
absorber column. Also installed for the testing was ION’s mobile analytical chemistry lab, with
capability of Karl Fischer titration, total inorganic carbon analysis, and gas chromatography.

After completing modifications to the PSTU and identifying the optimal test conditions for
parametric testing, two control strategies were prioritized to further define a successful test
campaign. The priority was to maintain process and water balances. The water in the wash
tower basin is normally recycled into the process to maintain a reasonable water balance. ION
maintained a water balance by operating at or around a prescribed temperature difference
between the flue gas containing CO₂ entering the absorber and the treated flue gas exiting the
absorber. Water balance was achieved shortly after startup and maintained through the end of
testing. The water balance was confirmed with Karl Fischer titrations conducted over the entire
testing campaign at NCCC.

Using baseline MEA process data provided by NCCC, ION modeled MEA performance
conditions in the PSTU using ProTreat® simulation software. Completing this evaluation prior
to the test campaign enabled ION to perform detailed analysis of the PSTU process and
equipment. Given the characteristics of ION’s solvent, optimal test conditions were identified,
and results from actual testing were subsequently verified against the predicted model results.
Some minor changes to the PSTU equipment were requested after completing a thorough review
of boundary conditions and existing equipment design, assisted by results from the simulations.
As shown in Figure 21, ION was able to capture more than 95% CO₂ at a range of L/G testing
conditions, satisfying the project objective of 90% or greater CO₂ capture.

 Figure 21. CO₂ Capture Rate at Various L/G Ratios for ION Engineering Solvent

 43

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Figure 22 plots the specific heat performance of ION’s solvent during actual testing in
comparison to design values predicted by ProTreat simulations, and the best performance
claimed by another developer at NCCC. Two flue gas outages occurred during the run time
shown, which resulted in inaccurate sensor readings of the PSTU instrumentation and of the
dependent calculations, such as specific energy requirements. The ION solvent’s specific energy
result obtained at NCCC was 1,600 BTU/lb CO₂ during steady-state operation, while the design
case was 1,090 BTU/lb CO₂. Running one bed of packing was likely the biggest energy penalty
compounded by over-stripping and obtaining capture rates exceeding 95%.

 Figure 22. Specific Energy Requirements of ION Engineering Solvent

Amine-based solvents are known to undergo molecular degradation due to the standard process
conditions of a carbon capture plant; this process transforms active solvent into less effective or
dangerous byproducts normally restored by implementing solvent replacement methodologies
(and their associated costs). For coal-fired carbon capture, solvent degradation also includes
irreversible reactions with flue gas contaminants. Throughout the testing, the ION solvent
maintained performance with respect to specific heat requirements and capture efficiency,
indicating high stability.

Studies of heat-stable salts showed that the highest detected compound was sulfate at about
0.2 wt% of the entire system. Sulfate is not thought to be a degradation byproduct of ION’s
solvent; rather, it is thought to originate in the flue gas supply and to react with excess oxygen in
the flue gas. No impact on solvent performance was observed with respect to specific heat
requirement (i.e., steam flow) or capture efficiency, even as degradation products and heat-stable
salts increased.

Solvent samples from the PSTU were analyzed for degradation products using gas
chromatography mass spectrometry. Of the degradation products identified, the highest level
measured was 0.4 wt% accumulated at the completion of the test campaign. ION did not observe
large accumulations of heat-stable salts or solvent degradation over the 1,116-hour test period,
suggesting solvent longevity, although longer testing would be needed to confirm solvent
durability.

Stainless steel corrosion coupons were tested in the PSTU during the solvent testing. At all
locations tested, the coupons showed no significant corrosion. Scanning-electron microscopy,

 44

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

energy-dispersive spectroscopy, and weight-loss analysis as per ASTM G1-G4 standard
methodologies substantiated the chemical and morphological tenacity observed of common
stainless steel metal coupons. Metal ions were also quantified and evaluated against MEA tested
at NCCC. The evaluation showed that the ION solvent accumulates significantly fewer metal
ions than MEA solvent.

2.9 ION Clean Energy ICE-31 Solvent

ION Clean Energy’s 2021 test campaign at the NCCC evaluated its third-generation solvent
technology, ICE‑31. The project aimed to scale the solvent from lab development to pilot-scale
demonstration in an industrially relevant environment and to validate its performance across a
range of flue gas conditions representative of power generation and industrial applications.

Testing was carried out in the PSTU over about six months, from March through October 2021,
accumulating more than 4,000 hours of operation. The primary goals of the project included:

 • Demonstrating performance of the ICE‑31 solvent at pilot scale
 • Generating operational data across a range of flue gas conditions
 • Evaluating process configurations and operating parameters
 • Assessing solvent stability and emissions performance
 • Supporting development and validation of process design tools for scale‑up
The campaign utilized multiple flue gas sources to evaluate performance under different
operating scenarios:

 • Simulated natural gas combined cycle (NGCC) flue gas (~4.4% CO₂)
 • Natural gas boiler flue gas (~7-8% CO₂)
 • Coal-derived flue gas (~12-13% CO₂)
Both parametric testing and extended steady‑state operation were conducted. Parametric testing
was used to explore the influence of key operating variables for evaluation of capture efficiency
and energy consumption, while long‑duration tests were used to evaluate system reliability and
solvent stability. Modifications to the PSTU were implemented to support the campaign,
including enhancements to solvent analysis and water wash systems to improve emissions
control and monitoring.

Across all flue gas conditions, the ICE‑31 solvent demonstrated consistently high capture
performance. The system achieved at least 95% CO₂ capture under all tested conditions using a
conventional regeneration configuration. For NGCC‑type flue gas, capture performance was
further increased to approximately 98% CO₂ removal. This increase in capture efficiency was
associated with only a modest rise in energy demand, with specific reboiler duty (SRD)
increasing by approximately 2-3% compared to lower capture conditions.

 45

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The parametric dataset showed stable and repeatable operation across a wide range of
liquid‑to‑gas ratios and operating conditions, indicating that the solvent performs reliably under
different process configurations.

Energy consumption, expressed as specific reboiler duty, was a key performance metric
evaluated during the campaign. Results demonstrated that ICE‑31 operates at relatively low
regeneration energy compared to conventional solvent systems. Under optimized conditions:

 • NGCC flue gas testing achieved SRD values as low as approximately 2.6 GJ/tCO₂ at high
 capture efficiency
 • Coal flue gas testing achieved SRD values around 2.5 GJ/tCO₂ at similar capture levels
Testing with a heat‑integrated stripping configuration, including the AFS, showed that energy
requirements could be further reduced relative to conventional operation. The results indicate
that process integration strategies can contribute to improved energy efficiency while
maintaining high capture performance.

A component of the test campaign involved comparison of measured pilot‑scale data with
predictions from ION’s ProTreat® process model. The results showed strong agreement
between modeled and measured performance. Model predictions of specific reboiler duty were
reported to match experimental data with an average error of approximately 0.4%, with a
standard deviation of ±1.7%. This agreement was achieved without extensive adjustment of
model parameters, indicating that the modeling approach can reliably represent system
performance and can be used to support scale‑up and process design.

Long‑duration testing was conducted for approximately 1,500 hours under NGCC conditions at
95% CO₂ capture. During this period, the system demonstrated stable operation with greater
than 99% uptime. No solvent addition was required during the extended test period, and
performance metrics, including capture efficiency and energy consumption, remained consistent
over time.

Mass balance and analytical results indicated minimal solvent degradation, while emissions
measurements showed low levels of solvent and ammonia downstream of the water wash system.
Reported concentrations were below 0.04 ppm for solvent and below 1 ppm for ammonia. These
findings indicate that ICE‑31 maintains its performance characteristics under extended operation
and that emissions control systems were effective in limiting solvent release.

The 2021 ION test campaign yielded the following key outcomes:

 • High CO₂ capture efficiency (≥95%) demonstrated across all tested flue gas types
 • Ability to achieve higher capture levels (~98%) with limited additional energy input
 • Specific reboiler duty values in the range of ~2.5–2.7 GJ/tCO₂ under optimized
 conditions
 • Improved energy performance through use of heat‑integrated stripping approaches,
 including the Advanced Flash Stripper

 46

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 • Strong agreement between pilot data and process model predictions
 • Stable long‑term operation with no required solvent replacement and low emissions

2.10 GE Global Research Aminosilicone Solvent and Desorber Unit

GE Global completed work to demonstrate a CO₂ capture process using its GAP-1 aminosilicone
solvent. Compared to alkanolamines such as MEA, aminosilicones possess properties that
substantially decrease the cost of solvent-based CO₂ capture, including lower vapor pressures,
higher boiling points, greater thermal stability, and lower heat capacity. In addition, the
replacement of water with a non-volatile co-solvent, triethleneglycol, results in a substantial
energy savings. In conjunction with the solvent development, GE developed a continuous stirred
tank reactor for CO₂ desorption as an alternative to traditional column strippers. Compared to a
stripper column, the CSTR is smaller and lower in cost. Based on promising lab results, GE
scaled up its technology for testing with the PSTU. Figure 23 shows the GE desorber as installed
in the PSTU structure.

 Figure 23. GE Global Desorber Unit Installed in PSTU Structure

Through lab testing, GE found that though its solvent exhibited high thermal stability at elevated
temperatures, the stability dropped as CO₂ loading increased due to urea formation. Adding
water limited the urea formation reaction and hence reduced degradation. Therefore, for initial
testing at the NCCC, the water content of the solvent solution was targeted at 5 to 10%.
Furthermore, water was found to be an effective way to enable steam stripping, lower desorption
temperature, and hence further reduce thermal degradation. Steam stripping also increased the
solvent working capacity by 30% due to more efficient desorption. In light of these advantages
to steam stripping, GE expanded the test scope from testing the GAP-1 solvent and CSTR to also
testing the solvent with the PSTU regenerator, a standard stripper column.

 47

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Solvent testing in the integrated PSTU/CSTR system was conducted in late 2016 through early
2017. Several issues were encountered during early operation, including the tendency of solvent
foaming in the CSTR, which resulted in solvent carryover and loss. Through experimentation, it
was determined that foaming and solvent carryover were minimized by maintaining the water
concentration at 5% or less while operating the CSTR at 7 psig. The PSTU/CSTR operation
consisted of more than 500 hours of operation with desorption temperatures of 110 to 130°C
(230 to 265°F), solvent-to-CO₂ molar ratios of 1.5 to 4, and flue gas flow rates ranging from
2,000 to 5,000 lb/hr. The system demonstrated 95% CO₂ capture efficiency at a solvent-to-CO₂
molar ratio of 4 and desorption temperature of 110°C (230°F) and yielded 65% CO₂ capture
efficiency at a solvent-to-CO₂ molar ratio of 0.5 and desorption temperature of 120°C (248°F).

After the PSTU/CSTR operation objectives were achieved, the system was switched to PSTU-
only operation with the standard regenerator, a steam stripping column. Fresh solvent was used
so that performances could be compared directly to that of PSTU/CSTR operation. The PSTU
regeneration column was found to be more robust regarding solvent entrainment, allowing water
contents up to 35%. Under stoichiometric conditions, CO₂ capture efficiency was between 90
and 95% at a solvent-to-CO₂ molar ratio of 0.5, a desorption temperature of 235°F, a pressure of
2 psig, and a water content of 19 wt%. Both CO₂ capture efficiency and specific duty reached
optimum conditions at 18 wt% H₂O. Little amine degradation (less than 0.5 wt%/day) was
recorded over 350 hours of operation. Controlled water addition to the solvent decreased the
desorption temperature, thermal degradation, and improved the CO₂ working capacity due to
more efficient absorption and desorption processes. Under these conditions, the GAP-1 solvent
exhibited a 25 percent increase in working capacity, and 10% reduction in specific steam duty as
compared to MEA, at 5.5ºC (10°F) lower desorption temperature.

GE updated its capture system process models using the data from PSTU testing and performed a
techno-economic analysis for a 550-MW coal fired power plant. The first-year CO₂ removal cost
for the aminosilicone-based carbon capture process was estimated at $48/ton CO₂ using a steam
stripper column. This represented a 20% reduction compared to MEA, primarily due to lower
overall capital cost. The estimated cost of CO₂ capture using the CSTR was dominated by the
economics of the solvent make-up required by a higher degradation rate. The steam stripper
column would be preferred due to higher desorption efficiency and lower solvent make-up rates.
Further cost reductions would be expected from reductions in the solvent manufacturing cost,
process optimization, and improvements in solvent stability and CO₂ working capacity with a
next-generation aminosilicone solvent.

2.11 UT-Austin Piperazine Advanced Stripper Process (2018)

In 2018, UT-Austin conducted the first of three major pilot-scale test campaigns at the NCCC,
each evaluating the PZAS process in the PSTU integrated with the advanced flash stripper to
assess performance, degradation, and system behavior under realistic operating conditions for
long-term operation. The first campaign was a collaborative effort between URS Group Inc.,
UT-Austin, and Trimeric Corporation.

The primary objectives of the testing campaign were threefold: (1) to demonstrate the robustness
of concentrated piperazine solvent in coal-derived flue gas, (2) to evaluate the performance of a
novel high-temperature regeneration process using the Advanced Flash Stripper (AFS)

 48

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

configuration, and (3) to compare this configuration with a conventional simple stripper (SS)
system. Additionally, the study aimed to identify and mitigate operational challenges such as
solvent degradation, aerosol formation, corrosion, and solids precipitation.

Approximately 2,000 hours of testing were completed and divided into four phases:
commissioning, operational development, parametric testing (for both AFS and SS), and long-
term AFS operation. The AFS regeneration skid, integrated into the existing NCCC pilot solvent
test unit, incorporated key components such as a flash stripper, heat exchangers, and an overhead
condenser. This configuration allowed systematic evaluation of process variables including
temperature, pressure, solvent loading, and gas flow rate.

Energy Performance and Process Efficiency
One of the most significant findings of the campaign was the superior energy performance of the
piperazine/AFS system compared to both piperazine/SS and other second-generation solvent
systems tested at NCCC. The AFS configuration achieved a heat duty ranging from 1.9 to 2.3
GJ per tonne of CO₂ captured, with an average of 2.1 GJ/tonne at 90% capture efficiency. This
represents the lowest reported energy requirement for any test campaign conducted at the NCCC.

In contrast, the simple stripper configuration exhibited substantially higher heat duties between
3.4 and 4.0 GJ/tonne CO₂ under comparable operating conditions. Other developers using
conventional stripping technologies reported heat duties in the range of 2.4 to 2.7 GJ/tonne.
These results highlight the effectiveness of the AFS design in reducing the energy penalty
associated with piperazine solvent regeneration.

The improved performance of the AFS is attributed to its ability to recover latent heat from water
vaporization and operate at higher pressures, thereby reducing both thermal energy requirements
and downstream compression costs. After accounting for estimated heat losses (5–15%), the
effective heat duty of the AFS system is projected to be as low as 1.8–2.0 GJ/tonne CO₂.
Furthermore, the system demonstrated the ability to achieve 99% CO₂ removal with only a
modest 10% increase in energy consumption, underscoring the high reactivity of piperazine and
its suitability for deep capture applications.

Solvent Performance and Stability
Piperazine demonstrated several advantages over conventional MEA, including resistance to
oxidative degradation, lower volatility, and reduced corrosivity. Throughout the testing
campaign, solvent degradation rates remained low, averaging less than 0.2 lb of piperazine per
tonne of CO₂ captured. Ammonia emissions, used as a proxy for oxidative degradation,
averaged approximately 6 ppmv, confirming the solvent’s stability under extended operation.

Thermal degradation, indicated by the accumulation of formate species, remained significantly
lower than in previous pilot campaigns, largely due to reduced NO₂ concentrations in the flue gas
at the NCCC. Additional mitigation strategies implemented during long-term testing—including
lowering the AFS sump level, nitrogen sparging, and thiosulfate addition to the pre-scrubber—
further reduced oxidative degradation and stabilized solvent composition.

 49

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Emissions and Aerosol Control
A critical aspect of solvent-based CO₂ capture is the management of solvent emissions,
particularly in the presence of aerosols formed by SO₃ in flue gas. During baseline operations at
NCCC, emissions were minimal due to low SO₃ concentrations. To simulate more challenging
conditions, SO₃ was intentionally injected into the flue gas during testing.

Results showed that piperazine emissions could be effectively controlled within 0.5–5 ppm by
adjusting absorber operating conditions, including increasing lean solvent temperature and
enhancing water wash performance. The addition of a second stage water wash significantly
improved aerosol removal efficiency by promoting particle growth and capture. These findings
demonstrate that emissions from piperazine systems can be managed effectively even under
elevated SO₃ conditions, which is critical for commercial deployment.

Operational Reliability and Solids Management
The PZAS system exhibited high operational reliability throughout the campaign. Notably, there
were no shutdowns attributable to the solvent or regeneration system itself, despite six
interruptions caused by external factors. The system operated stably across a range of
conditions, demonstrating robust process control and flexibility.

To mitigate the risk of solids formation, the solvent concentration was limited to 5 molal (30
wt.%), and the solvent inventory was stored in the CO₂-rich phase, where solubility is enhanced.
Although two instances of solids precipitation occurred, both were associated with non-standard
operating conditions and did not impact normal AFS operation. No precipitation events were
observed during steady-state operation, confirming the effectiveness of the chosen operating
strategy.

Corrosion Behavior and Materials Considerations
Corrosion analysis revealed mixed results depending on location and material. Carbon steel
exhibited generally acceptable corrosion rates (<100 µm/year) in most areas, with the formation
of protective FeCO₃ layers observed in some locations. However, elevated corrosion rates were
detected in high-temperature, high-velocity regions such as the hot lean solvent line.

Stainless steel, while typically more corrosion-resistant, showed unexpectedly high corrosion
rates in hot solvent environments, likely due to degradation of the protective oxide layer. These
findings suggest that strategic use of carbon steel could reduce capital costs in future systems,
although further long-term testing is required to confirm material suitability and durability.

Implications for Commercial Deployment
The results of this pilot-scale study demonstrate the strong potential of the PZAS process for
large-scale carbon capture applications. The system approaches DOE targets for second-
generation capture technologies in terms of energy efficiency and operational performance. The
reduced heat duty, combined with lower equipment footprint and potential capital cost savings,
positions the AFS configuration as a promising alternative to conventional stripping systems.

Additionally, the ability to operate at higher pressures offers advantages in downstream CO₂
compression, further improving overall process economics. The successful demonstration of

 50

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

long-term operation with minimal degradation and high reliability provides confidence in the
scalability of the technology.

2.12 UT-Austin PZAS Process Under NGCC Flue Gas Conditions (2019)

Following successful operation under coal-fired flue gas conditions (11% CO₂) in 2018, UT-
Austin, with funding from CCP4, completed a subsequent test campaign at the NCCC to evaluate
PZAS process performance under NGCC conditions (4.3% CO₂). The NGCC campaign utilized
aged solvent from the prior coal campaign and extended operation over a twelve-week period.
This study included multiple absorber configurations, oxidation mitigation techniques, and
corrosion monitoring to assess long-term operability and material compatibility. The primary
focus was to confirm the viability of the PZAS process under lower CO₂ concentration
conditions while maintaining high efficiency and manageable degradation rates.

Operational Stability and Process Conditions
Despite several potentially challenging operating conditions, the PZAS™ system demonstrated
strong stability and reliability throughout the NGCC campaign. Notably, there were no observed
precipitation issues in the piperazine solvent, even during shutdowns that required solvent
drainage and restart. This confirms the robustness of piperazine as a solvent under varying
operational states.

The absorber was operated at high liquid-to-gas ratios, handling approximately 8,000 lb/hr of
flue gas and 29,300 lb/hr of solvent flow in the packing section, including pump-around
circulation. Importantly, no flooding was observed, indicating that the system operated near its
hydraulic capacity without compromising performance.

Another key feature of the study was the direct contact of hot flue gas (up to 77°C) with rich
solvent at the bottom of the absorber. The absence of operational complications under these
conditions highlights the effectiveness of pump-around intercooling and suggests flexibility in
absorber design, potentially eliminating the need for a dedicated direct contact cooler.

The Advanced Stripper was operated across a temperature range of 139°C to 160°C and
pressures from 4.5 to 8 bar, with typical operation centered at 150°C and 6.3 bar. These elevated
conditions are essential to achieving effective solvent regeneration and high CO₂ purity.

Stripper Performance and Energy Efficiency
The PZAS process maintained strong energy performance across both coal and NGCC
conditions. At a rich loading of approximately 0.403 mol CO₂ per equivalent of piperazine, the
measured specific heat duty was 2.37 GJ/tonne CO₂ for both flue gas compositions. Under long-
term NGCC operation, an increased rich loading of 0.41 mol/mol further reduced the heat duty to
approximately 2.18 GJ/tonne CO₂.

These values are particularly notable given the lower CO₂ partial pressure in NGCC flue gas,
which typically results in higher energy requirements for solvent regeneration. The consistent
performance across conditions demonstrates the advantage of high-capacity piperazine solvent
and the efficiency of the Advanced Stripper design.

 51

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

It is important to note that the reported heat duty values were corrected for measured heat losses.
During the NGCC campaign, heat losses were estimated at approximately 0.078 GJ/hr,
representing nearly 30% of the total heat input. Additionally, corrections were applied to steam
flow measurements to account for discrepancies in density calibration. Alternative estimates
based on condensate measurements suggest that the true heat duty may be as low as 2.0–2.2
GJ/tonne CO₂.

At higher CO₂ removal rates, different trends were observed depending on flue gas composition.
While up to 98% CO₂ removal was achieved at coal conditions without significant increases in
heat duty, the NGCC system exhibited a sharp increase in energy consumption when removal
exceeded 95%. This indicates a practical optimization point for NGCC applications near 90–
95% capture.

Absorber Performance and Process Optimization
Absorber performance during the NGCC campaign was strongly influenced by solvent loading
and temperature control. Long-term operation with pump-around intercooling at 35°C enabled
CO₂ removal efficiencies of 89–90% while maintaining a rich loading between 0.40 and 0.41
mol CO₂ per equivalent piperazine.

In specific test runs, higher solvent flow rates and increased intercooling temperatures (up to
40°C) allowed for CO₂ removal up to 95%, though at lower rich loadings of approximately 0.37–
0.38 mol/mol. These findings demonstrate the trade-off between capture efficiency and solvent
loading, which directly affects overall process energy requirements.

Thermal and mass transfer analysis revealed that the absorber operates with a temperature bulge
that is less pronounced under NGCC conditions due to the lower CO₂ concentration.
Additionally, lower liquid-to-gas ratios tend to shift heat upward in the column, resulting in
cooler temperatures at the bottom section. This promotes lower CO₂ partial pressure and enables
higher solvent loading.

The absorber demonstrated excellent performance with only 40 feet of packing at near-maximum
gas throughput. Analysis suggests that the bottom section of packing may be reduced
significantly without compromising efficiency, as CO₂ flux approaches zero in this region. With
optimized design, the process could potentially operate with as little as 30 feet of packing,
reducing capital costs and equipment size.

Solvent Oxidation and Degradation
Oxidation of piperazine was more pronounced under NGCC conditions due to the higher oxygen
content (15% O₂ compared to 7% in coal flue gas). The measured oxidation rate was
approximately 0.106 mmol/kg solvent/hr, corresponding to 0.3 kg piperazine per tonne of CO₂
captured—three times higher than in the coal campaign.

Ammonia production, used as an indicator of oxidative degradation, increased accordingly.
However, the accumulation of other degradation products such as formate was significantly
lower than in previous pilot studies that involved NO₂ contamination. After 1,700 hours of
operation, formate concentrations were approximately eight times lower than those observed in
earlier campaigns with measurable NO₂ levels.

 52

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Implementation of activated carbon bed adsorption during the final 400 hours of operation
successfully removed colored degradation products and reduced ammonia formation rates.
While the carbon bed did not significantly remove iron, it effectively lowered chromium
concentrations and appeared to reduce both oxidation rates and corrosion tendencies.

Corrosion Behavior and Materials Performance
Corrosion testing revealed critical insights into material compatibility under high-temperature
PZAS conditions. Notably, 316L stainless steel exhibited unacceptable corrosion rates
exceeding 100 µm/year, particularly at stripper temperatures of 115–150°C. This degradation
was exacerbated by solvent aging and oxidative byproducts.

In contrast, 304 stainless steel and 2205 duplex stainless steel demonstrated strong corrosion
resistance across all tested conditions, identifying them as preferred materials for process
equipment. Carbon steel showed mixed performance; while acceptable under certain conditions,
corrosion increased significantly at elevated temperatures due to reliance on protective siderite
film formation.

High-alloy materials such as Hastelloy® C276 and Inconel® performed poorly, exhibiting
selective nickel dissolution in localized regions. These results highlight the importance of
selecting materials based on both chemical compatibility and mechanical stability under dynamic
process conditions.

The use of carbon bed treatment also contributed to reduced corrosion rates, likely due to the
removal of degradation products that promote corrosive environments.

Amine Emissions and Environmental Considerations
Amine emissions from the system remained low during the majority of the NGCC campaign.
Piperazine concentrations in the treated flue gas were below 0.3 ppm for approximately 2,900
hours of operation. However, a subsequent increase to 0.6–1.7 ppm was observed later in the
campaign.

This increase coincided with a shift in process configuration, where hot flue gas was introduced
directly into the absorber without prior cooling. The contact of hot gas with rich solvent likely
promoted aerosol formation, contributing to increased emissions. Despite this, overall emission
levels remained lower than those observed during the coal campaign, likely due to reduced SO₃
content in the NGCC flue gas.

Key Findings
Overall, the NGCC campaign confirmed that the PZAS™ process can achieve high CO₂ capture
efficiency with competitive energy performance under low CO₂ concentration conditions. The
process offers significant advantages in absorber design, solvent capacity, and operational
flexibility. However, challenges related to oxidation, corrosion, and emission control remain
critical areas for optimization in future studies.

 53

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

2.13 UT-Austin PZAS Oxidation Studies (2023)

UT-Austin conducted a third test campaign from December 2022 through October 2023 to
evaluate the performance and stability of the PZAS process under realistic flue gas conditions.
The system operated for approximately 5,900 hours using flue gas representative of that from an
NGCC unit (4.3% CO₂), with an average capture rate of ~6 tonne CO₂/day.

The primary objective of the campaign was to assess long-term solvent performance and identify
effective strategies to mitigate oxidative degradation. Four mitigation approaches were tested in
various combinations: (1) NO₂ pre-scrubbing, (2) nitrogen sparging to remove dissolved oxygen,
(3) activated carbon treatment, and (4) thermal reclaiming. In parallel, the campaign evaluated
absorber and stripper performance, energy requirements, emissions behavior, and solvent
degradation mechanisms under extended operation.

CO₂ Capture Performance and Energy Requirements
The PZAS process demonstrated sustained high capture efficiency throughout the campaign.
The absorber consistently achieved approximately 95.5% CO₂ removal, with a maximum
observed capture efficiency of 97.7% under optimized conditions. Process improvements,
including pump-around intercooling in the absorber, contributed to stable high capture rates
across varying operating conditions. The advanced stripper configuration operated at elevated
temperatures (~150°C), enabling effective CO₂ desorption with a measured net heat duty of
approximately 2.43 GJ/tonne of CO₂ captured.

Testing confirmed that high capture efficiency (93–97%) could be maintained alongside
manageable energy requirements, supporting the viability of the process for high-removal
applications. Heat duty measurements also highlighted sensitivity to operating variables such as
lean loading, solvent temperature, and flue gas conditions, emphasizing the importance of
process optimization in reducing energy consumption.

Oxidation Behavior and Degradation Indicators
Solvent degradation was primarily driven by oxidative reactions, with ammonia formation
serving as a key indicator. Ammonia concentrations in the treated flue gas initially ranged from
~1–3 ppm under controlled conditions but increased significantly when mitigation strategies
were removed. A notable “tipping point” occurred between approximately 4,300 and 4,900
operating hours, during which ammonia emissions rapidly increased from ~2.5 ppm to ~20 ppm,
indicating accelerated solvent degradation. Degradation products, including amino acids,
ethylenediamine, and heat-stable salts such as formate, accumulated steadily prior to this tipping
point. Total amino acid concentrations reached approximately 103 mmol/kg before declining
following solvent cleanup. These results indicate that solvent degradation follows a non-linear
progression, with relatively stable operation followed by rapid deterioration once critical
concentrations of intermediates and catalysts are reached.

Role of Flue Gas Impurities and Dissolved Oxygen
Nitrogen dioxide (NO₂) was identified as the dominant factor driving solvent oxidation.
Reducing NO₂ from ~2.5 ppm to ~0.5 ppm through pre-scrubbing decreased the oxidation rate
by approximately 50%, highlighting its strong catalytic effect.

 54

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Dissolved oxygen also contributed to degradation, particularly in downstream process regions.
Nitrogen sparging was effective in removing oxygen from solution, achieving greater than 75%
dissolved reduction and lowering oxidation rates. Measurements showed that most dissolved
oxygen was consumed through reaction with the solvent before reaching the stripper, indicating
that oxidation reactions occur primarily in the liquid phase rather than in downstream gas
streams.

Corrosion and Metal Accumulation
Episodes of localized corrosion were observed during the campaign, leading to increased
concentrations of dissolved metals such as iron, chromium, manganese, and nickel. These
metals contributed to solvent degradation by catalyzing oxidation reactions.

Two distinct corrosion events were detected, one associated with changes in NO₂ handling and
another coinciding with the degradation tipping point. Despite these events, corrosion coupons
indicated no widespread material degradation, suggesting that corrosion was limited to specific
system locations.

Accumulated metals were largely removed through solvent reclaiming, confirming their role as
reversible contributors to degradation rather than permanent system limitations.

Degradation Mitigation Strategies
Pre-scrubbing effectively reduced NO₂ concentrations and corresponding oxidation rates.
However, implementation requires additional chemicals and equipment, introducing cost and
complexity. As a result, while technically effective, pre-scrubbing may not represent a preferred
long-term solution compared to upstream NOₓ control technologies.

Nitrogen sparging reduced dissolved oxygen concentrations and associated oxidation rates.
Optimal performance was achieved at relatively low flow rates (~0.5 SCFM), representing less
than 0.03% of total gas flow, indicating that modest operational adjustments can deliver
measurable benefits.

Activated carbon treatment removed certain soluble degradation products and reduced solvent
discoloration but did not significantly reduce overall oxidation rates. In some cases, carbon beds
may have contributed to oxygen consumption reactions, indicating a complex role in system
chemistry.

Thermal reclaiming proved to be the most effective mitigation method. Vacuum distillation
removed a large fraction of degradation products and contaminants, including:

 • ~90–99% of dissolved metals and UV-active species
 • ~86–94% of amino acids and other degradation compounds
 • Significant reductions in ammonia emissions (to ~1.5 ppm)
Reclaiming also restored solvent performance and reduced oxidation rates by removing reactive
intermediates, effectively resetting the system after extended operation.

 55

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Emissions Performance
Ammonia emissions were strongly dependent on solvent condition and mitigation strategy,
ranging from ~1 ppm under controlled conditions to ~20 ppm during periods of accelerated
degradation. Implementation of an acid wash system significantly reduced ammonia emissions
to near-background levels (~20 ppb), while water wash systems were less effective. Total amine
emissions were also reduced to very low levels (~0.4 ppb) with acid wash operation. Hazardous
air pollutant emissions remained relatively low (50–270 ppb) and were largely unaffected by
wash configurations.

Key Findings and Implications
 • Sustained high CO₂ capture performance (>95%) was demonstrated over extended
 operation, with stable energy requirements under optimized conditions.
 • Solvent degradation is strongly influenced by flue gas composition, particularly NO₂, and
 follows a non-linear progression with a defined tipping point.
 • Thermal reclaiming is the most effective mitigation strategy, capable of restoring solvent
 conditions and reducing emissions.
 • Operational parameters (dissolved oxygen, temperature, contaminants) play a significant
 role in balancing performance, degradation, and emissions.
 • Carbon treating and sparging provide incremental benefits but are not sufficient alone to
 control long-term degradation.
The 2023 NCCC pilot campaign provided a detailed evaluation of the PZAS solvent system
under extended, realistic operating conditions. Results demonstrate that the process can maintain
high CO₂ capture efficiency with manageable energy demand, while also highlighting the central
role of solvent degradation management in long-term operation. Effective control of oxidation,
particularly through contaminant reduction and periodic reclaiming, was identified as a critical
requirement for maintaining performance and minimizing emissions.

2.14 Susteon Ionic Liquid-Catalyzed MEA

Susteon Inc. conducted pilot-scale testing using the PSTU to evaluate an ionic liquid-catalyzed
MEA solvent system. The work focused on enhancing conventional amine-based scrubbing by
incorporating a catalytic additive intended to improve absorption/desorption kinetics and reduce
overall regeneration energy requirements.

The primary objectives of the test campaign included:

 • Demonstrating the performance of MEA with and without the ionic catalyst under
 representative coal- and natural gas-derived flue gas conditions
 • Quantifying the impact of the catalyst on CO₂ capture efficiency, energy consumption,
 and kinetics
 • Generating experimental data to support process modeling and validation

 56

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 • Assessing the feasibility of catalytic enhancement as a pathway to improve the economics
 of solvent-based CO₂ capture systems
The test campaign consisted of a series of parametric and comparative operating tests using both
baseline MEA solvent and the ionic liquid–enhanced system. The study evaluated performance
under both coal-fired and NGCC-like flue gas compositions to enable comparison across a range
of CO₂ concentrations. Key elements of the methodology included:

 • Baseline testing of 30 wt.% MEA without catalyst to establish reference performance
 • Testing with MEA plus ionic catalyst under otherwise similar operating conditions
 • Adjustment of operating parameters such as solvent flow rate, temperature, and flue gas
 composition to evaluate performance sensitivity
 • Use of case studies and normalized comparisons to directly quantify the catalyst’s effect
 on process performance
Process modeling tools were also applied and calibrated using pilot data to validate predictions
of absorber and stripper behavior. This modeling component supports extrapolation of results to
larger-scale systems and evaluation of potential process improvements.

CO₂ Capture Performance
Testing demonstrated that both baseline MEA and the catalyzed system were capable of
achieving high levels of CO₂ removal under the tested conditions. The addition of the ionic
catalyst did not fundamentally change the achievable capture range but was evaluated in terms of
its ability to enhance reaction kinetics and improve process efficiency.

To enable a consistent comparison between baseline MEA and MEA with catalyst, a validated
process model was used to normalize key operating parameters, including amine concentration,
absorber and stripper temperature profiles, L/G ratio, CO₂ capture rate, and working capacity.
As shown in Figure 24, normalization of selected runs to common amine concentrations (23 -
31 wt%) indicates that the SRD for the catalyzed system was approximately 11-12% lower than
that of baseline MEA under comparable NGCC operating conditions.

 57

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 24. Model Prediction of SRDs with Normalized Amine Concentrations

A similar normalization approach was applied across multiple test runs under both NGCC and
coal flue gas conditions. The resulting trends, summarized in Table 8, indicate that the ionic
catalyst provided moderate reductions in regeneration energy, with the magnitude of
improvement varying with CO₂ removal level and operating conditions.

 Table 8. Improvement in SRD Values with the Addition of Susteon Catalyst After Normalization

Energy and Regeneration Behavior
A central focus of the project was the potential for catalyst-enhanced solvents to reduce thermal
energy demand in the stripper, which is a primary contributor to the cost of amine-based CO₂
capture. Results indicated that the presence of the catalyst affected the desorption characteristics
of the solvent, consistent with the intended mechanism of improving regeneration efficiency.
However, the degree of energy reduction observed at pilot scale was influenced by system
integration factors, including:

 • Heat transfer limitations
 • Process configuration
 • Operational constraints inherent to the PSTU

 58

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

As a result, while improvements in reaction behavior were observed, overall energy reductions
were modest and dependent on operating conditions, highlighting the importance of integrated
process optimization.

Modeling and Validation
The test data were used to validate process models describing absorber and stripper performance.
Model predictions showed reasonable agreement with measured data, supporting their use for
design studies and scale-up evaluations. Modeling results further indicated that the benefits of
catalytic enhancement may be more significant under optimized commercial scale
configurations, where process conditions can be more tightly controlled and energy integration
strategies can be more fully implemented.

Operational Considerations
The campaign provided insights into the operational behavior of catalytic solvent systems in
pilot-scale equipment. No major operability barriers were identified that would preclude further
development; however, the results highlighted several considerations relevant to scale-up:

 • The interaction between catalyst and solvent chemistry must be managed to ensure
 consistent performance over time.
 • System performance is sensitive to operating conditions, requiring careful control of
 temperature, solvent loading, and flow rates.
 • Integration of catalyst-enhanced systems into conventional amine processes may require
 modifications to process design or operating strategy to capture full performance benefits.
These findings are consistent with broader NCCC experience that emerging solvent technologies
must be evaluated not only for intrinsic properties but also for system-level performance in
realistic operating environments.

Implications for Scale-Up and Deployment
The results of this campaign contribute to the broader understanding of catalyst-enhanced solvent
systems as a potential pathway to improve the performance and economics of post-combustion
CO₂ capture. The data generated provide:

 • A validated experimental basis for evaluating catalytic solvent performance
 • Input for process modeling and techno-economic analysis
 • Insight into design and operational considerations for future development
Susteon identified the need for additional evaluation of the catalyst-enhanced solvent under a
broader range of operating conditions, along with further analysis to support process
optimization and improve overall performance assessment of the system.

2.15 ExxonMobil/MHI Solvents (2024)

Kansai Electric Power Co., Inc. (KEPCO) and MHI, Ltd. (MHI) have developed the KM CDR
Process™, a post-combustion CO₂ capture technology that has been commercially deployed

 59

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

across a range of industrial applications. Through a technology alliance with ExxonMobil, MHI
has expanded development and validation of advanced solvent systems, leveraging combined
operational and research expertise to advance carbon capture performance.

From February through April 2024, ExxonMobil and MHI conducted a test campaign with two
MHI solvents, the commercial KS-21™ and a novel Solvent B in the PSTU to collect data on
emissions and energy consumption. The KS-21 solvent was tested for 590 hours, and Solvent B
was tested for 850 hours. Testing included both parametric operation to assess sensitivity to
process variables and extended degradation testing to evaluate solvent stability over time.
Operating conditions were controlled to maintain a nominal 95% CO₂ capture rate, with flue gas
derived from the NCCC’s natural gas boiler and diluted with air to simulate consistent inlet

Daily sampling of lean solvent, rich solvent, and wash water was performed, with analytical
measurements conducted at both NCCC and ExxonMobil laboratories. Solvent emission data
was collected continuously using a Fourier transform infrared spectrometer, the University of
Oslo’s Proton Transfer Reaction-Time of Flight (PTR-TOF) mass spectrometer, and a manual
impinger and sorbent sampling train.

CO₂ Capture Performance
Both solvents demonstrated stable and repeatable capture performance, maintaining the target
capture rate throughout testing. KS-21 maintained a 95% capture over almost 600 hours of
degradation testing with no observable decline in performance. Solvent B achieved similar
performance over 850 hours, also without measurable degradation in capture efficiency. Capture
rates remained stable except during occasional operational disturbances, indicating strong system
controllability.

Sensitivity to Operating Conditions
For variation in the L/G ratio, key differences were observed between solvents. With KS-21, the
CO₂ capture efficiency increased with the L/G ratio due to improved gas-liquid contact and
reduced solvent loading. For Solvent B, the capture performance showed minimal dependence
on the L/G ratio, suggesting faster absorption kinetics and near-equilibrium behavior across
tested conditions. Similarly, when testing a 50% reduction in flue gas flow, KS-21 showed a
slight improvement in capture efficiency due to increased residence time, but no significant
impact was observed for Solvent B, with result remaining within normal variability.

Energy Consumption
Steam flow data were collected for energy assessment; however, a steam leak impacted
measurement reliability. The leak prevented calculation of validated regeneration energy
consumption. After identification and correction of the leak, a 20–25% reduction in steam usage
was observed, indicating earlier readings were overstated. As a result, energy performance
comparisons between solvents could not be completed.

Solvent Degradation Results
Both solvents exhibited time-dependent degradation, with byproduct accumulation observed in
liquid samples. Degradation impacts solvent availability and can affect process conditions over

 60

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

time. A strong correlation was identified between oxygen concentration in flue gas and
degradation rate.

For KS-21, degradation increased in a linear trend over the 22-day test period, as shown in
Figure 25. Higher degradation rates were observed during periods of elevated oxygen
concentration. Under steady conditions, degradation proceeded at a slower and more stable rate.
This behavior indicates predictable degradation kinetics with clear sensitivity to operating
environment.

 Figure 25. KS-21 Degradation over Time

Solvent B exhibited more complex degradation behavior but overall improved performance.
Initial degradation rates were similar to those of KS-21 under comparable conditions. At the
higher CO₂ concentration (8%), degradation rates decreased slightly. During steady-state testing,
degradation followed a linear trend at a lower rate than KS-21. By the end of testing,
degradation byproduct concentrations approached a plateau, indicating stabilization. As with
KS-21, degradation trends were strongly influenced by oxygen concentration.

 Figure 26. Solvent B Degradation over Time

 61

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Key Findings
 • Stable operation maintained across all test phases
 • CO₂ capture rate of ~95% consistently achieved by both solvents
 • Solvent B showed:
  Reduced sensitivity to L/G
  Slightly lower degradation rates
  Evidence of stabilization in long-term operation
 • Oxygen concentration was identified as a key driver of degradation kinetics
 • Energy consumption measurements were inconclusive due to a steam system issue
The PSTU campaign demonstrated that both KS-21™ and Solvent B provide reliable
performance under pilot-scale conditions. While overall capture performance was comparable,
Solvent B showed advantages in reduced sensitivity to operating conditions and improved
degradation behavior. The results highlight the importance of controlling operating
environment, particularly oxygen exposure, while continuing to refine solvent formulations for
improved long-term stability.

2.16      ExxonMobil/MHI Solvents (2025-2026)

In 2025-2026, ExxonMobil/MHI conducted a test campaign in the PSTU with a new solvent to
compare performance against commercial solvents and validate pilot-scale data previously
developed by the ExxonMobil/MHI team. The test campaign was conducted using the PSTU,
which was modified prior to testing to enhance process flexibility and emissions control
capability. Modifications included converting the fourth bed of the absorber to an independent
wash section, allowing operation with either two water washes in series or a water wash followed
by an acid wash, and installation of a proprietary demister. These changes provide increased
flexibility for process configuration and will support future testing activities at the NCCC.

The campaign commenced on September 12, 2025, following solvent loading, and continued
through February 4, 2026, achieving approximately 2,100 hours of operation. Testing was
conducted under natural gas-derived flue gas conditions, primarily at 4% CO₂, with selected
periods at 8% CO₂ to assess performance across a broader operating range.

A comprehensive sampling and monitoring program was implemented, including daily liquid
sampling of wash water and solvent streams, as well as continuous gas-phase monitoring using
FTIR and PTR-TOF mass spectrometer. Additional measurements included dissolved oxygen in
the solvent and oxygen concentration in the CO₂ product stream, supporting detailed evaluation
of system performance and solvent behavior.

Process Performance and Operating Sensitivities
Parametric testing was conducted to evaluate the effect of key process variables, including L/G
ratio, regenerator steam input, and flue gas CO₂ concentration. Increasing the L/G ratio
improved CO₂ capture by enhancing gas-liquid contact and reducing solvent CO₂ loading.
However, the new solvent achieved target capture performance at lower L/G ratios, indicating

 62

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

improved absorption efficiency and faster reaction kinetics. Figure 27 shows the relationship
between the L/G ratio and CO₂ capture performance, illustrating the ability of the solvent to
achieve target capture at reduced circulation rates.

 Figure 27. Effect of L/G Ratio on CO₂ Capture Rate for ExxonMobil/MHI Solvent

Regenerator performance showed a direct and predictable relationship between steam input and
capture performance. Increasing steam flow reduced lean solvent loading and improved capture
efficiency. Higher flue gas CO₂ concentrations (8% vs. 4%) required increased steam input, but
the overall response followed a consistent trend, indicating stable and controllable regeneration
behavior across operating conditions.

Absorber performance reflects the interaction of multiple rate-controlled phenomena, including
gas–liquid mass transfer, diffusion, and intrinsic reaction kinetics. The observed behavior
confirms that absorber operation is governed by time-dependent transport and reaction processes,
and not solely by thermodynamic equilibrium, highlighting the importance of rate-based
modeling approaches for predicting performance.

Operational Stability
The system operated reliably over approximately 2,100 hours, demonstrating stable performance
under pilot-scale conditions. CO₂ capture remained consistently near the target value, with
deviations primarily associated with intentional changes in operating conditions or transient
operational events. No decline in performance was observed over approximately 2,000 hours of
sustained operation, indicating stable absorber and regenerator behavior.

 63

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Solvent Stability and Degradation Behavior
Following parametric testing, a degradation study was conducted to evaluate long-term solvent
stability under controlled operating conditions. Analytical results demonstrated that solvent
degradation rates were strongly dependent on operating severity, particularly regenerator
temperature and flue gas composition. During the initial phase of testing, operation at elevated
regenerator pressure (~50 psig) resulted in higher bottoms temperatures (~145 °C) and
accelerated solvent degradation. When operating conditions were adjusted to standard levels
(~11 psig and ~120 °C), the degradation rate decreased significantly, demonstrating strong
sensitivity of solvent stability to thermal conditions. Figure 28 presents the degradation trends
observed over time, including the impact of operating conditions such as flue gas composition
and regenerator temperature.

 Figure 28. ExxonMobil/MHI Solvent Degradation Trends as a Function of Operating Time

Degradation behavior also showed correlation with oxidizing species such as oxygen and NO₂,
with elevated concentrations contributing to increased solvent breakdown. These findings
indicate that solvent durability is influenced by both process design and operating environment,
emphasizing the importance of controlling temperature and flue gas composition to minimize
degradation. Overall, long-term data indicate that the new solvent exhibits improved stability
under optimized operating conditions, supporting its potential for sustained operation in post-
combustion CO₂ capture systems.

Key Findings
The results from this campaign provide several key insights into the performance of the new
solvent formulation:

 • The solvent achieved target CO₂ capture performance at reduced L/G ratios, indicating
 improved absorption kinetics and potential reductions in solvent circulation requirements
 • Regeneration performance demonstrated a predictable response to steam input,
 supporting controllable operation across varying conditions

 64

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 • Stable operation was maintained over approximately 2,000 hours with no observed
 performance degradation
 • Solvent degradation was strongly influenced by:
  Regenerator temperature
  Operating pressure
  Presence of O₂ and NO₂ in the flue gas
 • Lower degradation rates were observed under standard operating conditions, indicating
 improved solvent durability

2.17 EPRI/PNNL/RTI Water-Lean Solvent

Researchers at PNNL have developed a new water-lean solvent, N-(2-ethoxyethyl)-3-
morpholinopropan-1-amine (EEMPA), for post-combustion capture of CO2 from coal and
natural gas flue gas. EEMPA has multiple characteristics that make it an interesting option for
reducing the cost of CO2 capture from these types of plants, including the potential to achieve
specific reboiler duties below 2.5 GJ/t CO2 and not requiring a diluent component even at very
low (<10 wt%) water content. EPRI, PNNL, RTI International, and their project collaborators
developed an engineering-scale test of EEMPA using the PSTU. The primary objective of this
test was to collect long-term data operating EEMPA with both coal- and natural gas-
representative flue gases at the ~0.5 MWe-equivalent scale.

Over 2,300 hours on stream were accumulated over the course of the campaign across three
different gas compositions. Table 9 summarizes the total operating hours and average CO2
concentration, and source for each flue gas type used in the campaign. Over the course of the
campaign, the CO2 capture rate ranged widely across the flue gas conditions, including periods
of operation above 90%. Over 700 hours were at conditions above 90% capture, with
approximately 318 of those hours above 95% capture.

 Table 9. Hours on Stream for EEMPA Testing with Different Flue Gas Sources
 Average CO₂ Average
 Flue Gas Source Concentration Hours on Stream SRD
                                          (vol%, dry)                                 (GJ/t CO₂)

             Gaston Unit 5 coal       12.14±0.415        167                      -

                                      8.28±0.226         1,197                    4.5±0.4

                                      4.00±0.093         954                      6.9±1.7

             Total                                       2,318

Table 9 also reports SRDs for the undiluted and diluted natural gas boiler conditions. SRDs
were higher than anticipated due to lower-than-expected heat transfer performance, resulting in
high absorber temperatures (and resulting lower-than-expected rich loading) and suboptimal heat

 65

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

recovery with the cross-flow heat exchanger. In addition, solvent purity started below targets
and declined more quickly than anticipated.

The test demonstrated the ability to control the water balance in the solvent, primarily through
adjustments to the absorber inlet gas temperature. The solvent was operated with water contents
as low as 0.9 wt% and up to over 10 wt% and achieved greater than 90% capture efficiency at all
water content levels in between.

Early in the campaign, it was discovered that the solvent had a propensity to form an aerosol or
fog in the regenerator column. A variety of experiments were conducted to attempt to determine
factors that lead to the fog, including varying reboiler circulation rate, changing reboiler output
temperature, use of cold rich bypass to the top of the regenerator, variation in water content,
regenerator operating pressure, and the presence of CO2 in the regenerator. The only factor that
appeared to directly affect the presence of the fog was weather flue gas was being fed to the
system, implying the presence of CO2 was an important contributing factor.

A major factor in the test was the lower-than-desired starting purity of the solvent, around
80 wt%, which also continued to decline more rapidly than expected from prior tests. It was
thought that this decline in EEMPA concentration was due to a combination of essentially
immediate dissolution of parts-per-million levels of iron and manganese in the solvent, the
interaction of high solvent viscosity and high steam temperatures leading to potential high skin
temperatures in the reboiler, and the complex mixture of impurities what may have contributed
to previously unobserved side reactions.

The test campaign verified the ability of EEMPA to achieve greater than 90% capture, even at
suboptimal thermal conditions. Water control was successfully demonstrated, and solvent
viscosity, although higher than expected, did not hinder stable operation of the PSTU.

The test highlighted the importance of using high-purity solvent when the properties are based on
a single amine component with a small amount of water. Elevated levels of impurities,
especially morpholine, may have contributed in a material way to the performance of the solvent,
masking the specific attributes of EEMPA.

Heat exchange limitations were also an important factor, which highlights the need for careful
consideration of the design and behavior of each exchanger while assuming solvent properties
are not precisely as measured in the lab, but will be operating under the influence of degradation
products and heat-stable salts that may raise the viscosity.

The test also lost time due to incompatibility between some of the pump seals and the solvent.
This reinforces the need for careful screening and materials compatibility testing, ideally with the
exact solvent composition to be used in the test and not highly purified samples.

2.18 University of Edinburgh Solvent Analyzer

During the 2014 MEA testing performed for the DOE’s CCSI, the University of Edinburgh
tested a real-time solvent analysis device developed to determine solvent concentration and
loading within a ten-second response window. This near real-time response is a significant

 66

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

improvement over current state-of-the-art technologies for solvent analysis that can take up to
30 minutes to provide data. The University of Edinburgh device would allow for changes in
process variables such as load demand in a commercial carbon capture process. The test
apparatus uses inferential solvent analysis to measure solvent concentration and loading online.
The advantages of the device over commercially available systems include:

 • Simple on- or off-line calibration with resilience to solvent complexities
 • Significantly smaller capital costs and maintenance costs
 • Compatible with automated control systems and data logging

During the field test at the NCCC, the sensor performed well and produced values of solvent
loading consistent with titrated samples. Figure 29 plots the continuous measurements of CO₂
loading and amine concentration taken by the analyzer. Points on the graph correspond to
discrete samples taken and analyzed in the lab using standard industry methods, and the
statistical error ranges for the analysis performed are provided for each sample point.

 Figure 29. Data from the University of Edinburgh Solvent Analyzer

Following the successful NCCC campaign, the online solvent analyzer was evaluated during a
three-month test campaign at SaskPower's Carbon Capture Test Facility. The instrument proved
to be robust and reliable, successfully tracking transitions to different plant operating modes and
providing operators with process insights to improve plant reliability, while not adding
significant measurement uncertainty compared to lab-based analysis methods.

 67

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

2.19 Cansolv Aerosol Mitigation Processes

This Cansolv project focused on the evaluation of technologies able to mitigate amine losses in a
carbon capture process by reducing the formation of amine-containing aerosols. These particles
are extremely difficult to capture due to their small size, and they typically leave the absorption
process and are emitted to the atmosphere via the stack. Technologies selected by Cansolv for
evaluation included a TSA and a BDU installed on the SSTU. The TSA was installed on the flue
gas supply line to remove aerosol-producing constituents, such as SO₂ and SO₃. The BDU vessel
was installed on the treated flue gas stream exiting the wash tower to remove aerosols that may
form in the CO₂ removal process. The equipment was tested in 2015.

The preliminary test objectives were accomplished using MEA, although sufficient time was not
available to test the equipment using Cansolv’s proprietary solvents. Completed tests included
TSA operation with SO₃ measurements and aerosol mitigation tests with ELPI+ measurements
taken by Southern Research. Figure 30 compares results from ELPI+ measurements taken on the
treated flue gas stream (wash tower outlet) without the TSA in operation and two measurements
at the same sampling point with the TSA in operation. Very little difference was observed in the
distribution and number count of aerosols at the operating condition.

Another test was conducted the following day at a different operating condition, and the results
are plotted in Figure 31. A noticeable increase of aerosols in the <0.03 micron range was
