On the last day of testing, data was gathered at the BDU exit to ascertain its collection efficiency.
The BDU outlet data showed some unexpected fluctuations, and the results were not considered
valid since current was observed across multiple impactor stages. Liquid droplets had collected
in several stages of the ELPI+ impactor, and the droplets may have contained solvent that was
lost from the absorber. Further detailed aerosol characterizations at the BDU outlet would be
necessary to determine the BDU performance.

2.20 RTI International Non-Aqueous Solvent Process

RTI has been developing a unique, non-aqueous CO₂ capture solvent process since 2010.
Compared to MEA, RTI’s solvent is effective at higher solvent regeneration pressure, reducing
the subsequent compression energy requirements. The RTI solvent also shows a lower corrosion
rate and higher thermal and oxidative stability than MEA. RTI tested its non-aqueous solvent in
the SSTU during 2018. The solvent had already been evaluated at smaller scales, so the focus at
NCCC was on how it behaves under real coal-fired flue gas conditions over extended operation
rather than proving basic performance. The main reason for bringing the solvent to the SSTU
was to investigate issues that tend to arise only under realistic conditions, including:

 • Solvent degradation over time
 • Corrosion impacts
 • Air emissions and solvent losses
 • General operability with coal-derived flue gas
Testing at NCCC provided access to a slipstream of actual flue gas from Plant Gaston Unit 5,
which includes contaminants (e.g., SOₓ-derived species, particulates) that are difficult to fully
replicate in lab setups.

 69

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Operating Conditions and Test Matrix
The evaluation included extended operation under coal-derived flue gas conditions with
controlled variation of key process parameters. Total operating time exceeded 500 hours,
enabling assessment of both steady-state and transient behavior.

Key operating variables evaluated include:

 • Lean solvent CO₂ loading
 • Flue gas velocity through the absorber
 • Temperature profiles within the absorber
 • CO₂ capture rate and efficiency
 • Aerosol concentration in the flue gas
Controlled addition of sulfur trioxide (SO₃) at concentrations of approximately 1–6 ppm was
used to simulate aerosol formation and evaluate its impact on emissions and solvent loss.

Process Performance
The system maintained stable operation over extended periods, demonstrating consistent
absorber and regenerator functionality under varying operating conditions. Continuous operation
for approximately 580 hours confirmed system reliability and the ability to sustain performance
with coal-derived flue gas.

Adjustments to operating parameters, including lean loading and temperature approach,
influenced CO₂ capture efficiency and system throughput. Reduced temperature differences
between lean solvent and absorber bulge regions were found to be important in maintaining
stable performance and minimizing operational variability.

Solvent Stability and Composition
Solvent samples were collected periodically throughout the system to evaluate chemical
composition and degradation. Analyses included:

 • Amine concentration
 • Water content
 • CO₂ loading (rich and lean)
 • Formation of degradation products
The results indicate gradual solvent degradation during extended exposure to flue gas. However,
the observed degradation did not prevent continued operation or significantly disrupt system
performance over the duration of testing.

Water accumulation was observed in the solvent system over time, requiring periodic
management. This behavior reflects interactions between the solvent and moisture present in the
flue gas and highlights the importance of maintaining an appropriate water balance in low-water
systems.

 70

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Emissions and Aerosol Effects
Emissions were evaluated through sampling of the treated gas stream and analysis of condensate
and sorbent-collected species. Both vapor-phase emissions and aerosol-based solvent losses
were quantified. The presence of aerosols was identified as a dominant factor influencing
solvent loss. Periods of SO₃ injection, which increased aerosol formation, resulted in
significantly higher emissions compared to baseline conditions. Aerosol measurements were
conducted using real-time particle monitoring equipment, and emissions were further
characterized by collecting samples from the water wash section. These results demonstrate that:

 • Aerosol formation enhances solvent entrainment
 • Emissions are strongly dependent on flue gas composition
 • Control of aerosol precursors is critical for minimizing losses
Figure 32 shows the effect of the SO₃ injection on the aerosol emissions by comparing the
average distributions from the four different tests. Large size aerosols (> 0.1 µm) increased in
concentration from 105 to 106 from the flue gas inlet to the wash tower outlet due to
accumulation of water, amines, and degradation products from the solvent. During the SO₃
injections, the smallest size aerosols (0.006 µm) increased from 108 number concentration to 109,
whereas larger size aerosols (0.35 µm) increased from 106 to 107 due to an increased number of
nucleation sites. The tests with SO₃ injections were repeatable even with the particulate
accumulation within the ELPI+ instrument.

  Figure 32. Comparison of Particle Number Concentrations During Baseline and SO₃ Injection Tests

Corrosion and Materials Evaluation
Corrosion was evaluated through exposure of system materials and analysis of solvent samples.
The results indicate that corrosion occurred under certain operating conditions, and the extent of
corrosion is dependent on solvent composition and flue gas impurities.

 71

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Material compatibility remains an important design consideration, particularly in systems with
reduced water content and different chemical environments compared to conventional aqueous
systems.

Operational Observations
Several operational considerations were identified during testing:

 • Water Management—Water introduced through the flue gas and wash sections
 accumulated in the system and required periodic adjustment.
 • Parameter Sensitivity—Performance was sensitive to operating conditions such as lean
 loading, gas velocity, and temperature profiles.
 • Aerosol Influence—Emissions and solvent losses were strongly influenced by aerosol
 formation, particularly under elevated SO₃ conditions.
These observations highlight the need for careful optimization of process conditions during
scale-up.

Key Findings
 • The non-aqueous solvent demonstrated stable operation over extended testing periods
 exceeding 500 hours.
 • Process performance is strongly influenced by operating parameters, particularly solvent
 loading and temperature control.
 • Solvent degradation occurs but remained manageable within the tested time frame.
 • Aerosol formation is a dominant mechanism for solvent loss and must be addressed for
 effective emissions control.
 • Water balance management is required despite the low-water design of the solvent
 system.

2.21 NETL Fiber Optic-Based Sensors

NETL is advancing fiber‑optic-based sensing systems at the NCCC to enable real‑time,
distributed monitoring of carbon capture processes and associated infrastructure under actual
plant operating conditions. These sensors integrate functional coatings and nanomaterials onto
optical fibers, allowing them to detect CO₂ and other gases along the length of the fiber with high
sensitivity while simultaneously capturing temperature and strain data. Unlike conventional
point sensors, the technology provides continuous, kilometer‑scale spatial coverage, supporting
monitoring, reporting, and verification of CO₂ capture and handling systems.

The sensors are capable of real‑time monitoring of liquids—such as detecting solvent
degradation—and gases, including measuring CO₂ concentrations in flue gas. NETL is assessing
the sensor technology using MEA solvent in the SSTU. A total of six sensor locations were
selected, two for gas and four for liquids. Testing began in the third quarter of 2025 and will
continue through June 2026.

 72

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

2.22 LLNL 3D-Printed Structured Column Packings

LLNL is demonstrating advanced structured packings fabricated using additive manufacturing at
the NCCC. These 3D‑printed designs feature tailored surface geometries and controlled channel
architectures that improve wettability, liquid distribution, and interfacial area, enabling enhanced
gas–liquid contact while minimizing pressure drop and maintaining high liquid handling
capacity. Lab testing has confirmed improved mass transfer performance relative to
conventional packings, and the technology has been scaled for validation in the SSTU using
MEA solvent. Testing began in the third quarter of 2025 and is planned for completion at the
end of July 2026. The campaign is being conducted in parallel with NETL’s sensor evaluations,
supporting integrated assessment of next‑generation carbon capture system performance under
realistic operating conditions.

2.23 MTR 1-Ton/Day CO₂ Membrane System

Beginning in 2011, MTR tested polymeric Polaris™ membranes for separating CO₂ from coal-
derived flue gas at the NCCC. Final testing was completed in August 2015. As shown by the
process diagram in Figure 33, the 1-TPD system removes CO₂ from flue gas in two steps. The
first step uses cross-flow modules with a vacuum on the permeate for CO₂ enrichment, and the
second step uses counter-current sweep air to remove additional CO₂ in the feed gas to meet the
overall capture target of 90%. Before entering membrane modules, the gas is compressed to 20
to 30 psig to generate the necessary pressure ratio for parametric studies.

 Figure 33. Schematic of MTR Flue Gas Membrane Skid

Operations of the 1-TPD unit during the contract period took place in 2014 and 2015. The
testing employed high-flux Polaris cross-flow modules in the first step and sweep modules with
high selectivity Polaris membranes in the second step. This combination of modules was used
for the first time in the PO-2 run from January to March 2015. The objectives were to determine
module performance with coal generated flue gas in the field, and to test module stability over
time and with the shutdowns and restarts that are inherent in plant operations.

 73

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Figure 34 demonstrates the performance of the system from January to July 2015. For the
operating periods with flue gas, the feed gas contained about 12% CO₂. The step-one permeate
ranged from 50 to 70% CO₂, representing an enrichment factor of four to six. The system
generated a treated flue gas between 2 and 5% CO₂ as the second step residue stream. The
restart of operations in June 2015 after an extended shutdown showed that stable operations
continued.
  CO2 Content, %

 CO2 Content, %
 Run Time, hours
 Figure 34. CO₂ Content of Feed, Permeate, and Residue Streams of MTR 1-TPD System from January
 through July 2015

The period of operations in early 2015 was defined by the external winter weather, with feed
streams cooling from around 27°C (80°F) at the start to 13°C (55°F). Lower feed temperature
resulted in lower permeate flux in both stages, lower stage cut in step one, and higher CO₂
concentration in the first step permeate, first step residue, and second step residue (the treated
flue gas). The fairly steady trend to cooler temperature meant a steady shift in observed CO₂
content as the modules responded over time. These results were consistent with those from
previous extended trials in respect to the impacts of operating temperature.

For a brief period from 833 to 855 hours the feed switched from pulverized coal flue gas to
simulated natural gas flue gas at 4% CO₂ content. The permeate from step one fell to about 40%
CO₂, representing an enrichment factor of about 10. With the lower CO₂ content in the feed
compared to coal flue gas, the second step residue was reduced to less than 2% CO₂. Upon
returning to 12% CO₂ feed, the performance recovered, with CO₂ in the first step permeate
returning to about 60% CO₂.

With the restart for run PO-3 in June 2015, the downtime was over 1,700 hours. Figure 35 and
Figure 36 detail the operations in June and July 2015. The key parameters governing system
performance are the feed flow rate, the feed gas temperature, and the sweep flow rate. Upon
restart, the feed rate to the system approached 200 kscfd, which increased the second step residue
to just over 5% CO₂. Lowering the feed rate to 150 kscfd returned the second step residue to
under 4% CO₂. In mid-June, the feed temperature to the membrane system increased to about
38°C (100°F). This increased the permeate rate, which increased stage cut, with the net result of

 74

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

lower CO₂ content in the first step permeate and in the second step residue. As seen previously,
the first step increased the CO₂ concentration from 12% in the feed to over 60% in the permeate.
The second step residue ranged from 2 to 7% CO₂. The sweep rate was intentionally varied
between 20 to 70 kscfd, affecting the step two residue CO₂ concentration and the composition of
the sweep out gas.

 and July 2015

 Figure 36. Operating Conditions for MTR 1-TPD System during June and July 2015

At 4,020 run hours, the feed rate was lowered to about 100 kscfd, which increased stage cut and
generated immediate changes in the first step permeate and the second step residue, both again to
lower CO₂ content. At the end of June, the sweep rate in step two was both lowered and raised
by 15 kscfd. This changed the both the pressure drop across the sweep side of the module and
the dilution rate of the permeate CO₂ for the second step. Increasing the sweep rate slightly
improved the amount of CO₂ captured by the sweep modules. Overall, the sweep flow rate had
much less impact on system performance compared to the feed flow rate or temperature.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Readings for CO₂ concentration were not available on July 16 starting at the run clock of
4,592 hours, and the flow and pressure data collection were ceased on July 17 at 4,622 hours.
The system achieved over six months of operations with this combination of cross-flow and
sweep modules. The modules continued to show stable operations even with the excursions in
feed temperature and flow rates.

After three and half years of testing, the system achieved a total of 11,466 hours of operation
with 9,100 hours on flue gas and 2,366 hours on air when flue gas was not available. Out of the
9,100 hours of testing on flue gas, 1,933 hours were conducted with simulated natural gas flue
gas. Carbon capture rates higher than 90% were achieved with coal-derived flue gas. The test
program proved valuable in guiding the design of MTR’s 20-TPD membrane system and in
serving as a test vehicle for second and third generation membranes following lab testing.

2.24 MTR 20-Ton/Day CO₂ Membrane System

In 2014, MTR completed installation and commissioning of the scaled-up, 20-ton/day CO₂
membrane system. The system first operated in 2015 during run PO-2, and for PO-3 operation, a
high efficiency plate-and-frame membrane module was added to replace the conventional spiral-
wound design. Figure 37 provides a photograph of the MTR system at the NCCC.

 Plate-and-Frame 20-TPD Small
 Sweep Module Skid Pilot System
 Figure 37. MTR 20-TPD Membrane System with Plate-and-Frame Sweep Module Skid

The system was commissioned on flue gas in January 2015. During PO-2, the system ran
intermittently on flue gas due to cold weather issues on the NCCC side, miscellaneous system
issues on the MTR side, and the installation of the plate-and-frame sweep module skid. In early
March, cold weather cooling water supply issues on the NCCC side tripped the 20-TPD system
causing damage to a critical flow switch. Due to the lead time of a replacement part and
scheduling considerations, the 20-TPD system did not run for the remainder of PO-2. During

 76

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

PO-2, the 20-TPD system operated on flue gas for approximately 325 hours with CO₂ capture
rates ranging from 85 to 95%.

The main goal of the PO-3 campaign was to operate the plate-and-frame sweep skid as the
second step of the 20-TPD system for a minimum of 500 hours to validate the lab performance
values and demonstrate the stability of the new module design under real coal-fired flue gas
conditions. Another goal was to determine the various operating parameters of the 20-TPD
system under summer conditions, with ambient temperatures of 32°C (90°F) and higher, as
compared to the sub-freezing winter conditions of PO-2.

Downtime of the 20-TPD system was minimized during PO-3 due to lessons learned during
PO-2. As shown in Figure 38, the 20-TPD system with the plate-and-frame sweep skid
consistently captured CO₂ at a rate of around 87%, with capture rates over 90% under certain
operating conditions.

 Figure 38. CO₂ Capture Rate for MTR 20-TPD System during PO-3

Figure 39 shows an example of the CO₂ enrichment produced by the membrane system by
tracking the CO₂ content of various gas streams over the period of June 10 through 13. As
shown in the figure, the typical flue gas feed to the membrane systems was 11-12% CO₂. The
first step permeate was enriched about four times to 45% CO₂, while the treated gas leaving in
the residue stream contained about 2% CO₂. This performance is very similar to that achieved
by the 1-TPD system and agrees with MTR’s design calculations. For an integrated system (as
opposed to the slipstream testing at the NCCC), the CO₂ recycled to the boiler would increase the
feed CO₂ content to around 20% (instead of 11% at NCCC). Thus, with an expected permeate
enrichment of three to four times, this stream would contain about 70% CO₂, which would then
be sent to a liquefaction system for purification and pumping to sequestration pressures.

In summary, the 20 TPD system was successfully installed, commissioned, and operated during
two post-combustion campaigns at NCCC. The system ran on flue gas for 900 hours, exceeding
the original goal of 500 hours, and demonstrated the performance of both the 20-TPD system and
the plate-and-frame sweep skid. Following the run, the system was decommissioned and
transported to another test site. Highlights include:

 • Stable system operation meeting design specifications under both sub-freezing winter
 conditions and high-temperature, high humidity summer conditions
 • 400 hours of operation during PO-2 with CO₂ capture rates ranging from 85 to 95% with
 spirals as the sweep step
 • 900 hours of stable operation during PO-3 with CO₂ capture rates over 85% with the
 plate-and-frame module design as the sweep step
 • About four times lower sweep-side pressure drop for plate-and-frame sweep modules
 compared to spiral sweep modules operated under the same field test conditions

At the conclusion of testing at the NCCC, MTR moved the 20-TPD unit to a Babcock & Wilcox
facility for testing with a pilot coal-fired boiler for the first demonstration of CO₂ recycle to a
boiler by a membrane process. Parametric testing will clarify the pros/cons of recycle/no-recycle
for partial and 90% CO₂ capture.

2.25 Linde-BASF Solvent Process

Linde and BASF are developing a solvent-based post-combustion capture technology offering
significant benefits compared to other processes as it aims to reduce overall energy consumption
and capital costs using solvents that are very stable under coal-fired power plant feed gas
conditions. The technology incorporates BASF’s novel amine-based process along with Linde’s
process and engineering innovations, as highlighted in Figure 41. The technology incorporates
use of an advanced aqueous amine-based solvent formulation marketed under the trade name
OASE® blue, which was developed by BASF. Under a DOE-supported project, Linde and
BASF demonstrated the solvent process at a 1.5-MWe scale at the NCCC.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Mechanical completion of the pilot plant was achieved in July 2014, and the final commissioning
activities were completed to enable start-up of operations in January 2015. Parametric tests were
performed from January to December 2015 to determine optimal test conditions and evaluate
process performance over a variety of operation parameters. A long-duration 1,500-hour
continuous test campaign was performed from May to July 2016 at a selected process condition
to evaluate process performance and solvent stability over an extended period to mimic
commercial operating conditions.

During the overall test period including startup, parametric testing, and long-duration testing, the
pilot plant operated for a total of 6,764 hours, out of which testing with flue gas was performed
for 4,109 hours. The testing demonstrated all the performance targets including CO₂ capture rate
exceeding 90%, CO₂ purity exceeding 99.9 mol% (dry), flue gas processing capacity up to
15,500 lb/hr (equivalent to 1.5 MWe), regeneration energy as low as 2.7 GJ/tonne CO₂, and
regenerator operating pressure up to 3.4 bar absolute. Testing validated the emission control
feature incorporated in BASF’s patented dry bed configuration, as well as several unique
equipment features incorporated in the pilot plant, including high-capacity structured packing,
gravity-driven absorber inter-stage cooler, blower location downstream of absorber, and the
unique reboiler configuration which minimizes solvent inventory and promotes fast response to
load changes.

Table 10 summarizes the process condition ranges for the pilot unit. The flue gas CO₂
concentration varied significantly, ranging from 11 to 12 mol% CO₂ during the morning and
peaking to as much as 13.5 mol% CO₂ in the afternoon on almost every day. This variation
resulted in substantial process fluctuations that were controlled to achieve as stable and

 80

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

consistent operation as possible while at the same time maintaining low specific regeneration
energy relative to industry standard MEA solvent-based CO₂ capture plants.

During the parametric testing, significant research was performed in characterizing aerosol
particle number concentration and size distribution in the flue gas and their impact on amine
carryover with the CO₂-depleted gas leaving the top of absorber using state-of-the-art analytical
measurements (see Section 2.2). Pilot plant operating parameters that minimize amine carryover
when the flue gas has a significant concentration of aerosol particles were also identified.

Figure 42 demonstrates the consistently low specific energy consumption, averaging
2.84 GJ/tonne CO₂ for the Linde-BASF technology for the duration of the long-duration test in
2016. The solvent was strengthened slightly at the end of July 2016 to test the impact on energy
performance, and the specific energy consumption reduced further to 2.75 GJ/tonne CO₂. The
Linde-BASF technology yielded significantly lower energy consumption than a standard MEA-
based capture process, which typically operates at 3.61 GJ/tonne CO₂.

 81

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

  Figure 42. Specific Energy Consumption for Linde-BASF Solvent Process during Long-Term Testing

Solvent stability can be measured in terms of both the energy consumption, used as an indicator
of solvent performance, and via analytical measurements of the solvent composition. The results
of both parametric and long-duration tests showed consistently low energy consumption. During
testing, bulk components of the solvent (amine, water, CO₂) were analyzed daily to correct the
plant inventory for the water balance as appropriate. In addition, batch samples of CO₂-lean and
CO₂-rich solvent liquid along with some of the process water and condensate streams were
collected on a regular basis and transported to BASF labs in Michigan for detailed analysis of
contaminants and heat-stable salts, which are an indicator of solvent degradation. The measured
heat-stable salt levels were well below the threshold that BASF uses for solvent degradation.

In the Linde-BASF process, essentially all the nitrogen and nearly all the oxygen leaves through
the top of the absorber in the CO₂-depleted gas, resulting in an extremely low concentration of
oxygen in the CO₂ product gas leaving the stripper column of less than 100 ppm under most
conditions. Figure 43 plots the oxygen concentration in the CO₂ product gas for a typical day
during the long-duration test campaign, which shows that the oxygen concentration can be
maintained within the limits required for applications in CO₂, utilization such as enhanced oil
recovery. The oxygen concentration averaged 13.3 ppm.

 Figure 43. Oxygen Concentration in Treated Flue Gas from Linde-BASF Solvent Process

 82

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Figure 44 and Figure 45 plot the steam requirements and corresponding CO₂ recovery rate during
the long-duration testing. Roughly 90% CO₂ recovery was maintained throughout the long-
duration test campaign while maintaining consistently low specific energy consumption over the
same period. The CO₂ recovery was maintained at 90% during the last week of testing in July
2016 when the solvent strength was slightly increased, indicating that the reduction in specific
energy consumption to 2.75 GJ/tonne CO₂ measured at that time could be consistently compared
to the rest of the data from the long-duration testing.

Following the NCCC test campaign, the Linde‑BASF technology advanced into DOE’s
large‑scale pilot program. An initial Phase I feasibility study was conducted for deployment at
the Abbott Power Plant; subsequent project phases progressed to a new host site at City Water,

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Light and Power’s Dallman facility, where a 10‑MWe pilot system is being designed,
constructed, and operated to further validate the technology at scale.

2.26 GTI Energy Hollow-Fiber Membrane Contactor

GTI Energy, in collaboration with Air Liquide Advanced Separations, evaluated a HFMC system
for post-combustion CO₂ capture at the NCCC. Testing was conducted over multiple operating
periods from 2017 through 2022, for a total of more than 3,000 hours. The technology integrates
membrane separation and solvent absorption into a hybrid process, aiming to achieve high
capture performance with reduced footprint and cost relative to conventional amine systems. In
the HFMC process, flue gas flows through the lumen side of super‑hydrophobic, porous hollow
fibers, while a CO₂-selective solvent circulates on the shell side. CO₂ permeates across the
membrane and is absorbed into the solvent, and the rich solvent is subsequently regenerated in a
separate membrane contactor operating in desorption mode.

The key enabling component is the poly(ether ether ketone) hollow-fiber membrane designed to
provide superior thermal and chemical resistance compared to conventional membrane polymers
and enables stable operation in harsh flue gas environments. The fibers are rendered
super‑hydrophobic, mitigating liquid intrusion (pore wetting), a major challenge in earlier
membrane contactor systems.

A principal advantage of HFMC systems is their very high surface-area-to-volume ratio, which
increases mass transfer rates by an order of magnitude relative to traditional packed or tray
columns. This leads to compact modular systems with reduced footprint, making the technology
particularly attractive for retrofit applications at existing power plants.

The GTI HFMC system was scaled from lab development to a 0.5-MWe-equivalent pilot plant,
for testing at the NCCC with coal-derived flue gas. Key features of the pilot system included:

 • 28 commercial scale membrane modules, each ~8 inches in diameter and 5 feet long
 • 30–40 m² membrane area per module
 • Structured, helically wound fiber packing for optimized flow distribution
 • Two-stage solvent regeneration with flash vessels and reboilers
 • Slipstream integration with NCCC flue gas infrastructure
By modifying and optimizing membrane preparation procedures and module construction
conditions, the membrane intrinsic CO2 permeance, essential in attaining high CO₂ capture rates
in membrane contactor mode, were as high as 2,000-2,670 GPU (1 GPU = 10-6 cm3
(STP)/(cm2*s*cmHg)), as shown in Table 11. The table also lists the effective area and specific
area of the bundles for each of the modules fabricated.

 84

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Table 11. Characteristics of GTI Hollow-Fiber Membranes

The testing program included system installation and commissioning, parametric testing, long-
term continuous operation targeting at least two months, and data collection for scale-up and
techno-economic analysis.

Initial Performance
Early testing of the HFMC system demonstrated strong CO₂ capture performance:

 • ~90% CO₂ removal achieved with coal-fired flue gas
 • >98 vol% CO₂ product purity from the regeneration section
 • High intrinsic membrane permeance (~2,150 GPU for CO₂)
Performance was initially validated with a smaller subset of modules (e.g., four-module
configuration), confirming the fundamental viability of the HFMC approach at pilot scale. The
system used aqueous activated methyl diethanolamine (aMDEA) as the solvent, which enabled
efficient chemical absorption of CO₂ without requiring high pressure ratios typical of
conventional membrane systems.

Parametric Testing
Extensive parametric testing was conducted to evaluate the sensitivity of system performance to
key operating variables, including flue gas temperature, solvent temperature, gas pressure, and
solvent flow rate and velocity. These tests were used to establish optimal operating conditions
for extended continuous testing and to guide scale-up design considerations. An important
operational issue identified during early testing was liquid-side concentration polarization, where
CO₂ accumulated near the membrane surface. This was mitigated by reducing solvent
concentration (from 50 wt% to 35 wt% aMDEA), improving mass transfer efficiency.

Long-Term Testing
While initial results were promising, long-term continuous testing with the full 28-module
system revealed performance degradation over time. The most significant issue was liquid
leakage across the module tubesheet, attributed to imperfect bonding between fibers and epoxy,
leakage at patch points, and potential non-uniform module fabrication. These leaks
compromised gas–liquid separation and reduced capture efficiency.

Additionally, scale-up discrepancies arose, as the performance observed at the module scale did
not fully translate to the multi-module system. The CO₂ capture rate declined during extended
runs, and variability between modules suggested manufacturing inconsistencies. Even after

 85

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

corrective fabrication improvements and retesting of new modules, performance degradation
trends persisted:

 • Declines in CO₂ removal rate and flux were similar to previous tests
 • No clear evidence of membrane wetting or loss of hydrophobicity
 • Indication of unresolved leakage or additional unidentified mechanisms
Implications for Further Development
The NCCC pilot testing demonstrated that HFMC technology has strong theoretical and short-
term performance advantages, including:

 • High CO₂ capture efficiency and purity
 • Compact modular design
 • Potential for lower capital cost due to smaller footprint
However, the testing also highlighted critical scale-up and reliability challenges, particularly in
module fabrication and long-term durability. The results suggest that further development is
needed in:

 • Manufacturing consistency and quality control
 • Tubesheet and sealing technologies
 • Long-term operational stability under realistic flue gas conditions
As a result, the project concluded that additional bench-scale and component-level development
is required before advancing to larger demonstration-scale systems.

2.27 TDA Research Sorbent

TDA Research, Inc. evaluated a solid sorbent CO₂ capture technology at the NCCC using a
0.5 MWe slipstream pilot unit incorporating an alkalized alumina adsorbent and a cyclic, multi-
bed adsorption/regeneration process. The system integrates a low-cost, alkali-promoted alumina
material with a simulated moving bed configuration implemented via multiple fixed beds,
enabling continuous counter-current gas-solid contact under near-isothermal conditions. The test
skids were installed in late 2017, with major testing campaigns conducted between 2017 and
2019, followed by additional testing and reconfiguration activities through 2021. Over these
campaigns, the system accumulated several thousand hours of operation, including both
parametric and steady-state testing under coal-derived flue gas conditions.

The project was intended to demonstrate that this sorbent-based approach could achieve high
CO₂ capture efficiency and product purity while maintaining a capture cost competitive with or
lower than conventional solvent-based systems. Pilot-scale operation was also used to generate
the performance and operability data required to assess scale-up to larger systems.

The TDA process is based on a solid alkalized alumina adsorbent that captures CO₂ through
reversible surface interactions. The sorbent is designed to operate effectively at elevated

 86

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

temperatures in the range of approximately 265 to 300°F, which allows it to treat flue gas with
minimal cooling and reduces the overall thermal penalty of the capture process. The heat of
adsorption is relatively low, typically reported between approximately 3 and 10 kcal/mol
depending on CO₂ concentration, which contributes to lower regeneration energy requirements
compared to conventional solvent systems. Regeneration of the sorbent is achieved using low-
pressure steam, producing a CO₂-rich product stream. Because the process operates near-
isothermal conditions, there is reduced need for large temperature swings or complex heat
integration. This approach is intended to simplify system design and improve thermal efficiency
relative to temperature swing or solvent-based processes.

A key feature of the system is the use of a simulated moving bed configuration realized through
multiple fixed-bed reactors. In this configuration, individual beds cycle between adsorption and
regeneration steps, while gas flows are arranged to approximate counter-current contacting. Flue
gas is passed in parallel through the adsorption section, while steam and recycled gas are routed
through the regeneration section. This design avoids the need for physically moving sorbent,
which reduces mechanical complexity and eliminates the parasitic power requirements
associated with solids handling. At the same time, it maintains the advantages of counter-current
operation, including improved sorbent utilization and higher effective CO₂ loading.

Test Results
The pilot unit was operated using coal-fired flue gas at NCCC, with additional testing conducted
using diluted streams to simulate NGCC conditions. The test program included both short-
duration parametric testing and longer steady-state campaigns to evaluate performance,
operability, and stability over time. Operating conditions such as temperature, flow rate, and
cycle timing were varied to assess their impact on capture efficiency and process performance.

Pilot testing demonstrated that the system was capable of achieving CO₂ capture efficiencies of
approximately 90 percent and producing a CO₂ product stream with purity near 95 percent,
meeting the primary performance targets established for the project. These results were obtained
under both coal-derived flue gas conditions and simulated natural gas flue gas.

In addition to overall capture performance, the testing program provided insight into process
behavior and the effectiveness of specific design features. The use of purge steps and steam
management strategies, such as steam-saver configurations, was shown to improve system
efficiency and reduce energy consumption. Counter-current gas-solid contact within the multi-
bed system contributed to higher sorbent loading and more efficient utilization of the adsorbent
inventory.

Techno-economic analysis based on pilot-scale data indicated that the process could achieve a
CO₂ capture cost on the order of $34.9 per tonne for a large supercritical coal-fired power plant.
This estimate falls below the DOE target of $40 per tonne and represents a meaningful
improvement relative to baseline amine solvent systems. The combination of relatively low
sorbent cost, reduced regeneration energy requirements, and simplified process design are the
primary factors contributing to the projected cost advantage.

While the pilot system achieved its core performance targets, testing also identified several
operational challenges, particularly related to sorbent performance and durability. Early in the

 87

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

test campaign, the initially supplied sorbent exhibited limited long-term stability due to
manufacturing issues. Characterization indicated that the material contained unreacted
components, which contributed to performance degradation during operation.

To address this issue, the project team removed the original sorbent and implemented a
reprocessing approach to improve material quality. The reactors were subsequently reloaded,
and additional procedures were developed to condition the sorbent prior to operation. In
particular, a controlled hydration process was introduced in which humidified air was gradually
applied to the sorbent to manage temperature increases associated with exothermic reactions
during initial conditioning. This step was necessary to prevent localized overheating and
maintain material integrity.

Despite these improvements, further testing revealed that the sorbent remained sensitive to
operating conditions, particularly with respect to moisture exposure. Following an extended
shutdown period of several months, the sorbent was found to have undergone physical or
chemical changes that significantly reduced its CO₂ capture capacity. This observation
highlighted a key challenge for the technology, namely the need to ensure long-term sorbent
stability under realistic plant operating and standby conditions.

In response, additional sorbent materials were evaluated, including modified commercial
products, to allow continued testing within project constraints. Although these alternative
materials did not match the performance of the optimized TDA sorbent developed at lab scale,
they enabled continued operation of the pilot system and provided additional insight into process
behavior and operational flexibility.

The testing underscores the importance of sorbent robustness as a critical factor in technology
development. While the underlying process concept and reactor design were successfully
demonstrated, the sensitivity of the sorbent to manufacturing quality and environmental exposure
represents a key risk for scale-up. Addressing these issues will require further development of
sorbent synthesis methods, improved material formulations, and a better understanding of
degradation mechanisms under cyclic operation.

The results also highlight the benefits of the multi-bed approach. By eliminating the need for
moving solids while maintaining effective counter-current contact, the process provides a
practical pathway to scaling adsorption-based capture systems. The near-isothermal operation
further simplifies system design and reduces integration challenges with existing power plant
infrastructure.

Testing of TDA Research’s alkalized alumina sorbent system at the NCCC demonstrated a
viable solid sorbent-based CO₂ capture technology with the ability to achieve high capture
efficiency and product purity at pilot scale. The system’s combination of a low-cost sorbent, low
regeneration energy, and simplified reactor configuration contributes to favorable projected
capture costs relative to conventional approaches.

 88

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

2.28 Air Liquide Hollow-Fiber Cold Membrane

Air Liquide’s hollow-fiber cold membrane project represents a multi-phase effort to develop and
validate a hybrid process for post-combustion CO₂ capture from coal-fired power plant flue gas.
The work culminated in a 0.3-MWe field test campaign at the NCCC totaling about 5,800 test
hours, with the primary objective of demonstrating the performance, durability, and scalability of
membrane-based CO₂ capture under realistic operating conditions. The technology combines
membrane separation with downstream CO₂ purification concepts, with particular emphasis on
improving capture efficiency and reducing overall cost.

At the core of the technology is a hybrid cold membrane process, represented in Figure 46, that
integrates sub-ambient membrane operation with a downstream CO₂ liquefaction and
purification step. In the envisioned full-scale process, treated and compressed flue gas is fed to
hollow-fiber membrane modules operating at temperatures around –30°C. Operation at sub-
ambient conditions increases CO₂/N₂ selectivity, allowing the membrane stage to pre-concentrate
CO₂ in the permeate stream. This enriched stream is then routed to a liquefaction and
purification system to achieve pipeline-quality CO₂. While the liquefaction portion is an
essential element of the overall process design, it was not a focus of the test campaign at the
NCCC, as this portion of the process relies on established cryogenic technology with a well-
understood performance basis. Consequently, the field testing concentrated on validating the
membrane system—the novel and less mature component of the hybrid process—under real flue
gas conditions.

 Figure 46. Schematic of Air Liquide CO₂ Capture Process

The project built on earlier efforts using commercial polyimide (PI‑1) hollow-fiber membranes
and introduced a next-generation material (PI‑2) designed to improve permeance and
productivity. The PI‑2 material represents a significant advancement in membrane performance,
enabling higher CO₂ flux at comparable selectivity. This increase in permeance reduces the
required membrane area for a given duty and therefore contributes directly to lowering capital
costs for CO₂ capture systems. Development of PI‑2 included progression from lab-scale
permeators to increasingly larger module sizes intended to represent commercial configurations.

 89

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Field testing at the 0.3 MWe scale utilized a dedicated test unit designed to process a slipstream
of actual coal-derived flue gas. The testing program included evaluation of both legacy and
next-generation membrane bundles, with a focus on scaling from smaller modules to 4-inch and
ultimately 6-inch diameter bundles. These progressively larger bundles were designed to
validate manufacturability, module performance, and the ability to meet throughput and capture
targets at increasingly relevant scales.

Performance targets for the larger membrane bundles included achieving approximately 90%
CO₂ recovery with permeate CO₂ concentrations on the order of 60%, along with substantial
increases in feed flow capacity. Field test results demonstrated that these targets were achieved
or exceeded. Commercial PI‑1 bundles showed stable and consistent performance during
extended operation, including long-duration steady-state testing exceeding several hundred
hours. These results confirmed that membrane systems can operate reliably in the presence of
real flue gas, including potential contaminants, without significant degradation over the duration
of testing.

Evaluation of the PI‑2 material at larger scales further demonstrated the benefits of the improved
membrane properties. When fabricated into 6-inch bundles, the PI‑2 modules achieved feed
flow rates exceeding the project targets while maintaining high CO₂ recovery and permeate
purity. These results represent a successful scale-up of the next-generation membrane from
laboratory development to field-tested modules and validate the manufacturability of larger-
diameter bundles representative of commercial systems.

The testing program also included extensive parametric studies to evaluate the influence of key
operating variables such as temperature, pressure, and feed composition. These tests were
designed to characterize system behavior across a range of operating conditions and to support
process modeling and optimization. Particular attention was given to understanding the benefits
of sub-ambient operation, which enhances CO₂ selectivity and overall system performance. The
results confirmed that reduced temperature operation is a critical factor in achieving high
separation efficiency in the membrane stage.

Although the project’s field testing scope was centered on the membrane system, the overall
process concept includes significant integration between the membrane and downstream
processing steps. In the full process configuration, the thermal and pressure characteristics of the
membrane stage are leveraged to reduce the energy requirements of the liquefaction system,
while the cryogenic section provides cooling that supports sub-ambient membrane operation.
This level of integration is central to achieving competitive energy efficiency, even though the
liquefaction equipment itself was not deployed as part of the field test campaign.

A techno-economic analysis conducted as part of the project assessed the impact of the hybrid
process configuration and improved membrane performance on overall CO₂ capture cost.
Results indicate that the hybrid cold membrane approach has the potential to meet cost targets
consistent with broader program goals. The improved productivity of the PI‑2 material
contributes to lower system capital costs by reducing the number of required membrane modules
and associated equipment, while the hybrid process configuration supports reductions in energy
consumption relative to more conventional approaches.

 90

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Overall, the Air Liquide hollow-fiber cold membrane project demonstrates a clear progression
from material development to field validation at a relevant scale. Key outcomes include
successful demonstration of sub-ambient membrane operation, validation of advanced membrane
materials through scale-up to commercial-representative modules, and confirmation of stable
performance under real flue gas conditions. By focusing testing on the membrane component
while leveraging established understanding of downstream liquefaction, the project efficiently
advanced the critical technical elements needed to support future commercialization of the
hybrid process.

2.29 Carbon America FrostCC™ Cryogenic Process

Carbon America conducted engineering-scale pilot testing of its FrostCC cryogenic carbon
capture technology at the NCCC in 2024. Over the course of the campaign, the system
accumulated more than 1,000 hours of carbon capture operation, including sustained continuous
runs that demonstrated stable, integrated system performance.

The FrostCC technology is a cryogenic process that separates CO₂ from flue gas by cooling the
gas below the CO₂ sublimation temperature, causing it to solidify (“frost”) onto heat exchanger
surfaces. The process uses a highly integrated thermodynamic cycle in which the flue gas itself
acts as the refrigerant through compression, cooling, and expansion steps, eliminating the need
for external refrigerants, water, or chemical solvents. Solid CO₂ is periodically removed from
the heat exchanger surfaces, melted, and recovered as a high-purity liquid product. This
fundamental process distinguishes FrostCC™ from solvent- or membrane-based systems by
relying entirely on phase change and physical separation mechanisms. Figure 47 provides a
diagram of the process.

 Figure 47. Carbon America FrostCC Process Diagram

 91

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The test campaign at NCCC marked the first fully integrated, continuous pilot-scale
demonstration of this cryogenic “frosting” concept using real flue gas in an industrially relevant
setting. Prior work had been conducted at lab scale under controlled conditions, but the NCCC
campaign enabled evaluation of system performance under variable flue gas compositions and
realistic operating constraints. The project was structured in phases, with initial testing focused
on validating individual subsystems—including compression, dehydration, expansion, and cold
recuperation—followed by integrated operation with the frosting heat exchangers and liquid CO₂
handling system. This phased approach supported troubleshooting, control system development,
and gradual expansion to full system capability.

One of the primary objectives of the field test was to demonstrate reliable and continuous
operation of the integrated system. The pilot successfully achieved this objective, operating for
extended periods without significant degradation of key equipment or system performance.
Stable, continuous capture runs provided critical validation of the underlying thermodynamic
cycle, the robustness of turbomachinery components, and the reliability of heat exchanger
operation under cyclic frosting and defrosting conditions. The ability to maintain consistent
performance across long-duration runs represents a key milestone in demonstrating technical
viability at scale.

Performance results from the pilot testing were strongly positive and demonstrated the
effectiveness of the FrostCC process. The system achieved CO₂ capture efficiencies of up to
approximately 99%, with captured CO₂ product purity reaching approximately 99.97%. These
results exceed typical performance targets for post-combustion capture systems and confirm the
ability of the cryogenic process to produce a near pipeline-quality CO₂ stream without extensive
downstream purification. In addition to CO₂ capture, the system simultaneously removed co-
pollutants from the flue gas stream, reducing nitrogen oxides (NOₓ) to below 0.5 ppm and SO₂ to
below 2 ppm. This co-removal capability highlights a key advantage of the process, as it
eliminates the need for separate emissions control systems for these pollutants.

The test campaign also demonstrated significant capture productivity, with the system achieving
capture rates exceeding 1,000 metric tonnes of CO₂ per year on an equivalent basis. These
results are particularly notable given the relatively small scale of the pilot unit, suggesting strong
potential for scaling the technology to commercial capacities. The modular nature of the system
design—using standard compressors, expanders, and heat exchangers—supports this scalability
and provides a pathway for cost reduction through replication and mass manufacturing.

Another critical aspect of the test campaign was validation of the physics-based models used to
design and optimize the FrostCC system. The pilot system was heavily instrumented, enabling
detailed comparison between measured performance and model predictions. Results showed
close agreement between experimental data and model outputs, providing confidence in the
accuracy of the simulation tools used for scaling and system design. This model validation is
essential for projecting performance at larger scales and reducing uncertainty in future
commercial deployments.

Parametric testing during the campaign explored the operational envelope of the system and
evaluated performance across a range of conditions, including variations in flue gas flow rate,
composition, and operating temperatures. These tests helped define operating limits, identify

 92

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

optimal conditions, and improve control strategies for stable operation. In particular, the
development of robust control methods for managing the cyclic frosting and melting process was
a key outcome, enabling reliable continuous operation and improved system efficiency.

The project also provided important insights into system integration and thermal management.
Scaling analysis indicated that larger systems are expected to benefit from reduced relative heat
losses, improved turbomachinery efficiency, and enhanced opportunities for energy recovery.
Design improvements identified during testing include more effective utilization of cold energy
from the liquid CO₂ product, increased efficiency of compression and expansion stages, and
improved integration of system components. These factors contribute to further reductions in
energy consumption and overall capture cost at commercial scale.

2.30 SRI Mixed-Salt Solvent Process

SRI is developing a novel mixed-salt solvent-based technology that combines potassium and
ammonium salts as the solvent with unique process configurations without chilling the solvent to
efficiently capture CO₂ from flue gas. The solvent mixture is non-precipitating, non-degradable,
inexpensive, readily available, and can be regenerated at elevated pressure. It minimizes the
volatility of the pure ammonia-based solvent and therefore chilling requirements, and it improves
the kinetics of the slow potassium carbonate reaction with ammonium salts as the promoter. The
MSP technology is supported by the U.S. DOE and has been developed in partnership with
organizations including Baker Hughes, which holds an exclusive global license for commercial
deployment.

In this project, SRI delivered modular skids to the NCCC’s pilot bay area to demonstrate capture
of 5 to 10 tonnes of CO₂ per day. Figure 48 shows the skids, which included four modules.
Field testing activities began in 2025, following system installation and commissioning. The
campaign included long‑duration operation, performance validation, solvent management
studies, and data collection for techno‑economic evaluation.

 Figure 48. Installed SRI Modules

 93

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The test campaign concluded in the second quarter of 2026, and formal results and reporting
have not yet been released. The data generated are expected to support scale‑up, performance
validation, and commercialization readiness.

Baker Hughes is a key industry partner in the MSP program, providing cost‑share support and
holding commercialization rights for the technology. The company’s involvement includes
technology development, scale‑up strategy, and commercialization planning. In addition, Baker
Hughes conducted a multi-week emissions testing campaign during operation of the MSP.
While detailed public reporting of emissions testing results is not yet available, the MSP program
explicitly targets low emissions—particularly reduced ammonia slip—through process design
and validation testing, which aligns with Baker Hughes’ role in ensuring commercial‑grade
environmental performance.

2.31 KC8 Capture Technologies UNO MK 3 Solvent Process

KC8 Capture Technologies is developing the UNO MK 3 solvent process, which uses a novel
catalytically enhanced precipitating solvent technology for NGCC application. Under Phase I of
this ARPA-E FLECCS-funded program, KC8 Capture Technologies proved the potential for the
process to be retrofitted to gas turbines to produce low emissions, low-cost power in a highly
variable renewable penetration grid and was awarded to proceed to field demonstration at the
NCCC in Phase II.

The UNO MK3 solvent features:

 • Phase-change (precipitating) behavior—Unlike traditional aqueous amine solvents that
 remain fully liquid, the UNO MK3 solvent is engineered to form a solid phase when it
 reacts with CO₂. This phase separation enables higher CO₂ loading capacity, reduced
 energy requirements during solvent regeneration, and more efficient separation of CO₂
 from the solvent.
 • Lower regeneration energy—A major cost driver in carbon capture is the heat required to
 release CO₂ from the solvent. The UNO MK3 system is designed to reduce regeneration
 energy by leveraging its phase-change chemistry and potentially lower steam demand
 compared to MEA systems.
 • Improved thermal and oxidative stability—The solvent formulation is expected to exhibit
 enhanced resistance to thermal degradation and reduced oxidative breakdown when
 exposed to flue gas contaminants. This can translate into longer solvent life and lower
 operating costs.
 • Reduced equipment size and cost—Because of its higher CO₂ carrying capacity and
 improved thermodynamics, absorber and stripper equipment may be smaller and the
 overall plant footprint and capital expenses could be reduced
KC8 Capture Technologies is testing the process at a scale of 5 to 10 tonnes of CO₂ per day.
The test campaign began in 2025 and is scheduled to continue through the end of 2026. The
primary objectives of the ongoing test program include:

 • Demonstrating stable long-term operation

 94

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 • Measuring CO₂ capture efficiency and energy consumption
 • Evaluating solvent degradation and make-up rates
 • Assessing operational challenges such as fouling, precipitation handling, and solids
 management
 • Generating data to support scale-up and commercialization
Because the program has not yet concluded, comprehensive performance results—including
capture efficiency, solvent stability, degradation rates, and cost metrics—are not yet publicly
available. Final conclusions regarding the system’s performance, scalability, and economic
viability will be determined after completion and full data analysis.

2.32 Trimeric/UT-Austin NO₂ Scrubbing

Trimeric and UT-Austin performed testing of a chemical process for removal of NO₂ upstream
of amine-based solvent systems to minimize solvent losses. Trimeric’s application addresses the
specific technical problem of how to prevent nitrosamine accumulation in the solvent system and
minimize solvent oxidation. The presence of nitrosamines increases the cost for capturing CO₂
and is an environmental concern. The process involved the use of a low-cost additive,
thiosulfate, in an existing SO₂ pre-scrubber upstream of NCCC’s Pilot Bay 3.

Because the flue gas used for testing is treated for NO₂ in an upstream selective catalytic reducer,
an NO₂ additive system was installed to achieve a typical concentration for untreated flue gas.
Figure 49 provides preliminary results, showing that NO₂ removal was strongly correlated to
sulfite concentration in the pre-scrubber solution. The sulfite concentration was increased by
adding thiosulfate. As sulfite concentration decreased during the test due to dilution from flue
gas moisture condensation, the NO₂ removal rate decreased accordingly.

Figure 49. NO2 Removal and Sulfite Concentration in Pre-Scrubber during Trimeric/UT-Austin Testing

 95

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Trimeric and UT-Austin personnel reviewed the data and presented findings at the 2017 NETL
CO₂ Capture Technology Review Meeting in Pittsburgh, PA.

2.33 SRI International Sorbent

In 2014, SRI International conducted bench-scale testing of an advanced carbon sorbent (ACS)
CO₂ capture process at the NCCC. The primary goal of the program was to demonstrate post-
combustion CO₂ capture from coal-fired flue gas using a novel solid sorbent system. Unlike
conventional amine-based solvent systems, the SRI process employs circulating carbon
microbeads that adsorb and release CO₂ in a continuous loop.

The specific objectives of the NCCC test campaign were to:

 • Evaluate CO₂ capture efficiency and product purity under realistic flue gas conditions
 • Characterize thermal performance and steam demand
 • Identify operational challenges at larger scale
 • Generate data for the design of a future pilot-scale (≈0.5–1 MWe) system
The NCCC testing utilized a 40‑kWe equivalent bench-scale system designed to simulate
slipstream operation on coal-derived flue gas. Key design parameters included:

    •   Flue gas flow: 1,000–2,000 L/min
 • Sorbent circulation: ~12 kg/min
 • Adsorber temperature: 20–30°C (design)
 • Stripper temperature: 100–120°C
 • CO₂ concentration: 4.5–12% in inlet gas
The process integrates adsorption and desorption in a single vertical column, consisting of an
adsorber, transition section, stripper, dehydrator, cooler, and pneumatic lift for sorbent
recirculation. The sorbent consists of high-surface-area carbon microbeads (100–300 μm) with
rapid adsorption/desorption kinetics and low heat of adsorption, enabling potentially lower
regeneration energy than solvent-based systems.

Initial operation was conducted in late 2013, with the primary testing occurring from January to
July 2014 for a total of about 250 hours of operation. The campaign consisted of continuous and
semi-continuous operation periods as well as parametric testing across multiple flow and
temperature conditions.

While the targeted CO₂ capture efficiency was greater than 90%, the observed efficiency was
around 67%. The lower-than-target capture efficiency was attributed primarily to:

 • Higher-than-expected adsorber temperatures (50–60°C vs. 20–30°C design) causing a
 reduction in the adsorption capacity of the sorbent
 • Reduced gas residence time in the adsorber

 96

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The CO₂ purity at the stripper outlet was around 93%, slightly lower than earlier lab-scale
results. The reduction was linked to the geometric design of the transition section and gas
mixing and flow distribution limitations.

Steam was used primarily for sorbent regeneration (heating) rather than as a stripping gas.
Steam demand was higher than expected during testing, mainly due to heat losses in the system.
However, analysis indicated that with improved insulation and design, heat requirements could
approach ~50 kJ/mol CO₂, consistent with design expectations.

The system experienced reduced heat transfer efficiency caused by wide spacing in heat
exchanger tubes and suboptimal cooling of recirculating sorbent. This contributed directly to the
elevated adsorber temperatures and degraded capture performance.

A significant outcome of the NCCC testing was identification of scale-up and operability issues,
including back-pressure effects causing oscillating solid flow and condensation in the stripper,
leading to plugging and flow interruptions. To mitigate these effects, restrictive filters were
replaced with impact separators, the dehydrator airflow was increased to remove moisture, and
the steam injection rates were adjusted.

Other issues identified included:

 • Large pressure differences between flue gas supply and return lines that complicated
 operation
 • Manual throttling required due to inadequate control valves
 • Some sorbent breakage occurring due to high-velocity impacts in pneumatic transport
The process improvements identified included:

 • Better thermal management and heat exchanger design
 • Improved gas and solids flow control systems
 • Optimization of adsorber height and residence time
 • Enhanced transition section geometry for purity
Despite lower-than-expected performance, the testing demonstrated:

 • Feasibility of continuous solid sorbent CO₂ capture
 • Viability of low-energy adsorption-based processes
 • Key engineering pathways to achieve DOE targets (<$40/ton CO₂)

2.34 DOE Sorbents

DOE designed the C2U to test CO₂ capture with sorbents composed of amines on a solid
substrate. The unit, which utilizes fluidized beds for both absorption and regeneration, was
designed and constructed at NETL. NETL operated the unit for about three years in both

 97

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

circulating and batch modes under a variety of conditions, using two sorbents composed of
polyethylenimine on a silica substrate. Testing of the C2U was conducted at the NCCC in 2014.
The focus of these tests was to evaluate the accumulation of trace metals such as selenium on the
sorbent. Testing was conducted in circulating and batch modes of operation. Figure 50 shows
the installed unit.

 Figure 50. Assembly of the DOE Sorbent Unit

The C2U operated for about 44 hours in circulating mode during the first quarter of 2014. As
testing progressed, the CO₂ capture efficiency declined, which was attributed to incomplete
sorbent regeneration. Operation resumed in batch mode without regeneration from June through
August, with over 346 hours of exposure under simulated natural gas conditions and 282 hours
under typical coal-fired conditions. Thermo-gravimetric analysis of sorbent samples taken
following the circulation tests and the batch tests showed no permanent loss of CO₂ capture
capacity.

Analysis of the samples taken before and after testing showed that the trace element
concentrations were significantly lower than the hazardous waste standards. Due to the
possibility of trace elements being removed from the flue gas upstream of the sorbent, chemical
analysis of the inlet flue gas stream is recommended to better gauge trace element accumulation.

2.35 Carbon Capture Scientific Gas Pressurized Stripping Solvent Process

Carbon Capture Scientific, LLC, in collaboration with CONSOL Energy Inc., Nexant Inc., and
Western Kentucky University, has been developing a novel GPS process for post-combustion
CO₂ capture. A flow diagram of the bench-scale GPS test unit is shown in Figure 51. The GPS
process is an innovative technology in which CO₂ is removed from the flue gas in the absorber
and released at elevated pressure with high purity in the stripper. Parametric testing was

 98

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

performed during run PO-2, and final, long-term testing was completed in August 2015 in run
PO-3. The total flue gas operating time was 1,145 hours.

 Figure 51. Flow Diagram of Carbon Capture Scientific Bench-Scale GPS Unit

An amine-based Carbon Capture Scientific proprietary blended solvent was used in all the
parametric and long-term tests. Parametric tests investigated the influences of total flue gas
flow rate, gas-to-liquid (G/L) volume ratio, stripper operating pressure, and nitrogen flow rate
on the GPS system performance. The performance was characterized by the CO₂ removal rate in
the absorber and CO₂ product pressure and purity from the stripper. The CO₂ removal rate was
calculated based on the average CO₂ concentration at the absorber inlet and outlet, and the CO₂
purity was based on the average CO₂ concentration at the stripper outlet.

Figure 52 illustrates the influence of the total flue gas flow rate on the GPS unit performance. In
these tests, the G/L volume ratio was kept at 145, and the stripper operating pressure was at 6.1
bar. The nitrogen flow rate into the stripper was adjusted proportionally with the flue gas flow
rate. With the increase of total flue gas flow rate, the CO₂ removal rate in the absorber reduced
markedly, while the CO₂ purity from the stripper reduced slightly. As the flue gas and solvent
flow rates increased, the residence time in the absorber was reduced. Less reaction time resulted
in lower reaction conversions, and thus lower CO₂ removal rates. The height of stripper packing
was more over-designed than that of absorber. Additionally, the stripping reaction was faster
than the absorption reaction due to its higher temperature. Thus, the reduced reaction time had
less influence on the conversion in the stripper than that in the absorber, which explained why
the reduction of CO₂ purity was less significant.

 99

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 CO2 Purity and Removal Rate, %

 Flue Gas Flow Rate, slpm

 Figure 52. Influence of Flue Gas Flow Rate on the Performance of GPS Process

Figure 53 shows the effect of the G/L volume ratio on process performance. The stripper
operating pressure (6 bar), the solvent flow rate (0.89 gpm), and the nitrogen flow rate into the
stripper (2.35 slpm) were kept constant. The G/L volume ratio was changed by changing the flue
gas flow rate and keeping the solvent flow rate constant. With an increased G/L volume ratio,
the CO₂ removal rate reduced significantly, but CO₂ purity was not affected. As the G/L volume
ratio increased, the amount of flue gas processed per mass of solvent increased. In this case, to
achieve the same the CO₂ removal rate as the low G/L volume ratio, the height of absorber
packing would need to be increased. Because the operating conditions in the stripper were kept
constant, as G/L volume ratio increased, there was little variation in CO₂ purity from the stripper.
 CO2 Purity and Removal Rate, %

 G/L Ratio
 Figure 53. Influence of G/L Ratio on Performance of the GPS Process

Figure 54 demonstrates the influence of the stripper operating pressure on the GPS process
performance. A flue gas flow rate of 455 slpm (16 scfm) and a solvent flow rate of 0.95 gpm
were maintained. During the experiments, the nitrogen flow rate was increased accordingly to

 100

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

ensure the lean loading was still low enough to achieve over 90% removal rate in the absorber.
With the increase of stripper operating pressure, both the CO₂ purity from the stripper and CO₂
removal rate in the absorber declined. Because the CO₂ equilibrium partial pressure was a strong
function of temperature and the stripper temperature was kept the same for the four tests, CO₂
purity at the top of the stripper decreased when the total pressure increased. As the stripper
operating pressure increased, the lean loading of the solvent at the outlet of the stripper increased
to maintain a higher equilibrium partial pressure of CO₂. Higher lean loading solvent
compromised the performance in the absorber, resulting in lower CO₂ removal rates.
 CO2 Purity and Removal Rate, %

 Stripper Pressure, bar
 Figure 54. Influence of Stripper Operating Pressure on GPS Performance

The effect of nitrogen flow rate on GPS performance is shown in Figure 55. In these tests, the
flue gas flow rate was maintained at 500 slpm (18 scfm), the stripper operating pressure was kept
at 5.9 bar, and the solvent flow rate was maintained at 1.09 gpm. With the increase of nitrogen
flow rate, the CO₂ purity reduced, while the CO₂ removal rate increased. As the nitrogen flow
rate increased, the lean loading of the solvent at the outlet of stripper decreased due to the lower
CO₂ concentration at the bottom of the stripper. The solvent with lower lean loading enhanced
the performance in the absorber subsequently. The nitrogen dilution reduced the CO₂ purity
from the stripper.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 55. Influence of Nitrogen Flow Rate on GPS Performance

Energy consumption tests were conducted to validate Carbon Capture Scientific’s previous
simulation results. Since the sensible heat amount strongly depends on the selection of a heat
exchanger, the tests were designed only to measure the reaction heat and the stripping heat. The
sensible heat of the solvent can be estimated based on the working capacity and specific heat of
the rich and lean solvents. The estimated sensible heat was in the range of 250 to 500 kJ/kg CO₂
captured. To measure the actual reaction and stripping heat, the steam usage data was obtained
after the unit reached steady-state operation. The net steam usage representing the sum of
reaction and stripping heat was the difference between the steam usage when the unit was
operated with flue gas flow and without flue gas flow. The steam usage for operation without
flue gas flow was the sum of sensible heat and heat loss. Table 12 provides the results for the
sum of reaction heat and stripping heat, which were very close to the predicted value of
1,570 kJ/kg CO₂ captured.

For the long-term test, the unit was set to run at a condition that could achieve 90% CO₂ removal
and 95% CO₂ purity as established in parametric testing. The incoming flue gas and the solvent
flow rates were 330 slpm and 0.56 gpm, respectively, producing a G/L ratio of 156. The stripper
pressure was maintained at 6 bar. The skid was operated constantly except for interruptions for
maintenance or troubleshooting. The long-term test was conducted from late May through early
August 2015. The operation was fairly stable, and CO₂ concentration data from a representative
24-hour period is shown in Figure 56.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026
