The tests of the bench-scale GPS skid at the NCCC achieved the project goals. The parametric
tests validated the influences of operating parameters on GPS process performance, which were
simulated before the tests. The long-term test showed the process could run continuously for an
extended period. The results demonstrated that GPS process can achieve 90% CO₂ removal from
typical coal-derived flue gas, with high-pressure, high-purity CO₂ product. The energy
consumption of the GPS process was much lower than that of the MEA baseline case, with the
total heat requirement for the GPS process at about 1,310 BTU/lb CO₂ captured. Carbon Capture
Scientific has identified ways to potentially optimize the GPS process to achieve further energy
cost reductions and may pursue further testing in the future.

2.36 Ohio State University Membranes (2015)

OSU conducted their first membrane test campaign at the NCCC in 2015. The primary purpose
of this testing effort was to validate the performance of spiral-wound membrane modules
fabricated from inorganic/polymer composite materials when exposed to real coal-derived flue
gas. Previous testing had been conducted at OSU using simulated gas mixtures, but the NCCC
campaign represented a critical step in confirming whether those results would translate to
realistic power plant conditions, including exposure to oxygen, sulfur oxides, nitrogen oxides,
and high humidity.

The membrane consisted of a thin selective polymer layer incorporating zeolite nanoparticles,
supported on a polymer substrate. This design sought to combine the high selectivity of
inorganic materials with the manufacturability and scalability of polymer membranes. The
membranes were fabricated into spiral-wound modules, a configuration commonly used in
industrial gas separation systems. During testing, these modules were installed in a controlled
gas permeation unit that allowed precise regulation of temperature, humidity, and gas
composition. Typical operating conditions included a temperature of approximately 135°F and
water vapor concentrations near saturation (~17%), which closely mimic actual flue gas
environments.

 103

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

A key feature of the test program was the use of actual flue gas supplied by NCCC. This gas
contained roughly 12% CO₂, about 7% oxygen, and the balance nitrogen, along with trace
amounts of sulfur dioxide and nitrogen oxides. The oxygen concentration was somewhat higher
than typical coal plant conditions, providing a conservative test of membrane durability. Testing
under these conditions allowed the research team to evaluate both the transport performance of
the membrane and its resistance to degradation mechanisms associated with impurities and real
operational variability.

The field campaign evaluated three different membrane modules, each tested for on the order of
100 to 200 hours. The first module, designated SW‑154, operated for 96 hours and showed
stable performance throughout the test period. The second module, SW‑162, ran for
approximately 208 hours, although this test included a 60-hour interruption due to a flue gas
shutdown. The third module, SW‑161, was tested for about 200 hours, also with a temporary
shutdown during the test. These results demonstrate that the 2015 campaign achieved
continuous operational periods on the order of roughly 200 hours per module, which is sufficient
for short-term stability assessment but not indicative of long-term commercial durability.

In terms of performance, the membranes exhibited strong CO₂ transport characteristics under
field conditions (see Table 13). Measured CO₂ permeance values were approximately 800 GPU,
while CO₂/N₂ selectivity ranged from about 150 to as high as 270 depending on the module and
test conditions. These results compared favorably with laboratory measurements conducted at
OSU, where similar permeance and selectivity values had been obtained using simulated flue
gas. The consistency between lab-scale and field-scale performance was a significant outcome,
confirming that the membrane design and fabrication processes could be successfully scaled.

 Table 13. Performance of Spiral-Wound Membrane Modules at 135oF

The behavior observed during testing provides additional important insight. In general, CO₂
permeance remained relatively stable over the duration of each test. However, selectivity was
more sensitive to operational events, particularly shutdowns and restarts. In the case of module
SW‑162, CO₂/N₂ selectivity dropped significantly after the flue gas supply was interrupted and
then resumed. Similar, though less severe, effects were observed in module SW‑161. These
changes were attributed primarily to mechanical and fabrication-related issues, such as
incomplete curing of adhesive materials used in module construction and physical deformation
of the membrane caused by spacer materials. These issues led to leakage pathways and feed gas
bypass, which reduced effective separation performance.

Importantly, the results indicate that the membrane material itself was relatively robust in the
presence of flue gas impurities. No severe chemical degradation attributable to oxygen or trace
contaminants was identified over the test durations. Instead, the dominant performance

 104

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

limitations were associated with module design and construction, rather than intrinsic membrane
properties. This distinction is critical, as it suggests that further engineering improvements—
such as better sealing methods, optimized spacer materials, and improved fabrication
procedures—could significantly enhance overall system performance without requiring major
changes to the membrane chemistry.

The test campaign also highlighted the importance of comparing field results with laboratory
testing. At OSU, modules were tested with simulated gas mixtures under controlled conditions
and demonstrated stable selectivity values around 200 for up to approximately 150–200 hours.
These results were largely reproduced in the field, aside from the mechanical issues discussed
previously. The agreement between lab and field data reinforces confidence in the experimental
methods and the scalability of the membrane technology.

2.37 Ohio State University Membranes (2018)

In partnership with American Electric Power, OSU completed a second test campaign at the
NCCC in 2018. This campaign represented a significant advancement over earlier field trials by
demonstrating extended-duration operation, improved membrane module design, and enhanced
CO₂ capture performance under realistic flue gas conditions. This campaign built on prior OSU
efforts by focusing on prototype spiral-wound membrane modules designed for higher
performance and durability, as well as on the validation of facilitated transport membrane
technology under industrially relevant conditions.

The central objective of the campaign was to evaluate the performance of newly developed
membrane modules when exposed to actual coal-derived flue gas, with particular emphasis on
assessing CO₂ permeance, CO₂/N₂ selectivity, stability over extended operation, and sensitivity
to flue gas impurities including oxygen, sulfur oxides, and nitrogen oxides. A key differentiator
of the 2018 campaign relative to earlier work was the inclusion of a long-duration (~500-hour)
stability test, enabling a more meaningful assessment of membrane durability and operational
reliability.

The membrane modules evaluated in this program were based on a facilitated transport
mechanism, in which CO₂ preferentially reacts with amine carriers embedded in a thin polymer
layer. This chemistry provides enhanced separation performance compared to conventional
solution-diffusion membranes. The membranes were fabricated into spiral-wound modules with
a significantly larger membrane area (approximately 14,000 cm² or 1.4 m² per module),
representing a scale-up from earlier laboratory-scale devices. The module design incorporated
improved sealing features, including face-compression O-rings and a reinforced housing
structure, which were intended to eliminate leakage and improve reproducibility of performance.

Testing was performed using coal-derived flue gas. The gas stream, after pre-treatment and
drying, contained approximately 12% CO₂, 7% O₂, and the balance nitrogen, along with trace
levels of SO₂ and NO₂. Before entering the membrane module, the gas was filtered to remove
particulates and then humidified to replicate realistic operating conditions. The membrane
system operated over a range of temperatures (approximately 57-67°C), feed pressures (1 to 4
atm), and permeate pressures (0.2–0.3 atm vacuum), enabling evaluation of performance across
different driving force conditions.

 105

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The testing campaign involved three spiral-wound modules. The primary module underwent an
extended stability test lasting approximately 500 hours, while two additional modules were
tested for shorter periods (24 and 30 hours, respectively) to confirm reproducibility. The long-
duration test demonstrated stable membrane performance despite several operational
disturbances, including reduced flue gas flow rates and two separate flue gas shutdown events.
During these interruptions, the system was maintained using alternative gas feeds to preserve
membrane hydration and structural integrity. Upon restoration of normal operating conditions,
the membrane performance recovered and remained stable, indicating robustness to transient
system upsets.

In terms of separation performance, the membranes achieved significantly improved results
compared to earlier testing campaigns. At elevated temperature (67°C) and feed pressure (4
atm), the modules exhibited an average CO₂ permeance of approximately 1,450 GPU and a
CO₂/N₂ selectivity of about 185 (see Figure 57). At lower pressures and temperatures,
permeance values decreased to the range of 900-1,000 GPU with slightly reduced selectivity,
reflecting the dependence of facilitated transport on operating conditions such as temperature and
partial pressure driving force. Overall, the performance remained consistent with laboratory
results obtained at OSU using simulated flue gas, confirming successful scale-up and
transferability of the membrane technology.

 Figure 57. Stability Plot of Spiral-Wound Module Tested with Flue Gas for 500 Hours

An important aspect of the campaign was the quantitative evaluation of CO₂ capture
performance, including recovery and purity. At baseline conditions (135°F and 1 atm feed
pressure), the CO₂ recovery was relatively low (approximately 7–10%) due to limited driving
force. Increasing the feed pressure to 4 atm improved recovery to approximately 30% and
further increasing the temperature to 153°F raised recovery levels to roughly 40–45%. Under
certain transient conditions such as reduced flow rate or declining feed CO₂ concentration, even
higher apparent recovery values were observed. The permeate stream consistently achieved high
CO₂ purity, typically 95% or greater on a dry basis under steady-state conditions. These results
demonstrate the capability of a single membrane module to achieve meaningful levels of CO₂
capture while producing a high-purity CO₂ stream suitable for compression and transport.

 106

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Another critical outcome of the 2018 test campaign was the demonstration of reproducibility
across multiple modules. Two additional spiral-wound modules were tested under similar
operating conditions and produced nearly identical permeance and selectivity values, confirming
that the fabrication process had matured to the point of producing consistent and reliable module
performance. This represents a key step toward commercialization, as reproducibility is essential
for scale-up and deployment in industrial systems.

The campaign also provided important insights into the sensitivity of membrane performance to
various operating parameters. Increasing feed pressure enhanced CO₂ recovery but could lead to
carrier saturation effects that limit permeance. Temperature increases improved both permeance
and recovery due to enhanced reaction kinetics and transport rates in the facilitated transport
mechanism. Variations in flue gas flow rate affected concentration polarization and therefore
had a measurable impact on performance. These observations highlight the importance of
optimizing operating conditions in the design of full-scale membrane systems.

Overall, the 2018 OSU membrane test campaign successfully demonstrated the combined
attributes of high performance, extended stability, and reproducibility in a field setting. The
results showed that spiral-wound facilitated transport membrane modules can achieve CO₂
permeance on the order of 1,450 GPU, maintain selectivity around 185, and operate continuously
for at least 500 hours with stable performance, even under conditions involving operational
disturbances and variable flue gas supply. Additionally, the modules achieved CO₂ capture rates
exceeding 40% and produced a high-purity CO₂ stream (>95%), indicating strong potential for
application in post-combustion carbon capture systems.

These results represent a meaningful step forward relative to earlier campaigns, particularly the
2015 OSU testing, by demonstrating longer-term stability, higher permeance, and improved
module reliability. The data generated in this campaign provide a foundation for scaling up to
larger membrane systems, including multi-stage configurations capable of achieving higher
capture efficiencies (e.g., ~90% CO₂ capture). The work also supports ongoing development
efforts aimed at integrating membrane modules into pilot-scale capture systems and ultimately
into commercial power plant applications.

2.38 Ohio State University Membranes (2022)

The 2022 test campaign of OSU membrane testing at the NCCC marked a transition from single-
module validation to integrated system-level demonstration of membrane-based carbon capture.
Conducted as part of a multi-year DOE-supported program focused on “novel transformational
membranes,” this campaign extended prior work from 2015 and 2018 by incorporating larger
membrane modules, improved materials, and a multi-module skid-based process configuration.
The primary objective was to evaluate the performance of a bench-scale, integrated membrane
system capable of achieving high CO₂ capture efficiency and purity under realistic flue gas
conditions while advancing toward pilot-scale deployment.

At the core of the 2022 campaign was the continued evolution of OSU’s facilitated transport
membrane technology. These membranes consist of a thin selective polymer layer containing
amine-based CO₂ carriers, supported by a highly permeable polymer substrate and reinforced
backing material. The facilitated transport mechanism enhances CO₂ separation by combining

 107

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

diffusion with reversible chemical reaction, leading to significantly higher permeance and
selectivity compared to conventional membranes.

A major advancement in this campaign was the scale-up of both membrane fabrication and
module size, enabled by continuous roll-to-roll manufacturing techniques. This allowed the
production of wider membranes and larger spiral‑wound modules suitable for integration into
practical capture systems. Unlike earlier campaigns that tested individual modules, the 2022
effort incorporated these modules into a bench-scale process skid, representing a more realistic
configuration for industrial deployment.

The skid system, represented in Figure 58, included all major process components required for
membrane-based separation, including feed gas conditioning, humidification, pressure control,
vacuum generation, and product gas handling. The configuration was designed to simulate a
multi-stage capture process, enabling evaluation not only of membrane performance but also of
system-level interactions such as flow distribution, pressure drops, and stage-to-stage coupling.

 Figure 58. Schematic of OSU’s Two-Stage Membrane Process

Testing involved both simulated and real flue gas streams, with the latter sourced from coal-fired
power generation. As in previous campaigns, the flue gas contained approximately 10-15% CO₂
along with nitrogen, oxygen, and trace contaminants such as SO₂ and NOₓ. The gas stream was
conditioned prior to entering the membrane system, including particulate removal and humidity
control, to ensure consistent and representative operating conditions.

The testing program emphasized parametric evaluation and steady-state operation, examining the
influence of variables such as feed pressure, temperature, flow rate, and CO₂ concentration on
system performance. The operating envelope was expanded relative to earlier tests, with higher
pressures and optimized vacuum conditions applied to maximize the transmembrane driving
force.

In contrast to earlier module-focused campaigns, the 2022 testing emphasized continuous system
operation, capturing the dynamic behavior of the integrated skid. This included evaluation under
steady-state conditions as well as under transient events such as startup, shutdown, and variations
in feed gas composition.

 108

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The test campaign demonstrated substantial improvements in both membrane performance and
overall system capability. The advanced membranes maintained high CO₂ permeance and
selectivity, consistent with or exceeding values observed in earlier campaigns, indicating that
performance gains achieved at the material level were successfully translated into larger modules
and system configurations.

More importantly, the integrated skid system demonstrated the ability to achieve high CO₂
capture efficiencies and product purity. The process design enabled capture levels in the range
of approximately 60-90%, with CO₂ purity exceeding 95% under optimized conditions. These
results represent a significant advancement beyond earlier single-module tests, which were
typically limited to partial capture due to single-stage operation.

The results also confirmed that multi-stage membrane configurations are effective for deep CO₂
capture, addressing one of the key limitations of membrane systems. By staging the separation
process, the system was able to progressively increase CO₂ concentration in the permeate stream
while maintaining favorable driving forces across each membrane stage. This demonstrates the
feasibility of achieving high capture efficiency without reliance on energy-intensive solvent
regeneration processes.

A critical focus of the 2022 campaign was the evaluation of system stability over extended
operation. While earlier campaigns had demonstrated module-level stability for durations up to
500 hours, the integrated skid testing added complexity by introducing additional components
and control requirements.

The results showed that the membrane system could operate consistently over extended periods,
maintaining stable permeance, selectivity, and overall capture performance. The system also
demonstrated resilience to operational variability, including fluctuations in flue gas composition
and flow rate. These findings indicate that both the membrane materials and the integrated
process configuration are sufficiently robust for sustained operation.

Another important outcome was the confirmation that flue gas impurities did not significantly
degrade membrane performance over the course of testing. The membranes showed good
tolerance to oxygen and low levels of sulfur and nitrogen oxides, continuing the trend observed
in earlier campaigns and reinforcing the suitability of the technology for real-world applications.

The integrated testing campaign provided valuable insights into process-level behavior and
optimization strategies. One key finding was the importance of balancing feed pressure and
vacuum conditions to maximize CO₂ flux while avoiding limitations such as carrier saturation or
excessive pressure drop. The interplay between temperature, humidity, and membrane chemistry
was also shown to be critical, particularly for facilitated transport membranes where reaction
kinetics play a significant role.

Additionally, the tests highlighted the impact of flow distribution and module configuration on
overall performance. Proper design of the skid system, including uniform gas distribution and
effective sealing, was essential to achieving consistent performance across modules. These
insights will be directly applicable to the design of larger pilot-scale systems.

 109

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The campaign also demonstrated the importance of process integration, including the
coordination of multiple membrane stages and the management of intermediate streams. The
ability to control and optimize these interactions is a key factor in achieving high capture
efficiency and minimizing energy consumption.

Compared to the 2015 and 2018 OSU testing campaigns, the 2022 effort represents a clear
progression toward commercialization. The earlier campaigns established the feasibility of
membrane technology and demonstrated module-level performance and stability. In contrast, the
2022 campaign validated the integration of multiple modules into a functional capture system,
addressing key challenges related to scale-up, system design, and operational control.

Performance also improved in terms of both permeance and capture efficiency, reflecting
advances in membrane materials and module fabrication. Perhaps most importantly, the ability
to achieve high capture levels (approaching 90%) with high purity CO₂ demonstrates that
membrane systems can compete with conventional solvent-based technologies in terms of
performance, while offering potential advantages in energy efficiency and operational simplicity.

2.39 NETL Membrane Materials

Beginning in 2015, NETL has conducted multiple test campaigns in the development of new
membrane materials for the purpose of CO₂ separation and capture. Computational approaches
such as atomistic modeling inform the development and synthesis of materials for
experimentation in the automated test skid at the NCCC (see Figure 59). The skid offers the
opportunity for NETL to obtain performance data on real flue gas for these new materials in the
early stages of development and with a very small amount of membrane required. The project
objective is to develop a scalable thin-film composite membrane for industrial carbon capture
that has a CO₂ permeance greater than 3,000 GPU and CO₂/N₂ selectivity of more than 25.

 Figure 59. NETL Membrane Test Equipment

 110

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Unlike conventional lab experiments that rely on idealized gas mixtures, NCCC testing exposes
candidate membranes to actual coal‑derived or natural gas boiler flue gas obtained from a
slipstream of an operating power plant. This capability allows researchers to directly assess how
membrane materials behave in the presence of water vapor, oxygen, and trace contaminants,
which are known to significantly influence separation performance.

The purpose of these bench‑scale campaigns is not to demonstrate process-scale capture, but to
generate high‑fidelity material performance data that can inform design decisions, identify
degradation mechanisms, and support scale‑up.

A key feature of the bench‑scale setup is its ability to maintain steady exposure to real flue gas
over periods ranging from tens of hours to multiple days. This operational mode enables
observation of transient effects, such as conditioning of the membrane in humid gas, as well as
early indicators of degradation or performance drift.

The membranes evaluated at the NCCC have primarily been polymer-based systems and their
derivatives. Early testing included baseline polymers such as polydimethylsiloxane and
fluorinated phosphazene materials, which provide reference points for permeability and
selectivity. More advanced configurations included thin‑film composite membranes, where a
selective layer is applied to a porous support, often in hollow fiber geometry.

In addition, significant effort has been directed toward evaluating mixed‑matrix membranes,
which incorporate inorganic filler materials—such as metal‑organic frameworks—into a polymer
matrix. These hybrid systems are intended to combine the processability of polymers with the
enhanced separation properties of engineered porous solids. Bench‑scale testing at the NCCC
provides one of the few opportunities to assess whether such enhancements persist under realistic
flue gas conditions, as opposed to idealized lab environments.

One of the most consistent findings from NCCC bench‑scale testing is that membrane
performance under real flue gas deviates significantly from laboratory expectations.
Measurements of CO₂ permeance conducted at NCCC are often lower than those obtained using
dry or simplified gas mixtures. This reduction is accompanied by changes in selectivity,
particularly for separations involving nitrogen and oxygen.

The discrepancy arises primarily from the presence of water vapor and other flue gas
constituents. Water can interact with both the polymer matrix and the transport pathways within
the membrane, altering diffusivity and solubility of CO₂. In some cases, water also introduces
concentration polarization effects, particularly when operating at low stage cuts or with limited
membrane area. These effects can reduce the apparent permeation rate and complicate
interpretation of results.

Trace contaminants such as sulfur oxides and nitrogen oxides further influence performance.
While concentrations are typically low, their presence over extended exposure periods can
contribute to chemical interactions or gradual fouling of the membrane surface. Oxygen in the
flue gas also introduces the possibility of oxidative degradation, particularly for certain classes of
polymers. Together, these factors create a testing environment that is substantially more
challenging than conventional laboratory conditions.

 111

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Bench‑scale testing at NCCC has demonstrated that many membrane materials can operate
stably over short to moderate durations when exposed to real flue gas. Individual test
campaigns, typically spanning on the order of 20 to 100 hours or more, show that performance
metrics such as permeance and selectivity can be measured reproducibly under controlled

Although these timeframes are shorter than those associated with long‑term durability testing,
they are sufficient to identify early‑stage degradation phenomena. For example, changes in
permeance over the course of a test can indicate plasticization, fouling, or chemical interaction
with impurities. Because the bench‑scale systems allow relatively rapid turnaround, multiple
membrane formulations can be evaluated sequentially, enabling efficient screening and
comparison.

The role of bench‑scale testing at NCCC is fundamentally tied to reducing uncertainty in
membrane development. Lab testing alone tends to overpredict performance because it does not
capture the full complexity of real flue gas. By introducing realistic conditions early in the
evaluation process, NETL is able to identify which membrane materials are robust enough to
warrant further development.

The data obtained from these tests are also critical for process modeling. Accurate permeation
measurements under realistic conditions are required to design membrane systems and to
estimate their energy requirements and cost. Without such data, projections of performance at
larger scale would be highly uncertain.

Equally important is the ability of bench‑scale testing to reveal failure modes. Observations of
reduced permeance, altered selectivity, or instability under continuous exposure provide insight
into how membrane materials must be improved. This feedback loop between material design
and field-relevant testing has been a defining feature of NETL’s membrane program.

Bench‑scale membrane testing at the NCCC has provided a uniquely valuable dataset for
understanding the behavior of CO₂ separation materials under realistic post‑combustion
conditions. The use of actual coal‑derived or natural gas boiler flue gas distinguishes this work
from conventional lab studies and has revealed important performance limitations associated
with moisture, contaminants, and complex gas compositions.

The results consistently demonstrate that membrane performance in real environments is more
constrained than suggested by idealized tests, underscoring the importance of field‑relevant
evaluation. At the same time, the testing has shown that many membrane materials can operate
stably over short durations and that meaningful, reproducible performance data can be obtained.

2.40 Akermin Enzymes

In 2016, Akermin conducted a test campaign with their advanced biocatalyst enabled solvent
system for low-cost CO₂ capture with a scale-up of a project tested at the NCCC in 2013. The
novel system, depicted in Figure 60, combines the next-generation biocatalyst delivery system
and a non-volatile salt solution, the AKM24 solvent. The biocatalyst delivery system entails
carbonic anhydrase enzymes, immobilized within proprietary polymeric micro-particles, which

 112

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

accelerate hydration of CO₂ to bicarbonate to enhance the performance of the solvent for CO₂
capture. The micro-particles have high surface area to enable higher mass transfer rates and
provide a protective environment for the enzymes against inactivation by temperature, solvent,
and shear forces.

 Figure 60. Process Flow Diagram of Akermin Enzyme Process

Parametric testing began in June 2016, with the addition of 0.7 kg of biocatalyst. The capture
immediately increased, from about 48% without catalyst to as much as 93% with catalyst.
However, unlike Akermin’s experience in the laboratory, the particle concentration in the
recirculating solution began to decline precipitously, and the CO₂ capture rate declined
correspondingly. To improve capture, another 0.7 kg of biocatalyst was added into the system.
The addition of the biocatalyst resulted in the immediate improvement of system performance.
However, as the biocatalyst concentration in the suspension declined, the CO₂ capture declined
as well. Controlling spillover rate was found to be problematic. After two days of operation, the
system was brought to partially stable performance, and data were gathered for the peak
performance at the design lean-to-rich spread with 30 wt% AKM24. Table 14 lists the peak
performance values.

 113

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Table 14. Peak Performance Values of Akermin Enzyme System
 Parameter Value
 Gas Flow Rate, scm/hr 30
 Liquid Flow Rate, L/hr 200
 CO₂ Loading Range, mol CO₂/mol K+ 0.35 – 0.65
 CO₂ Capture Rate 93.3
 KGa, mmol/m /s/kPa

 0.10
 Lean Multiplier, X 11
 Regeneration Energy, GJ/ton CO₂ 3.1

The measured regeneration energy of the bench unit at 3.1 GJ/ton CO₂ was higher than predicted
by the developed Aspen model. The reasons for this discrepancy appeared to be the following:

 • Due to issues balancing water make-up rates, the AKM-24 solvent concentration was
 diluted to 30 wt%, lower than the model value of 35 wt%. This increased the sensible
 duty compared to that predicted.
 • The NCCC rich loading target of 0.7 mol/mol was achieved, but commercially the rich
 loading target is 0.8 mol/mol CO₂. This higher rich loading is expected to have a
 significant impact on the observed energy.
 • High heat losses occurred in lines to and from the stripper due to scale.
The system operated over a one-month period. However, the run was affected by several
engineering issues making the performance unstable. The primary issues were uncontrolled rich
solvent spillover resulting in significant fluctuations of the particle hold up in the biocatalyst
recovery system and in solvent loading and a general loss of recirculating particles in the system
resulting from their accumulation in the absorber.

 It was hypothesized that the significant and unexpected particle retention in the absorber
resulted from low area efficiency of the absorber column (about 15%) that was primarily due to
the low liquid load in the system. Indeed, when the recirculation rate was increased for a brief
time, a significant amount of particles were eluted from the absorber. When the flow was
brought back to the design condition, the particles again concentrated in the absorber. Due to
these issues and a temporary plant shutdown (resulting in flue gas and steam supply
interruptions) the system operated for about 20 days. The anticipated performance of 90%
capture was rarely achieved due to particle accumulation in the absorber. The CO₂ capture rate
for the run is plotted in Figure 61. The average capture was around 70%.

 114

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 61. CO₂ Capture with Akermin Enzymes in 30 wt% AKM24

It remains unclear why such a significant accumulation of particles occurred in the pilot unit
while only little accumulation occurred in the lab-scale reactor. It was hypothesized that low
area efficiency of the bench unit absorber resulted in the particles accumulating in dry areas of
the column. Higher temperature of the bench unit absorber (sometimes reaching 58°C or 136°F)
could have contributed to the particle drying and increased adherence to the internals of the
column. Another hypothesis was that some unintended misalignment of the column contributed
to the decreased area efficiency consequently to the particle retention in the column. After one
month of operation, it became clear that the unit in the current configuration was not able to meet
the project objectives, and it was shut down. The following steps to improve the performance
might be considered:

 • Check the column alignment and re-align if necessary
 • Redesign the absorber to improve area efficiency
 • Modify configuration in the biocatalyst recovery system to achieve a more stable
 spillover and improve separation efficiency to minimize particle accumulation in
 multimedia filters

2.41 Precision Combustion Inc. Microlith Sorbent

Precision Combustion Inc. (PCI) conducted a series of bench-scale test campaigns at the NCCC
under a DOE SBIR Phase II project focused on developing a high efficiency post-combustion
carbon capture system. The central innovation evaluated during these campaigns is a structured
sorbent system consisting of a nanosorbent (modified metal-organic framework, MOF) coated
onto a proprietary Microlith mesh substrate.

The main objective of the test program was to validate the technical performance of this
structured sorbent system under realistic flue gas conditions and to demonstrate improvements
over conventional packed-bed or monolithic sorbent systems. Specific goals included:

 115

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 • Achieving high CO₂ capture efficiency with low pressure drop
 • Demonstrating rapid adsorption/desorption kinetics
 • Reducing regeneration energy requirements
 • Improving sorbent utilization and system compactness
 • Generating experimental data to support process modeling and future scale-up
The work aimed to advance a cost-effective, modular carbon capture technology suitable for
retrofit applications on fossil fuel power plants.

The PCI system integrates advanced sorbent materials with a structured metallic substrate. The
Microlith substrate is composed of ultra-short-channel metallic meshes with high geometric
surface area. This structure enables significantly enhanced heat and mass transfer compared to
conventional packed beds. The sorbent itself is a high-capacity nanosorbent—specifically, a
modified MOF—applied as a thin coating on the mesh. This configuration offers several
advantages:

 • Low pressure drop—Open mesh geometry minimizes flow resistance
 • High volumetric efficiency—Greater active surface area per unit volume
 • Rapid thermal response—Metal substrate allows efficient heat transfer during
 regeneration
 • Improved sorbent utilization—Thin coatings reduce diffusion limitations
 • Regenerability—Compatible with low-temperature swing or resistive heating
 regeneration
The system is designed as a modular cartridge-based unit, enabling scalable deployment and
flexible integration with existing plant systems.

PCI carried out three separate bench-scale test campaigns at the NCCC between 2020 and 2023.
These campaigns evaluated both system hardware and sorbent performance under different
operating conditions.

The first campaign in 2020 tested a first-generation sorbent and test bed, completed initial
shakedown with simulated flue gas followed by operation with coal-derived flue gas, and
verified basic system functionality and adsorption/desorption cycling.

The second campaign in 2022 introduced a second-generation, optimized sorbent and improved
test bed design. The flue gas source was switched to natural gas-derived flue gas from the
NCCC boiler. Testing focused on performance improvements and repeatability.

In 2023, PCI conducted a third campaign, which continued testing with the second-generation
system, extended operational duration and further optimization, and generated data for
performance validation and economic modeling. The test campaigns evaluated both steady-state
and dynamic cyclic performance.

 116

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The Microlith sorbent system demonstrated effective cyclic CO₂ capture performance, with
stable adsorption and desorption over repeated cycles. Key observations include:

 • Rapid breakthrough and regeneration—The structured geometry enabled fast adsorption
 kinetics and short cycle times.
 • Efficient thermal desorption: —The metallic substrate allowed quick heating and cooling,
 reducing cycle duration.
 • Consistent cyclic stability—Minimal performance degradation was observed over
 multiple cycles.
These results confirm the suitability of the technology for rapid temperature swing adsorption
processes.

The structured sorbent exhibited high mass transfer rates, attributed to short diffusion path
lengths and enhanced gas-solid contact. The sorbent also produced a low pressure drop,
significantly lower than packed-bed alternatives. This combination is particularly important for
large-scale systems, as it reduces parasitic energy consumption associated with flue gas handling.

The Microlith® configuration improved effective sorbent utilization compared to bulk or
pelletized forms. Benefits included more uniform exposure of active sites, reduced internal
diffusion resistance, and higher effective working capacity within the system. Performance
improvements between first- and second-generation sorbents demonstrated the impact of
material optimization and coating processes on overall capture efficiency.

The test campaigns also provided valuable engineering data:

 • Thermal management—The Microlith® substrate enables efficient heat transfer,
 supporting low-energy regeneration.
 • System integration—Modular design facilitates potential retrofit into existing plants.
 • Durability considerations—Coating adhesion and sorbent stability were validated over
 extended operation.
 • Scalability—Bench-scale data supports development of computational models for scale-
 up.
Additionally, results were integrated into computational fluid dynamics and process models to
predict performance at commercial scale.

2.42 GTI Energy ROTA-CAP Packed-Bed Solvent Process

The GTI ROTA‑CAP test campaign at the NCCC evaluated a process‑intensified, solvent-based
carbon capture system that replaces conventional packed columns with rotating packed beds
(RPBs). The integrated system, depicted in Figure 62, consisted of a dual-stage RPB absorber
with intercooling and a single-stage RPB regenerator using an advanced amine solvent. Testing
progressed from controlled parametric operation to extended campaigns with real flue gas,

 117

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

ultimately accumulating more than 1,600 hours of operation, including over 1,000 hours with
flue gas under continuous operation.

 Figure 62. Simplified ROTA-CAP Process Flow Diagram

The test results demonstrated consistently high CO₂ removal efficiencies exceeding 95%, along
with CO₂ product purity above 95%, maintained across a wide range of operating conditions.
Performance was sustained over inlet CO₂ concentrations ranging from approximately 4% to
22% by volume, confirming the system’s ability to operate effectively under conditions
representative of both natural gas and coal-derived flue gas streams. Importantly, these
performance levels were maintained during long-duration testing campaigns, with continuous
operation over multi-week periods, demonstrating strong process stability and reliability.

A key outcome of the campaign was the confirmation of enhanced mass transfer enabled by the
rotating packed-bed design. The centrifugal forces generated by the RPB create thin liquid films
and high interfacial area, increasing gas–liquid mass transfer rates by one to two orders of
magnitude compared to conventional packed columns. This intensification allows high capture
efficiency to be achieved at much shorter contact times and with reduced solvent inventory. As a
result, the ROTA‑CAP process significantly reduces equipment size and footprint while
maintaining or exceeding the performance of traditional solvent systems.

The integrated absorber and regenerator also demonstrated effective and stable solvent
regeneration performance. The single-stage RPB regenerator consistently produced sufficiently
lean solvent to support high CO₂ removal efficiency in the absorber. The compact rotating
equipment enabled effective heat and mass transfer in the stripping process, supporting
continuous operation without performance degradation. These results confirm that process
intensification can be successfully applied to both absorption and regeneration steps without
compromising system stability.

 118

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The testing program also included evaluation of the solvent using the SSTU to assess
degradation, performance retention, and operational robustness under realistic flue gas
conditions. The SSTU testing showed that the solvent maintained stable absorption performance
with limited degradation over extended exposure, even in the presence of typical flue gas
impurities. The evaluation confirmed that solvent properties, including CO₂ loading capacity
and regeneration effectiveness, remained sufficiently consistent to support long-duration
operation of the ROTA‑CAP system. This provided important validation that the advanced
solvent is compatible with the intensified RPB environment and does not introduce additional
operational risks.

From a system perspective, the ROTA‑CAP process demonstrated flexibility across varying flue
gas compositions while maintaining high performance. Stable operation was achieved across the
full range of test conditions, confirming that the system is suitable for a variety of industrial
applications. In addition, the extensive operating hours and continuous campaigns verified that
the rotating equipment and integrated process configuration can sustain reliable, steady-state
operation.

Techno-economic analysis based on the NCCC test data estimated a CO₂ capture cost of
approximately $41.18 per tonne, compared to about $45.75 per tonne for a conventional solvent
benchmark, representing roughly a 10% reduction in cost. While this value does not yet meet
DOE cost targets, the analysis indicates a clear pathway for further reductions through design
optimization, improved heat integration, and scale-up. Supporting engineering assessments
determined that the technology is scalable, with preliminary feasibility for systems on the order
of several thousand tonnes per day of CO₂ capture.

Overall, the NCCC testing demonstrated that the ROTA‑CAP system combines high CO₂ capture
efficiency, high product purity, compact equipment design, stable long-duration operation, and
validated solvent performance. These results confirm that rotating packed-bed technology can
deliver meaningful process intensification while maintaining robust and consistent operation,
supporting further scale-up and future deployment.

2.43 GTI Energy Graphene Oxide-Based Membrane

The GTI Energy test campaign evaluated a bench-scale GO membrane process for
post‑combustion CO₂ capture in 2023 and 2024. The technology, referred to as the GO2 process,
integrates two complementary membrane types—a high-selectivity membrane (GO‑1) and a
high-flux membrane (GO‑2)—to achieve both efficient separation and high throughput. Figure
63 provides the GO2 process flow diagram. The test campaign focused on scaling up membrane
fabrication to practical module sizes, validating membrane performance under simulated flue gas
conditions, and demonstrating integrated system operation at bench scale using a multi-stage
configuration representative of a deployable capture system.

 119

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 63. Process Flow Diagram of the GO2 Process

A major component of the testing effort was the development and scale-up of the GO
membranes themselves, which are formed from nano-engineered graphene oxide layers
deposited onto hollow fiber supports. These membranes rely on controlled nanochannels and
CO₂‑affinitive transport pathways to preferentially separate CO₂ from nitrogen. Over the course
of the project, the membranes were successfully scaled from small laboratory coupons to
modules with effective surface areas ranging from approximately 50-100 cm² and further to
~1,000 cm². At these scales, the GO‑1 membranes achieved CO₂/N₂ selectivity of at least 200
with permeance exceeding ~1,000 GPU, while the GO‑2 membranes achieved much higher
permeance (≥2,500 GPU) with moderate selectivity (≥20). These performance levels exceed
conventional membrane benchmarks and are critical to enabling a practical membrane-based
CO₂ capture process.

Following membrane development, testing advanced to integrated system operation, where the
GO‑1 and GO‑2 membranes were combined in sequence to form a multi-stage capture process.
This configuration allowed the high-selectivity membrane to perform primary separation while
the high-flux membrane provided additional CO₂ recovery and enrichment. Under steady-state
operation at NCCC conditions, the integrated system achieved CO₂ removal efficiencies in the
range of approximately 70–90% along with CO₂ product purity of at least 95%, demonstrating
that the combined membrane approach can meet performance targets typically associated with
solvent-based systems.

Testing also emphasized operational stability and robustness. The membranes were evaluated
under repeated startup and shutdown conditions and maintained stable performance across
multiple operating cycles. Testing demonstrated good durability at typical process temperatures
(~50-57°C), and the membranes showed minimal performance degradation when exposed to
simulated flue gas containing contaminants, including oxygen and acid gases. This stability is
particularly important for deployment in real-world applications, where flue gas impurities and
transient operation can impact performance of conventional membranes.

The campaign further confirmed that the GO-based membranes can operate effectively within a
compact, modular system configuration. Because membrane separation does not require phase

 120

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

change, the process avoids the large thermal duty associated with solvent regeneration and
enables a simplified process flow. The combination of high permeance and selectivity reduces
the required membrane area and compression load relative to conventional membrane systems,
contributing to improved overall process efficiency.

Techno-economic analysis conducted using the experimental data indicated that the GO2
membrane system can provide reductions in both the cost of electricity and cost of CO₂ capture
relative to a baseline amine process, even prior to further system optimization. The primary
drivers of this improvement are reduced equipment size, elimination of large solvent
regeneration systems, and potential reductions in parasitic energy consumption. While further
optimization and scale-up are needed to fully meet long-term cost targets, the results demonstrate
a clear pathway toward competitive membrane-based carbon capture.

The combination of high membrane performance, demonstrated stability, and successful
integration into a multi-stage process represents a significant step forward in membrane-based
capture systems. The results indicate strong potential for further development toward pilot-scale
demonstration and eventual commercial deployment, particularly in applications where compact
footprint and modularity are critical.

2.44 CORMETECH Sorbent

CORMETECH is conducting a bench‑scale test campaign to advance the development and
optimization of a monolithic amine contactor system for carbon capture applied to NGCC units.
The technology utilizes a structured monolith with an amine‑functionalized sorbent designed to
enhance mass transfer, reduce pressure drop, and improve overall CO₂ capture efficiency for
low‑CO₂‑concentration flue gas typical of NGCC systems.

The test campaign is focused on evaluating key aspects of sorbent and system performance,
including the impacts of nitrogen oxides and sulfur dioxide on sorbent stability and capture
efficiency. Additional objectives include demonstrating a minimum of one month of continuous,
steady‑state operation achieving greater than 95% CO₂ capture efficiency and greater than 95%
CO₂ product purity, as well as assessing system performance under dynamic operating conditions
representative of load‑following NGCC operation.

Following initial parametric testing conducted in late 2024, CORMETECH completed additional
flue gas testing in 2025 to further characterize sorbent behavior, regeneration performance, and
system operability. Based on these results, the system has undergone targeted modifications to
improve durability and performance. Testing is scheduled to resume in the third quarter of 2026
with next‑generation sorbents expected to provide enhanced capacity, stability, and overall
process efficiency.

2.45 RPI/SUNY Buffalo Sorbents

RPI and SUNY Buffalo (University at Buffalo, The State University of New York) are
developing a transformational MLD tailor-made, size-sieving sorbent and using a pressure swing
adsorption process developed by University of South Carolina for CO₂ capture. The sorbent is
designed to have high CO₂ adsorption capacity and excellent stability in the presence of water

 121

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

vapor. The objective of this project was to develop a transformational MLD tailor-made size-
sieving sorbent integrated with a tailored PSA cycle schedule that can be installed in new or
retrofitted into existing pulverized coal power plants for CO₂ capture with a cost of electricity at
least 30% lower than a supercritical pulverized coal power with CO₂ capture, or approximately
$30 per tonne of CO₂ captured, and with it being ready for demonstration by 2030.

In this project, the project team member at the State University of New York at Buffalo
developed a transformational Ti-13X sorbent by employing MLD or atomic layer deposition
technology to passivate the strong adsorption sites of conventional 13X zeolite. This treatment
increased the CO₂ working capacity and CO₂/N₂ separation selectivity, while also reducing the
CO₂ heat of adsorption and improving tolerance to water vapor and flue gas contaminants.
Meanwhile, the University of South Carolina developed a proprietary PSA cycle schedule
concept for CO₂ capture from flue gas. This process requires far fewer number of adsorption
beds (relative to conventional adsorption processes), thereby reducing the CO₂ capture cost. By
combining the advantages of the Ti-13X sorbent and the proprietary PSA cycle, a bench-scale
skid was designed and constructed. The skid was extensively tested at the University of South
Carolina including comparison between 13X and Ti-13X and effects of water vapor on CO₂
capture performance. During these tests, greater than 90% CO₂ capture efficiency with >95%
CO₂ dry-basis purity was achieved for the Ti-13X adsorbent. The skid was then moved to the
NCCC for testing with actual coal flue gas or actual natural gas boiler flue gas modified to
replicate the CO₂ concentrations found in coal flue gas, NGCC, and industrial flue gases. GTI
Energy led the field tests at the NCCC.

A three-bed PSA system loaded with approximately 2 kg of Ti-13X sorbent was installed in
NCCC’s Lab-Scale Test Unit, where testing was first conducted using simulated coal-fired flue
gas (15.7 vol% CO₂, wet basis). Figure 64 provides a flow diagram of the process, and Table 15
identifies the streams numbered in the figure.

 Figure 64. University of South Carolina Patented Flowsheet for CO₂ Capture from Wet Flue Gas

 122

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Table 15. Identification of Streams from Figure 64

Under a feed flow rate of 13 standard liter per minute (SLPM) and a vacuum pressure of 5 kPa,
the system achieved a CO₂ dry-basis purity of 96.3 vol% and a CO₂ capture efficiency of 92.6%
and demonstrated good long-term and dynamic stability over a 1,000-hour period. Parametric
tests indicated:

 • The CO₂ dry-basis purity remained constant while CO₂ capture efficiency decreased with
 increasing vacuum pressure.
 • The water vapor concentration had minimal impact on CO₂ capture performance.
 • Higher feed flow rates and CO₂ concentrations resulted in slightly higher CO₂ dry-basis
 purity but lower capture efficiency.
When testing simulated NGCC flue gas (5 vol% CO₂, wet basis), under a vacuum pressure of 15
kPa and a feed flow rate of 18 SLPM, the system achieved a CO2 dry-basis purity of 85.6 vol%
and a CO₂ capture efficiency of 31.1%. Tests were then conducted for simulated industrial flue
gas (24 vol% CO₂, wet basis). Under a mild vacuum pressure of 20 kPa and a feed flow rate of 8
SLPM, the system achieved a CO₂ dry-basis purity of 96.4 vol% and a CO₂ capture efficiency of
70.5%. The performance also remained highly stable during a 200-hour long-term test. Testing
results indicate the Ti-13X/PSA technology is promising for CO₂ capture at coal-fired power or
industrial plants where CO₂ concentrations are higher than 10 vol%.

Overall, the Ti-13X/PSA bench-scale skid had been tested for over eight months under multiple
test periods at the NCCC with different types of flue gases for about 1,200 hours. During these
tests, the Ti-13X adsorbent did not undergo regeneration beyond standard PSA cycling,
demonstrating good stability. This good stability enables continuous, reliable PSA operation
while reducing energy consumption, maintenance and operational costs.

 123

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Following these successful tests at the NCCC, the future plan is to design and build an
engineering-scale Ti-13X/PSA CO₂ capture system, conduct tests at coal-fired power or
industrial plants, and demonstrate a continuous, steady-state operation for a minimum of two
months.
