National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

3.0 Carbon Conversion and Removal Test Campaigns

3.1 Southern States Energy Board/Aircapture Sorbent-Based DAC Process

SSEB, in partnership with Aircapture and supported by the U.S. DOE, conducted a multi‑year
field demonstration of DAC technology at the NCCC. The effort represents one of the first
integrated, commercially relevant DAC field tests in the U.S. and is a key step in advancing
atmospheric CO2 removal technologies toward deployment.

The DAC RECO2UP project was executed over a reporting period spanning October 2020
through late 2024, with field testing conducted primarily during the later phases after design and
construction were completed. The NCCC provided a controlled but industrially relevant
environment that allowed the project team to bridge the gap between lab-scale validation and
real-world operation.

Testing consisted of five distinct campaigns focused on evaluating system performance,
operability, and integration under varying conditions. Across these campaigns, the system
accumulated more than 3,300 hours of operation, generating a substantial dataset for
performance characterization and techno-economic analysis.

The Aircapture system tested at NCCC is based on a solid-amine adsorption-desorption process
designed to capture CO₂ directly from ambient air. Unlike traditional carbon capture systems
that treat concentrated industrial flue gases, DAC systems must process very low concentrations
of CO₂ (approximately 400 ppm in air), which fundamentally increases energy and mass transfer
challenges.

The system uses a structured contactor—described as a honeycomb or monolithic structure—
impregnated with polymeric amine sorbents. Ambient air is drawn through the contactor, where
CO₂ molecules diffuse into the sorbent material and chemically bind to active amine sites. Once
saturated, the contactor undergoes regeneration using low-grade heat and vacuum. Steam
releases the captured CO₂, producing a high-purity gas stream suitable for sequestration or
utilization.

A key feature of the RECO2UP concept is the integration of energy recovery. The system is
designed to utilize low-quality waste heat commonly available at industrial sites, thereby
reducing the net energy penalty of DAC. Thermal integration and heat recovery were central
objectives of testing, as these factors strongly influence overall cost and scalability.

Testing at NCCC demonstrated that the integrated DAC system could consistently produce a
concentrated CO₂ stream exceeding initial targets, achieving purities up to approximately 99
percent in some operational modes. This level of purity is sufficient for pipeline transport,
geologic sequestration, or conversion into value-added products such as fuels or chemicals.

The extended operational period (over 3,300 hours) enabled the team to evaluate system
durability, cyclic performance, and sensitivity to operating conditions such as temperature,
humidity, and airflow. The dataset supported development of a techno-economic analysis and

 125

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

life cycle analysis, which were used to estimate cost trajectories and environmental impacts of
scaled deployment.

The testing also validated improvements in system design, including enhanced mass transfer in
the contactor structure, reduced pressure drop, and better thermal integration. These
improvements contributed to lowering both capital and operating cost projections relative to
earlier DAC concepts.

A major objective of the project was to evaluate DAC technology under conditions
approximating real industrial environments rather than controlled laboratory settings. The
NCCC testing infrastructure allowed integration with utilities, heat sources, and operational
constraints that mimic potential commercial sites. This provided confidence that the technology
can transition beyond pilot scale.

The results demonstrated that modular DAC units can be deployed flexibly at locations with
access to low-grade heat and suitable infrastructure. The concept of co-locating DAC systems
with existing industrial facilities—especially those with waste heat streams or dilute CO₂
emissions—was shown to offer meaningful efficiency gains.

In addition, the project confirmed that DAC systems can be designed as modular units, enabling
distributed deployment rather than requiring large, centralized facilities. This has important
implications for scaling strategies, as it allows deployment in a wide range of geographic and
industrial contexts.

The extensive operational dataset generated during testing was one of the most valuable
outcomes of the project. With thousands of hours of field data, the team was able to:

 • Develop and refine process models for adsorption and desorption cycles
 • Quantify energy consumption and recovery efficiencies
 • Assess system reliability and maintenance requirements
 • Validate assumptions used in economic models
This information supports both future system optimization and broader industry understanding of
DAC performance at scale.

The project also advanced the technology’s maturity to approximately Technology Readiness
Level (TRL) 5, indicating validation in a relevant environment. This represents a significant step
toward commercial demonstration and deployment.

3.2 Southern Research Ethane-to-Ethylene Process

Southern Research conducted a field-scale test campaign at the NCCC to demonstrate an
innovative thermocatalytic process that converts ethane and CO₂ derived from coal-fired flue gas
into ethylene. The work was performed over a project period from February 2019 through
November 2021, representing a 33‑month effort that included catalyst development, scale-up,
and extended field testing.

 126

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

The technology centers on a nano-engineered catalyst enabling a modified oxidative
dehydrogenation pathway using CO₂ as a soft oxidant. This approach differs from conventional
steam cracking by reducing energy intensity, lowering operating temperatures, and enabling
utilization of CO₂ as a feedstock. The overarching goal of the project was to validate this
concept at field scale, demonstrating both technical feasibility and potential economic and
environmental advantages when integrated with carbon capture systems.

Key objectives included scaling up catalyst production, validating catalyst performance under
realistic conditions, integrating a field-scale skid with NCCC infrastructure, and performing
long-duration operation to assess durability and process stability. An additional objective was to
evaluate techno-economic and lifecycle performance relative to conventional ethylene
production routes.

The project progressed through lab validation and ultimately into field-scale testing at NCCC.
After initial delays related to flue gas availability and pandemic restrictions, continuous
operation with actual process streams began in 2021. The field testing included two primary
operating modes:

 • Captured CO₂ (concentrated stream) derived from an amine-based capture system at the
 NCCC
 • Direct flue gas (dilute stream) from coal and natural gas combustion sources
The most significant performance data were obtained using concentrated CO₂ streams. Field-
scale operation began in August 2021 and continued for approximately 73 days, during which
about 600 hours of catalyst runtime data were collected.

The project achieved multiple extended-duration test periods, with cumulative testing exceeding
1,000 hours across lab and field environments, and at least 500 hours of continuous field-scale
operation with captured CO₂, representing the most relevant dataset for commercialization
assessment.

Under optimal conditions using captured CO₂, the catalyst demonstrated strong and consistent
performance. Ethane conversion rates ranged from approximately 30–50% depending on feed
composition, with ethylene being the primary product. Ethylene productivity reached on the
order of 300 g/kg catalyst/hour, accompanied by co-production of CO and small amounts of
hydrogen.

Catalyst stability was a key outcome of the campaign. Across the 500-hour field run with high
purity CO₂ feed, the catalyst maintained relatively consistent performance despite operational
interruptions, with minimal degradation and stable reaction-regeneration cycling. The process
operated at approximately 650°C and near atmospheric pressure, conditions that are milder than
traditional steam cracking and favorable for integration with existing industrial infrastructure.

Lab studies conducted prior to field testing had already demonstrated high selectivity
(approaching 95%) and ethylene yields exceeding 50% under controlled conditions, with less
than 5% degradation over extended runs. These results were largely corroborated at field scale
when high-purity CO₂ was used.

 127

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

A major finding of the campaign was the sensitivity of the process to flue gas composition,
particularly the presence of oxygen and water vapor. While the catalyst showed strong tolerance
to contaminants such as SO₂ and NO, even modest levels of oxygen and water negatively
affected performance.

When using direct flue gas, the presence of oxygen led to competitive combustion reactions,
reducing CO₂ utilization and decreasing ethylene selectivity. Approximately half of the ethane
feed could be lost to combustion under these conditions, significantly diminishing both process
efficiency and economic benefits.

Mitigation strategies, including upstream oxygen removal via controlled combustion, were tested
and enabled additional operation time (up to 500 hours). However, these configurations reduced
the overall economic and environmental advantages compared to the captured CO₂ case.

Techno-economic analysis performed as part of the project indicated that the captured CO₂
scenario provided the most promising pathway. Estimated ethylene production costs were
approximately $0.29/kg, comparable to the low end of conventional production costs.

While further engineering development is required—particularly for handling dilute flue gas
streams—the results represent a meaningful step toward commercializing CO₂ utilization
pathways for the petrochemical industry. The Southern Research test campaign at the NCCC
provided validation of a promising alternative to conventional ethylene production, with
demonstrated operational durability, competitive economics, and significant emissions reduction
potential.

3.3 University of California, Los Angeles Reversa Process (2021)

UCLA conducted two major test campaigns at the NCCC with the Reversa CO₂ mineralization-
based concrete production process: an initial 2021 demonstration establishing feasibility of the
carbonation curing approach using flue gas, followed by a 2024 campaign that built upon these
findings to expand product scope and further refine process performance. The later effort
applied insights from the earlier test to advance the technology toward improved efficiency,
scalability, and readiness for commercial implementation.

The 2021 test campaign represented one of the first practical evaluations of converting dilute
CO₂ emissions directly into construction materials at pilot scale. The technology evaluated in
this campaign was developed by the UCLA CarbonBuilt team and had previously been
recognized as the NRG COSIA Carbon XPRIZE grand prize winner, highlighting its potential
for large-scale carbon utilization and emissions reduction.

The campaign was conducted over a multi‑week period in 2021 and focused on validating
process performance, product quality, and operational feasibility using continuous concrete
production methods. The process is based on accelerated carbonation of alternative cementitious
materials, where calcium-rich feedstocks react with CO₂ to form calcium carbonate that serves as
the primary binding phase. A defining feature of the technology is its ability to use CO₂ directly
from flue gas streams without the need for capture, purification, or compression, allowing
simplified integration with industrial emission sources.

 128

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Testing involved installation and operation of a modular concrete production system connected
to flue gas streams representative of fossil fuel combustion. The system was configured to
produce masonry-scale concrete products, with CO₂ introduced during the curing phase under
controlled conditions. The campaign simulated industrial production workflows by continuously
cycling through mixing, forming, and curing operations, demonstrating compatibility with
conventional precast manufacturing practices.

During the test campaign, more than 5,000 concrete masonry units were produced and cured
using the CO₂-based process. These units were manufactured using standard production
equipment adapted to enable carbonation curing. The results showed that the process could be
implemented using commercially relevant methods, with no significant barriers to integration
into existing manufacturing lines.

A primary objective of the testing was to assess whether CO₂-cured products could meet
performance requirements comparable to conventional concrete. The produced units achieved
compressive strength and durability characteristics consistent with traditional masonry products.
Strength development occurred rapidly due to the fast carbonation reaction, supporting curing
times aligned with industrial production rates. The resulting products were demonstrated to be
functional replacements for conventional concrete masonry units.

Process performance during the campaign indicated that CO₂ uptake occurred effectively within
the curing system, with a portion of the injected CO₂ permanently mineralized into the concrete
matrix as stable calcium carbonate. This mineralization both contributed to structural integrity
and enabled long-term carbon storage within the product. The system showed tolerance to dilute
CO₂ concentrations and typical flue gas constituents, with no significant degradation in product
quality observed under the tested conditions.

Operationally, the campaign demonstrated stable and repeatable performance across multiple
production cycles. The system maintained consistent curing conditions despite variations in flue
gas composition and supply. Process parameters such as gas flow rate, residence time, and
moisture conditions were adjusted to optimize carbonation efficiency and product uniformity.
The testing highlighted the importance of curing chamber design and gas distribution in
achieving consistent carbonation throughout the product volume.

Environmental performance was a key outcome of the campaign. The Reversa process achieved
a substantial reduction in embodied carbon relative to conventional concrete production, driven
by both the reduction or elimination of Portland cement and the direct incorporation of CO₂ into
the product. The combined effect resulted in reductions in lifecycle emissions exceeding 50%
compared to standard concrete masonry production. The process also demonstrated the potential
to further reduce emissions depending on material selection and process optimization.

The campaign further evaluated production throughput and scalability. Concrete manufacturing
rates were consistent with typical precast operations, indicating that carbonation curing could be
incorporated without significantly affecting plant productivity. The modular design of the
system allowed adaptation to different product geometries and production scales, supporting
potential application across a wide range of concrete products.

 129

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Several technical considerations were identified during testing. While the process performed
well with dilute flue gas, carbonation efficiency was influenced by CO₂ concentration, moisture
content, and feed material composition. Optimization of curing conditions and material
formulations was necessary to achieve consistent product properties. These findings provided
guidance for further process refinement and scale-up efforts.

3.4 University of California, Los Angeles Reversa Process (2024)

The 2024 test campaign conducted by the UCLA CarbonBuilt team represented a focused effort
to validate scale, consistency, and operational flexibility of the process under extended, realistic
test conditions. Building directly on the earlier demonstration, the work shifted toward sustained
production, higher throughput, and the ability to operate across multiple concrete product
formats without sacrificing performance or process stability.

The campaign was carried out over a multi-week testing period, using natural gas-derived flue
gas streams with CO₂ concentrations representative of industrial conditions. Operating in this
environment allowed the system to be evaluated under continuous flow conditions rather than
discrete batch testing, which is a critical step in understanding how the process behaves over
extended durations. The test configuration integrated a modularized production system with the
available flue gas infrastructure, enabling direct coupling between emissions and concrete curing
operations.

A central aspect of the 2024 program was the deliberate expansion beyond a single product type.
In addition to concrete masonry units, which had been the basis for prior testing, the campaign
included segmental retaining wall blocks and precast concrete manhole structures. This required
the process to accommodate differences in size, geometry, and curing requirements while
maintaining uniformity in carbonation. The successful production of all three product categories
demonstrated that the system can be adapted to varying manufacturing conditions without major
modifications.

Production during the campaign reached sustained multi-ton levels across all product types.
Approximately 31 tonnes of concrete masonry units, 51 tonnes of retaining wall blocks, and 40
tonnes of manhole structures were produced over the course of the test campaign. These values
reflect continuous operation at rates that exceed typical pilot-scale demonstrations, indicating
that the system can maintain throughput over time rather than only achieving short-duration peak
performance. The ability to sustain these rates is particularly important, as it confirms that the
process is not limited by transient conditions or short-term stability constraints.

Process performance was also evaluated through measurement of CO₂ uptake and consistency
across production runs. The system achieved uptake levels exceeding 0.2 grams of CO₂ per
gram of reactive material, indicating effective utilization of the available flue gas stream. This
level of performance was maintained over extended operation and across multiple product forms,
suggesting that the process does not exhibit significant sensitivity to changes in geometry or
production conditions. The results indicate stable reaction kinetics and effective gas-solid
interaction throughout the curing process.

 130

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Material performance testing confirmed that all products met applicable industry standards for
strength and durability. The concrete produced during the campaign exhibited properties
consistent with conventional materials, supporting its use in standard construction applications.
The consistency of these results across different product types also indicates that the curing
process can be controlled with sufficient precision to produce uniform outcomes.

Relative to the earlier campaign, the 2024 effort demonstrated clear improvements in operational
control and system robustness. Expanding to multiple product types required tighter control over
curing conditions, including gas flow distribution, exposure time, and handling of varying mold
geometries. The results show that these parameters can be managed effectively, with no evident
loss in carbonation efficiency or material performance.

Another important observation from the campaign is the system’s ability to operate continuously
with real flue gas inputs. Industrial gas streams can fluctuate in composition, temperature, and
flow rate, all of which can influence reaction behavior. The campaign demonstrated that the
process could accommodate these variations without significant impact on performance,
suggesting resilience to the types of variability encountered in practical operating environments.

3.5 Helios-NRG Algae Production

Helios-NRG conducted a test campaign at the NCCC in 2022 to evaluate a biological carbon
capture and utilization process. The system uses a multi-stage continuous algae cultivation
process to capture CO₂ from post-flue gas desulfurization streams and convert it into algal
biomass with potential downstream value in fuels, feed, and other products. This effort marked
the first time an algae-based carbon capture system was tested at the NCCC, expanding the range
of technologies assessed beyond conventional solvent- and sorbent-based systems.

Field testing was conducted between June and September 2022, with the primary operational
period occurring from mid-June through the end of August. The system was installed at the
bench-scale test area, with outdoor placement allowing the use of natural sunlight as the primary
energy input. Supporting inoculum preparation was performed indoors. The process treated flue
gas from Plant Gaston Unit 5, representing a blend of coal- and natural gas-derived flue gas with
an average CO₂ concentration of 10.8%. The mixture in the blend varied as power generation
requirements necessitated the burning of different amounts of natural gas and coal. CO₂ cylinder
gas was utilized to make up the deficit to mimic 12% CO₂ flue gas when the power plant was
mainly utilizing natural gas for power generation, which is nominally ~6% CO₂.

Over the course of the campaign, more than 900 hours of gas flow were processed through the
system, including a continuous operating period exceeding 100 hours on flue gas derived from
coal only. Throughout testing, a range of environmental and process variables were monitored,
including solar intensity, temperature, gas composition, flow rates, and biomass productivity.
The outdoor configuration allowed evaluation under realistic conditions but also introduced
variability due to weather and sunlight fluctuations.

The system achieved performance levels that exceeded project targets. CO₂ capture efficiency
reached 87.2 percent, while algae productivity averaged 30.7 g/m²/day, both surpassing the
respective targets of 80 percent capture and 25 g/m²/day productivity. Figure 65 provides a plot

 131

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

of the performance parameters during a selected test period. These results demonstrate that the
MSC system can operate effectively on actual flue gas while maintaining high conversion
efficiency and stable biomass production. Performance was consistent with or better than prior
testing using simulated flue gas, indicating that contaminants present in treated power plant
emissions did not adversely affect system operation.

 Figure 65. Test Period Meeting Target Goals in Productivity and Capture

Environmental conditions were a key factor in system performance. Variations in sunlight
intensity, particularly extended periods of low light, reduced productivity and ultimately
contributed to early termination of the test. Despite these challenges, average test results
remained strong and aligned with previous outdoor trials, suggesting that the technology is
robust but sensitive to site-specific conditions such as shading, weather patterns, and ambient
temperature.

From an operational perspective, the test demonstrated that algae-based systems can be
integrated with existing post-combustion infrastructure and can operate continuously under field
conditions. Additionally, the results highlight important considerations for scale-up, including
the need for consistent solar exposure, improved management of environmental variability, and
design modifications to accommodate rainfall and temperature extremes.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

4.0 Gasification and Pre-Combustion Carbon Capture Test Campaigns
The NCCC gasification process, depicted in Figure 66, featured several key components of an
integrated gasification combined cycle (IGCC) plant, including a Transport Gasifier, syngas
cooling and filtration, and ash removal systems. Operation of the gasification process supported
testing of gasification-related and pre-combustion CO₂ capture technologies.

The NCCC’s pre-combustion CO₂ capture program allowed evaluation of solvents, sorbents,
catalysts, membranes, and other emerging technologies at an appropriate scale with coal-derived
syngas produced during gasification runs. The flexibility and scale of the NCCC was well suited
to test CO₂ capture and gas cleanup technologies and accelerate their advancement through the
component testing and pilot plant stages of development. The facilities supported testing of
multiple projects in parallel with a wide range of test equipment sizes leading up to pre-
commercial equipment sufficient to guide the design of demonstration-scale processes.

4.1 Gasification Operation

During the contract period, the gasification process operated for about 4,000 hours over five test
runs, G-1 through G-5, bringing the total gasification operating experience beginning in 1999 to
over 24,800 hours. The fuels used included PRB coal, raw biomass, and Mississippi lignite.
Table 16 summarizes the gasification runs and the technologies tested. Table 17 provides the

4.2 Southern Research Fischer-Tropsch Catalyst and Steam Reformer

Southern Research developed two separate technologies for improving the technical and
economic viability of converting coal and/or biomass to jet fuel. The first technology is a
Fischer-Tropsch process using Chevron’s hybrid cobalt-zeolite F-T catalyst to selectively
produce jet fuel from syngas. The second technology, autothermal reforming, was evaluated by
testing a high-temperature reforming catalyst in the presence of H₂S and other syngas

 134

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

contaminants. Autothermal reforming research focused on developing improved reforming
catalysts for lower cost and improved durability in the presence of syngas contaminants and
demonstrating the performance on both simulated syngas and gasifier-derived syngas. After
completing initial testing in 2014, Southern Research performed additional tests with the F-T
catalyst in 2015. In 2017, Southern Research tested the scaled-up F-T catalyst and tested the
steam reformer for the first time. Figure 67 shows the reformer enclosure as well as the F-T
reactor installed at the NCCC.

 Reformer
 F-T Skid
 Enclosure

 Figure 67. Southern Research Fischer-Tropsch Catalyst and Steam Reformer Equipment

Specific project objectives were to:

 • Improve a high-temperature autothermal reformer to reform tar and light hydrocarbons,
 decompose ammonia in the presence H₂S and other coal syngas contaminants, and deliver
 the required H2/CO ratio for F-T synthesis
 • Test a second-generation cobalt-zeolite hybrid F-T catalyst that will produce hydrocarbon
 product containing at least 75% C5-C20 hydrocarbons, no C21+ waxy hydrocarbons,
 productivity greater than 0.7 g C5+/g catalyst/hr, and at least 65% jet-fuel-range (C8-
 C15) hydrocarbon selectivity in the C5 to C20 product
 • Incorporate a novel and efficient heat-exchange reactor system at bench-scale to enable
 the use of the highly active and productive catalyst and large diameter reactor tubes
During the 2015 testing, the F-T catalyst was tested in Southern Research’s coal-biomass-to-
liquid 2-inch reactor skid utilizing a micro-fiber entrapment catalyst technology to aid in heat
transfer for the highly active catalyst. The test campaign was a continuation of parametric
experiments conducted in the lab to screen catalysts for productivity and selectivity performance.
The selected catalyst was tested at a variety of pressures, temperatures, and syngas compositions.
The tests successfully achieved the catalyst performance targets. However, due to difficulties in

 135

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

precisely controlling the operating conditions, the results were not quite as high as those
observed in the lab. Nonetheless, the catalyst was able to achieve the necessary hydrocarbon
productivity and product selectivity at conditions that were less than optimal. Figure 68 plots the
CO conversion, methane selectivity, and light hydrocarbon selectivity with varying operating

 Figure 68. CO Conversion and Product Selectivity of Southern Research Fischer-Tropsch Catalyst

Table 18 summarizes the yields and carbon distributions for the experiments. The target
productivity and jet fuel (C8-C16) selectivity values were reached or exceeded for several
experiments.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

For 2017 testing of the F-T catalyst, Southern Research modified a reactor system previously
tested at the NCCC, increasing the reactor diameter from two inches to four inches. The
previous testing demonstrated catalyst activity of greater than 0.7 grams of C5+ hydrocarbons
per gram of catalyst per hour (gC5+/gcat/hr) and product selectivity of greater than 65% jet fuel.
The modifications were made to further demonstrate scalability of the reactor heat removal
technology. This work required redesigns of the feed, sampling, and cooling systems. As
expected, temperature control was more difficult, but Southern Research successfully tested the
catalyst for 125 hours. Southern Research’s thermo-syphon system maintained reactor operating
temperature along the axis to within ±5 C degrees (2.6 F degrees). Results indicated similar
catalyst productivity and liquid hydrocarbon selectivity but with higher jet fuel selectivity.
Liquid hydrocarbon distribution measurements for the samples indicated the jet fuel-range
hydrocarbon selectivity was 75% with almost no hydrocarbons above C22 detected.

The most notable operational issue that occurred was an abrupt loss of carbon monoxide (CO) on
the F-T process followed by a later abrupt restart of CO that caused a temperature runaway,
damaging about 50% of the F-T catalyst. The operating teams were required to develop new
control strategies to operate without constant risk of temperature runaways.

For reformer testing, Southern Research modified a lab-scale steam reformer skid for operation
in a classified industrial environment. The reformer system consisted of two parallel flow paths:
a 1-slpm stream routed to the reformer reactor vessel containing 10 grams of catalyst, and a
10-slpm stream used for syngas characterization using an online gas chromatograph.

Southern Research successfully operated the reformer skid for 125 hours on syngas. Results
indicated high conversion of methane (up to 90%) and near-complete conversion of tar and
ammonia in the presence of 380 ppm H₂S. Testing demonstrated a strong effect of temperature
on reforming catalyst performance, as a temperature drop of 50°C (122°F) decreased methane
conversion from 90 to 60%. However, the conversion rate quickly recovered when the
temperature was again increased, as demonstrated in Figure 69. Southern Research also
demonstrated a key goal during the experiments by controlling the hydrogen-to-carbon
monoxide ratio exiting the reformer at 2-to-1 by manipulating the steam flow rate to the reactor.
Catalyst performance did not indicate any damage by contaminants.

 137

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 69. Results of Southern Research Reformer Testing

4.3 Johnson Matthey Mercury Sorbent

In collaboration with NETL, Johnson Matthey has been developing a palladium-based sorbent to
remove mercury and other trace contaminants, such as arsenic and selenium, at high temperature
in coal gasification processes. Compared to low-temperature capture by activated carbon, high-
temperature capture of these trace elements retains the high thermal efficiency of coal
gasification power generation. The sorbent has demonstrated greater than 99% capture of
mercury, arsenic, and selenium.

Testing of palladium on alumina sorbents for the removal of trace contaminants from several
types of coal-derived syngas at elevated temperatures and pressures was conducted. Between 96
and 100% removal of mercury, arsenic, and selenium from all syngas types, sour and sweet, was
observed at 500°F and pressures of 150-200 psig. The results indicate that the Pd sorbents
exhibit large capacities for the capture of Hg, Se, and As under varying conditions and over
extended test periods, that the sorbent is regenerable, and that the sorbent is just as effective at
capturing these contaminants after regeneration

Testing at the NCCC began in 2008, with the most recent testing conducted during run G-4, for a
total of about 3,760 hours. Table 19 lists the test runs and duration for the mercury sorbent. To
optimize the sorbent, palladium loadings of 2 wt% and 5 wt% have been tested for performance

 138

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

comparisons. Test conditions for the mercury sorbent are given in Table 20. Initially, the syngas
flow rate was limited to about 40 lb/hr. Prior to the R10 run, the NCCC installed a new reactor
to accommodate a syngas flow rate of 50 lb/hr.

 Table 19. Summary of Johnson Matthey Mercury Sorbent Testing
                                    Run             G-1       G-2          G-3/G-4
                            Hours Tested            556       680             1,167

During the runs, gas samples from the sorbent bed inlet and outlet were collected using a
modified EPA Method 29 for trace metal analysis. Analyses were carried out primarily for
mercury, arsenic, and selenium. Typical results showed mercury inlet concentrations between
5 and 10 µg/Nm3 and outlet concentrations for mercury, arsenic, and selenium below detection
limits.

Following the runs, the sorbent was removed from the reactor in layers and returned to Johnson
Matthey for elemental analyses to determine contaminant penetration levels. Johnson Matthey’s
sorbent analyses support near-complete absorption of mercury, as the penetration of mercury and
other trace metals was limited to the first layers of the sorbent bed.

4.4 MHI Water-Gas Shift and COS Hydrolysis Catalysts

MHI developed a WGS catalyst designed to operate at a steam-to-CO ratio as low as 0.8 while
achieving conversion of 60% or greater. In conjunction with this project, MHI developed a COS
hydrolysis catalyst with a honeycomb configuration. Both catalysts were subjected to parametric
and long-term durability testing.

The WGS catalyst offers the following advantages:

 • Manufactured in a pelletized form and available as sour or sweet shift catalyst
 • High performance in the low temperature range of 300 to 480°F
 • High durability for sulfur loading (applicable for both sour and sweet conditions)
 • Low steam-to-CO ratio requirement
 • Applicable for a wide range of sulfur loading
Table 21 lists the operating conditions for WGS catalyst testing.

Figure 70 provides a summary of the parametric test results with a chart of CO conversion ratio
versus steam-to-CO ratio. Although there was no additional steam injection, the steam-to-CO
ratio varied depending on the fuel type and gasifier operating conditions. The curve of
equilibrium CO conversion ratio at 400°F is shown in the chart. The CO conversion values with
the lower syngas feed rate of 25 lb/hr were close to the equilibrium values. However, the data
for the operating temperatures of 410 and 427°F, which were taken at a syngas feed rate of
50 lb/hr, showed lower CO conversion due to the higher space velocity. The testing confirmed
that the catalyst showed acceptably high activity at temperatures (400 to 430°F) lower than those
required by conventional WGS catalysts.

During the contract period, the WGS catalyst operated for 3,352 hours, bringing the total
operational time to about 7,600 hours since testing began in 2011. Figure 71 presents the long-
term durability results for the total operation time. The CO conversion rate was stable and over
60% at steam/CO ratio of 0.6 to 1.5 during the PRB coal testing from 2011 to 2014,
corresponding to the beginning of testing up to about 5,000 hours. During the 2015 testing, over
the next 680 hours, the conversion decreased by 55% due to an occurrence of oxygen
breakthrough. During the final test, beginning at hour 6,800, the catalyst performance indicated
degradation, presumably due to a temperature excursion to 1,500°F caused by an equipment
failure. Near the end of final test, the catalyst performance improved, and the test was
completed.

4.5 Emerson Sapphire Thermowell for Gasifier Service

Rosemount sapphire thermowells, supplied by Emerson Process Management, were tested in the
gasifier during the G-2 run. The thermowells, which are designed to withstand erosive
environments and thermal stress, were placed in three different sections of the gasifier (see ) with
varying degrees of solids flow rates and temperature fluctuations. Emerson model numbers
TCPL-R-1782 and TCPL-R-2002, designed to provide temperature measurements up to 1300°C
(2400°F), were used. This testing was an expansion on previous testing at the NCCC beginning
in 2010, during which a single thermowell was installed in the gasifier riser above the coal feed
nozzles for several runs.

The sapphire-housed thermocouple installed in the riser above coal feed nozzles was operable
throughout the run. As shown in Figure 75, the temperatures recorded from this thermocouple
were compared to a reference thermocouple with an HR-160 thermowell (the standard type used
in the gasifier) located nearby in the same temperature region. The difference in the temperature
readings was 65F° (36C°) on average. Although the sapphire thermowell unit gave lower
measurements than the reference thermocouple, it showed good responsiveness and agreement
with temperature changes.

4.6 Ohio State University Syngas Chemical Looping

OSU’s SCL process uses counter-current moving beds and iron-based composite oxygen carriers
under reduction-oxidation conditions, converting coal-derived syngas into carbon-free energy
carriers for electricity or hydrogen production. SCL has been successfully demonstrated at
bench and sub-pilot scales and was tested at the NCCC at a 250-kW pilot scale utilizing up to
900 lb/hr of syngas.

During the G-2 run, OSU conducted startup activities of the SCL process over a 120-hour test
run. The SCL pilot test unit was heated to an operating temperature of 870°C (1,600°F) at a
pressure of 75 psi. Initial syngas testing commenced with greater than 98% conversion of syngas
components (carbon monoxide, methane, and hydrogen) in the reducer reactor observed
throughout syngas operations. Control of the pressure seal and solids flow was maintained
throughout the startup and testing. Further syngas operation was prevented by issues with
fluidization, which required refractory repairs on the combustor vessel.

Following the repairs, OSU resumed testing during the G-5 run. OSU completed system heat-up
and operated the system with syngas feed while circulating oxygen carrier particles to prepare for
operation with steam and begin hydrogen production. The SCL process operated with steam for
a short period and produced hydrogen for the first time. Syngas operation was achieved for 33
hours, including a continuous period of 15 hours. The typical gas profile measured at the SCL

 144

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

reducer outlet during steady-state operation is shown in Figure 77. The majority of syngas was
converted into CO₂, with small amounts of carbon monoxide and hydrogen remaining
unconverted.

in the SCL reducer as well as the proper operation of the non-mechanical gas sealing device that

Operation of the SCL pilot unit ended on April 30 due to a mechanical failure of the secondary

of air into the NCCC cooling water system, which led to the shutdown of the cooling water
system and subsequently the gasifier system.

4.7 TDA Ammonia Sorbent

TDA Research completed the first NCCC testing of a sorbent-based gas cleanup technology to
remove ammonia and hydrogen cyanide as well as mercury and other trace metals from syngas at
high temperatures in a single process step. The sorbent is operated in a regenerable manner to
remove ammonia and mercury, while irreversibly absorbing hydrogen cyanide and other
contaminants. TDA’s cleanup system operates at 260°C (500°F) downstream of a warm gas
desulfurization system.

TDA operated the system for approximately 50 hours. Figure 80 shows the first three cycles of
the field test during which the sorbent achieved greater than 99% ammonia removal efficiency.
The capacity in Cycles 2 and 3 were lower due to the regeneration step being carried out under
pressure swing conditions instead of a combination of pressure and mild temperature swing

 Figure 80. TDA Research Ammonia Sorbent Results over Three Cycles

Figure 81 shows the ammonia concentration, temperature, and flow rate profiles during a typical
pressure swing cycle illustrating the ammonia removal and subsequent release. Ammonia
measurements were taken with a mass spectrometer and with Gastec detector tubes.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

4.8 Air Products Pressure Swing Adsorption

Air Products conducted testing during G-1 to evaluate the performance of a two-bed sour PSA
unit and to determine the stability of the PSA adsorbent. The test unit consisted of a pre-
treatment skid followed by a PSA skid. The pretreatment skid contained a guard bed used to
remove organic tar species from the raw syngas, a compressor to pressurize the clean sour syngas
to 24 bar, and a cooler/knockout assembly to remove water and other condensable syngas
components. The treated syngas was then passed to the sour PSA skid where it entered one of
two PSA vessels containing adsorbent for the removal of sulfur species and CO₂.

During the regeneration steps, the bed was depressurized, purged with product gas at
atmospheric pressure, and then re-pressurized with product gas. Gas exiting the bed during
regeneration was characterized. The PSA product gas composition was monitored during
cycling. Once stable, the inlet and outlet gas flow rates, composition, pressures, and
temperatures were measured during one cycle to provide data for comparing with process
simulations. The overall mass balance, hydrogen recovery, and hydrogen sulfide (H₂S) and CO₂
rejection were then calculated. A set of breakthrough tests were conducted before and after the
PSA cycles to determine if the adsorbent properties changed during the sour syngas exposure.
Breakthrough runs were conducted at 27.6 bar and 30°C (86°F) with a feed gas of 2% methane
in helium and with 0.5% H₂S in helium.

The PSA operated for a total of 12 days, for 1,460 PSA cycles, on the sour syngas. The guard
beds accepted sour syngas for a total of roughly 625 hours. In addition to processing sour
syngas, the PSA was operated for another 560 cycles on syngas that did not contain H₂S (before
breakthrough in the guard beds).

The overall mass balance was within 1%, while component balances were within about 8%. The
PSA rejected 99.7% of the H₂S and more than 95% of the CO₂ in the feed gas. Product gas

 148

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

purity was maintained at 1.4 ppm H₂S with a feed gas concentration of around 250 ppm. The
recovery of hydrogen to the product gas was 72.

The PSA system appeared to yield the same performance when similar process set points were
used at the beginning and end of the sour gas testing, suggesting that the adsorbent
characteristics remained stable through that period. After the syngas tests, another set of
breakthrough runs were conducted, and the results showed a modest 3% reduction in the
methane capacity and essentially no change in the H₂S capacity. These results support the
observation of stable cyclic PSA performance.

4.9 Membrane Technology & Research Hydrogen Membranes

MTR conducted testing of the Proteus hydrogen membranes during each of the gasification runs,
building on previous testing, which began in 2009. The operation employed 4-inch, semi-
commercial membrane modules and 2.5-inch membrane stamps. From 2009 to 2017, MTR
achieved 9,125 hours of testing with Proteus modules and membrane stamps.

Semi-Commercial 4-Inch Modules
Over the duration of testing semi-commercial Proteus modules at NCCC, iterative improvements
in module design and module components selection led to hydrogen enrichment from about 13%
in the feed syngas to more than 70% in the module permeate for a single stage. Hydrogen
spiking of the syngas was performed during some runs, and increasing the feed syngas hydrogen
content to 30% led to a permeate hydrogen concentration of greater than 90%.

Figure 82 shows the feed and permeate hydrogen concentrations for the Proteus semi-
commercial module tested during both the G-3/4 and G-5 runs. The data for the first 100 hours
of the G-5 campaign (hours 300 to 400 in the figure) are not shown due to inconsistent or
incomplete data.

  Figure 82. Hydrogen Concentrations of the Syngas Feed and Permeate Streams of a Proteus Semi-

 149

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Figure 82 demonstrates how sensitive Proteus membrane modules are to the hydrogen partial
pressure driving force. Between 400 and 450 hours of run time, the hydrogen permeate
concentration was around 50% before rising to 60% for virtually the rest of the G-5 run. The
reason for the difference in permeate hydrogen concentration is related to the hydrogen content
in the feed syngas, which varied based on the conversion rate of a water-gas shift reactor
upstream of the MTR membrane skid. Between 400 and 450 hours of run time, the average
hydrogen concentration in the syngas feed was 9.6%, while after 450 hours the average was
11.7%. The influence of the water-gas shift reaction conversion rate can also be seen in the
concentration of CO (5.2% vs 3.0%) or CO₂ (11.6% vs. 13.3%) in the feed syngas before and
after 450 hours of runtime.

Another example of the effect of the hydrogen partial pressure differential on membrane module
performance can be found towards the end of G-5. Between 640 hours and the end of the run,
the permeate hydrogen concentration dropped off considerably while the feed hydrogen content
was virtually unchanged. The drop in permeate hydrogen concentration was due to an increase
in the permeate pressure, which lowered the hydrogen partial pressure driving force across the
membrane by 85%. The permeate pressure increase was thought to be caused by the
accumulation of debris in the permeate piping.

While the hydrogen permeate concentration dropped from 70% during the G-3/4 run to 60% in
the G-5 run, the hydrogen enrichment performance of the module remained virtually unchanged.
Although PRB coal was used in both the G-3/4 and G-5 run, the average hydrogen concentration
in the feed gas was 13.0% in G-3/G-4 but was 11.4% during G-5. During these runs, the Proteus
semi-commercial module enriched hydrogen five-fold. The consistent enrichment showed that
the module remained defect-free with no deterioration in performance over multiple test
campaigns.

Membrane Stamps
The membrane stamp testing involved Proteus membranes of second-generation (Gen-2)
material, which MTR is developing specifically for operation up to 200°C (392°F). During the
testing, the Gen-2 membrane stamps with higher selectivity were stable up to 190°C (375°F) and
demonstrated improved hydrogen enrichment (from 12% in the feed syngas to 85% in the
permeate) compared to earlier membrane versions.

During the G-2 run, MTR conducted parametric tests with the 2.5-inch membrane stamp. For
stage cut (changing syngas feed flow rate to the module) experiments, the feed pressure
(170 psig), system temperature (120°C or 248°F), and permeate pressure (5 psig) were held
constant. The feed flow rate was varied to meet the desired module stage cut, which is defined
as the total amount of gas permeating the module relative to the feed gas flow rate. Figure 83
shows the permeate concentration of the small module over a stage cut range of 5 to 50%.
Consistent with membrane module process design fundamentals, the bulk removal of hydrogen
increased while the hydrogen purity in the permeate stream decreased with increasing stage cut.

points. Figure 84 shows the permeate stream composition for different pressure ratios. As the
pressure ratio increases, the driving force for hydrogen permeation increases, and because the
Proteus membrane is selective to hydrogen over the other present gas species, the hydrogen
permeate concentration also increases.

4.10 Media & Process Technology Hydrogen Membrane

MPT’s concept for hydrogen separation for IGCC systems with CO₂ capture involves a dual-
stage membrane process. The first stage uses MPT’s CMS to separate 75 – 85% of the hydrogen
from the syngas after pre-treatment in a WGS reactor. A palladium-based membrane recovers
the residual hydrogen in the CO₂-rich retentate stream following cold gas cleanup and
compression prior to CO₂ sequestration.

In previous testing, MPT produced hydrogen with greater than 99% purity using the CMS along
with a downstream single-tube palladium membrane. During the contract period, MPT operated
the CMS for a short time to optimize the CMS design (during run G-1), installed and validated
an in-situ syngas water measurement (runs G-3/G-4), and scaled up the palladium membrane to a
multi-tube bundle and operated it independently (run G-5).

The test objectives for the G-5 run were to evaluate the physical integrity of the palladium
membrane bundle, determine the impact of syngas contaminants in the presence of tar-like
species, and assess the membrane’s ability to be regenerated. To achieve these objectives, MPT
used as the feed gas syngas that was pre-treated with water-gas shifting and desulfurization but
not treated to remove tars.

 152

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Prior to operation, modifications were made to the system to allow in-situ oxidation and
regeneration of the membranes, which comprised brief exposure to air at 310°C (590°F). The
G-5 testing included 156 hours of syngas permeation run time and 176 hours of membrane
regeneration. Three membrane bundles were tested, each consisting of 12 tubes, which were
12 inches in length. Each bundle was oxidized in-situ before syngas feed was started.

Bundle A was a relatively low selectivity bundle intended for initial testing while proving out the
system prior to testing higher quality bundles. Figure 86 plots the overall hydrogen separation
performance, which was low for a palladium-based membrane. At hours 5 through 7 and 42, the
bundle was bypassed. Following in-situ oxidation at hour 44, the performance improved
slightly, although the hydrogen purity remained below 50%. Pure hydrogen permeance values
taken intermittently during the test period confirm very low hydrogen permeation rates in the
range of 300 to 550 GPU.

  Figure 86. Hydrogen Concentration of Feed and Permeate Streams of MPT’s Membrane Bundle A

Bundle B was a higher quality bundle which showed hydrogen permeance values between 3,200
and 4,000 GPU and a separation factor for hydrogen/nitrogen of approximately 1,800 prior to
G-5 testing. Figure 87 plots the overall gas separation performance of this bundle. The initial
pure hydrogen permeance ranged from 350 to 500 GPU, indicating that the initial in-situ
oxidation did not fully remove all the in-system tar and any other contaminants on the membrane
surfaces. In-situ oxidation at hours 74, 80, and 85 yielded modest improvements in pure
hydrogen permeance (up to 700 GPU) and slight improvement in syngas separation performance.

 153

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

  Figure 87. Hydrogen Concentration of Feed and Permeate Streams of MPT’s Membrane Bundle B

Testing of the third bundle, Bundle C, was limited by the unexpected early shut down of the
gasifier, and thus not enough data was generated for comparison of this bundle with the others.
Prior to testing, the hydrogen permeances of Bundle C ranged from 2,750 to 3,500 GPU, and
during G-5 operation, the permeance ranged from 400 to 625 GPU.

Though in-situ regeneration was attempted for restoration of membrane activity, the presence of
tar in the syngas and residual tar in the system prevented measurements of true hydrogen
permeance. However, MPT demonstrated that the permeance could be restored in the lab with
air treatment at raised temperatures. Following the G-5 testing, three membranes were cut from
Bundles A and B for this regeneration testing. Table 23 lists the hydrogen permeance values at
the beginning and end of the tests. The regenerated membranes maintained their original
permeance for an extended period in the lab.

4.11 SRI Polybenzimidazole Hydrogen Membrane

SRI has been developing a hydrogen membrane fabricated with spun hollow fibers of the
temperature- and chemical-resistant polymer polybenzimidazole (PBI). The membrane can
operate at temperatures up to 250°C (482°F), allowing the CO₂ to be captured at gasifier pressure
(up to 40 bar, or 580 psi). The membrane skid installed at the NCCC is shown in Figure 88.
Testing was performed with two modules, one with first-generation (Gen-1) fibers, and one with
Gen-2 fibers. Operation was conducted at a 50-kWth scale using syngas and syngas
supplemented with hydrogen and CO₂. The Gen-1 module operated continuously for 500 hours
at varying temperatures and pressures to evaluate its performance as a function of stage cut and
different hydrogen and CO₂ concentrations. In parallel, SRI tested the Gen-2 module for 48
hours to measure its selectivity.

 Figure 88. SRI PBI Membrane Skid

Figure 89 shows that the hydrogen/CO₂ selectivity in the Gen-2 module increased with
increasing temperature. This behavior is a unique property of the PBI hollow-fiber membranes;
conventional polymer membranes show the opposite effect. Figure 89 also shows the
comparison of the selectivity data for the Gen-1 fibers and the data from the one-inch module
testing at SRI facilities with Gen-1 fibers. The 1-inch Gen-1 data showed that operating
temperatures above 190°C (374°F) would be required to achieve a hydrogen/CO₂ selectivity of
25. In comparison, the Gen-2 module achieved a selectivity of 25 at temperatures below 140°C.
The NCCC field test conditions confirmed that the Gen-2 fibers are superior.

 155

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 89. Comparison of Hydrogen/CO₂ Selectivity for GEN-1 and GEN-2 PBI Modules

The module with Gen-1 fibers was tested with varying stage cuts and supplied with either a
syngas only feed or a syngas feed augmented with additional hydrogen and CO₂. The overall
observed performance data is given in Figure 90. As expected, the hydrogen recovery was
greater (about 99%) at the higher stage cut (0.6), while the CO₂ recovery was higher (greater
than 90%) at the lower stage cut (0.4). The data also showed that, at temperatures above 150°C
(302°F), the hydrogen recovery depended mostly on the operating stage cut. The modules tested
were constructed with a dead-end design, i.e., fiber shell side gas feed with one end of fiber bore-
side sealed in the potted module. SRI expects much improved performance for the membranes
potted with both bore-side ends open to allow a bore-side flow-through configuration enabling
the use of permeate sweep gas to further optimize hydrogen recovery and CO₂ capture.

 Figure 90. Hydrogen Recovery with Varying Stage Cuts

 156

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

4.12 TDA Research CO₂ Sorbent Systems

TDA Research is advancing a sorbent-based pre-combustion CO₂ capture technology to remove
more than 90% of CO₂ from shifted syngas at relatively low cost. The sorbent is a proprietary
carbon-based material modified with surface functional groups to physically adsorb CO₂ from
shifted syngas. The CO₂ capture system operates at relatively high temperature (greater than
400oF) and uses multiple beds to operate based on PSA and regeneration.

Two systems were tested at the NCCC. In the 50 lb/hr CO₂ sorbent combined with WGS project
the 8-bed PSA system houses both the WGS catalyst and the CO₂ capture sorbent and includes
some heat management concepts included to keep the bed temperature under control. In the
0.1-MW system, the WGS reaction occurred in two stages upstream of the 8-bed PSA system
(i.e., three separate process units, including high-temperature WGS, low-temperature WGS, and
an 8-bed CO₂ capture unit). Both the 0.1 MW and 50 lb/hr sorbent plus shift project use the
same CO₂ capture sorbent. However, the difference between them is in the process
intensification achieved by reducing the number of process units to two and having advanced
reactor design concepts in the PSA beds to simultaneously carry out the exothermic WGS
reaction and CO₂ capture.

G-2 Operation
During the G-2 run, a four-bed unit containing the combined WGS/CO₂ removal and thermal
management system was tested. The goal of the testing was to demonstrate the key aspects of
the combined shift and CO₂ separation system, such as CO conversion, overall carbon capture,
temperature rise in the beds, and the functionality of the thermal management system while using
coal-derived syngas. The testing apparatus was operated for over 90 hours on syngas during
which several parameters were explored such as altering the inlet CO concentration and varying
the water injection rate while observing their effects on the bed temperature, carbon removal, and
CO conversion.

TDA successfully demonstrated the integrated WGS catalyst/CO₂ removal sorbent operation, as
well as the thermal management system in controlling the reactor temperatures during adsorption
and WGS. Shown in Figure 91, the amount of CO entering the adsorption/WGS beds was
increased from 1.5 mol% to 4.0 mol%; during this time, the amount of water injected into the
reactors was increased from 1.0 to 5.0 mL/min. The energy used to convert the injected water
into steam was significant enough to maintain the reactor bed temperatures despite the increase
in WGS activity. During the entire testing period, the system averaged greater than 97% carbon
capture. The average CO conversion in the reactor beds was greater than 76%.

 157

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Figure 91. Effects of Increasing CO Inlet Concentration on Bed Temperature

G-3/G-4 Operation
During G-3/G-4, TDA operated the WGS catalyst/CO₂ removal sorbent system for over
650 hours on syngas. TDA conducted additional parametric tests, altering the reactor inlet CO
concentration and varying the injected water rates while observing their effects on bed
temperature, carbon removal, and CO conversion.

TDA demonstrated that reactor bed temperatures can be precisely controlled by direct water
injection, achieving a temperature decrease of 20 C° (54 F°) throughout the bed when steam
addition was stopped, and direct water injection began. To investigate the effect of water
injection on CO conversion and sorbent capacity, water was injected to achieve an H₂O-to-CO
ratio of three. During this period, average CO conversion was 90%, and the average sorbent
capacity was 5.5 g of CO₂ adsorbed per liter of sorbent. For a direct comparison, a similar
amount of steam was added in the inlet syngas stream to achieve an H₂O-to-CO ratio of three or
more. The results showed that the CO conversion was always lower when steam was added to
the inlet stream compared to direct water injection into the reactors.

To assess the effect of inlet CO concentration on CO conversion and ultimately overall carbon
capture, inlet CO concentrations were altered by adjusting how much of the syngas feed was pre-
treated in an upstream water-gas shift reactor. As shown in Figure 92, the inlet CO was varied
between 3 and 9%. During the periods when the CO inlet was below 5.5%, the system was able
to achieve 90% CO conversion, resulting in a carbon capture rate of greater than 95%. At CO
concentrations above 5.5%, the system had difficulty achieving high CO conversions, resulting
in a lower carbon capture rate of 85 – 90%.

 158

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 Run Time, hr

 Figure 92. Operational Data from TDA Research WGS/CO₂ Sorbent Unit

Test Run G-5 Operation
For G-5 testing, TDA’s test unit consisted of eight beds and the subsystems needed to support
the operation of the full PSA cycle. The sorbent test skid was designed to treat up to 2,000 slpm
of raw syngas. The pilot-scale evaluation system also included a gas conditioning skid,
consisting of desulfurization sorbent beds, water-gas shift reactors (both high temperature and
low temperature) and a trace contaminant removal bed (mainly for mercury and arsenic).

TDA varied the main parameters along the range of operation shown in Table 24. For these
parameters, 75 unique combinations were tested. The robust control system allowed for wide
range of testing, which permitted fine tuning of the performance for each of the cycle sequences.
TDA achieved 740 hours of testing.

4.13 Worcester Polytechnic Institute Hydrogen Membrane

The Center for Inorganic Membrane Studies at WPI has been developing a high-temperature
palladium-based membrane to separate hydrogen from syngas for power generation. WPI tested
13 palladium and palladium-alloy pilot-scale membranes during eight test runs (runs R06
through R13) at the NCCC. Testing was conducted at 450°C (842°F) using single membrane
modules in nitrogen/hydrogen (N2/H2) bottle gas mixtures and in syngas. All membranes were
fabricated with a porous surface area about 200 cm2, having a one-inch outer diameter and ten-
inch-long porous stainless steel seamless tubular supports from Mott Corporation. The total
testing time for all 13 membranes was 4,275 hours, representing about a half of a year of
operation. Testing achieved 1.4 lb/day of hydrogen production and 99.85 – 99.95% hydrogen
purity at 450°C (842°F) and 12.6 bar under actual syngas feed conditions.

In continued development of its palladium-based hydrogen membranes, WPI tested a multi-tube
membrane module during G-2. The module was a seven-fold scale-up of previous testing, and
an intermediate design for further planned scale-up. The multi-tube module was composed of
seven palladium-gold composite membranes, with a total permeable area of 1,050 cm2. Each
tube was fabricated with a dense inner layer of palladium about 5.4 microns thick, a second layer
of 0.4 microns of gold for improved stability and recoverability, and a final layer of palladium of
about 1.6 microns. With only 1% deviation in thickness among the tubes, the consistent
fabrication of the membrane tubes demonstrated the replicability of this technology. Lab testing
performed prior to the gasification run showed a cumulative helium leak rate of less than
0.01 cm3/min/bar.

The performance of the membrane module was evaluated based on its hydrogen permeance,
hydrogen product purity, and hydrogen recovery. Hydrogen permeance is defined as:

 Permeate H2 Flow Rate
 𝐻𝐻2 Permeance=
 Surface Area (�pH2,avg -�pH2,permeate )

where pH2,avg and pH2,permeate are the partial pressures of hydrogen in the module and in the
permeate, respectively.

 161

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

Hydrogen purity is calculated as the flow rate of the permeate hydrogen divided by the flow rate
of the retentate. Hydrogen recovery is defined as the difference between the hydrogen feed flow
rate and the hydrogen permeate flow rate divided by the hydrogen feed flow rate.

During G-2, the module operated at a temperature of 450°C (842°F) and a pressure of 12.6 bar
(183 psig) with 10 lb/hr of syngas enriched with bottle hydrogen to achieve an inlet hydrogen
concentration of 34%. Testing was conducted continuously for 837 hours, with 207 hours under
hydrogen/nitrogen mixtures and 630 hours under syngas conditions. The module was in
excellent condition following the testing.

Figure 95 plots the hydrogen permeance and purity of the module as a function of time. Initially,
the membrane module showed a permeance in the range of 7 to 10 Nm3/m2/h/bar0.5 (6 to
8.6 scfh/ft2/psi0.5) when tested under a hydrogen/nitrogen mixture as depicted in the figure with
solid black circles. When syngas feed to the module began, a slight increase in permeance was
observed, followed by steady performance. The maximum flux achieved by the seven
membranes was 6 lb hydrogen/day. For comparison, an eighth membrane of the same
fabrication tested with pure hydrogen in WPI labs showed hydrogen permeance of
80 Nm3/m2/h/bar0.5 (69 scfh/ft2/psi0.5), equivalent to 1.03 lb hydrogen/day at a pressure difference
of 0.35 bar.

 Figure 95. Hydrogen Permeance and Purity Profiles of the WPI Membrane Module

The hydrogen purity produced by these membranes began at 99.5% with hydrogen/nitrogen feed.
When syngas feed began, the purity increased to 99.6% and was as high as 99.9% after 67 hours
of operation. The product purity then decreased steadily, reaching a minimum purity of 98.8%
after 670 hours. At the end of the test, the hydrogen purity was 98.1% with hydrogen/nitrogen
feed.

Figure 96 shows the hydrogen recovery achieved as a function of time. When using
hydrogen/nitrogen feed at the beginning of the test, the recovery reached 52%. Under syngas
conditions, the hydrogen recovery decreased to 35% and remained fairly constant throughout the

 162

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

test. The maximum theoretical hydrogen recovery achievable by the module is greater than 90%
and can be further improved by the presence of sweep gas in the permeate side and/or by
increasing the retentate pressure.

 Figure 96. Hydrogen Recovery Profile of the WPI Membrane Module

4.14 Carbon Clean Solutions CO₂ Solvent

The Carbon Clean Solutions solvent was tested in the bench-scale solvent batch reactor with
bottle gas and with syngas. Initial bottle gas testing was conducted at temperatures ranging from
45 to 120°C (113 to 248°F) and CO₂ concentrations ranging 20 to 80 mol% to obtain vapor-
liquid equilibrium data. After the completion of bottle gas testing, tests were performed with
syngas during the G-3/G-4 runs. Total gas flow to the batch reactor was maintained at 6 lb/hr
with various amounts of CO₂ enrichment added to elevate the CO₂ concentration for certain tests.
Each test was run until the inlet and outlet CO₂ concentrations reached equilibrium. Syngas
testing could only occur at lower partial pressures due to the limit of the syngas supply pressure
and operable range of the syngas flow control valve. Figure 97 plots the results for the bottle gas
and syngas testing.

Figure 97. Results of Carbon Clean Solutions Solvent Testing with Bottle Gas at Various Temperatures
 and with Syngas

Data was then used to compare the Carbon Clean Solutions solvent with two solvents widely
used in high pressure commercial applications—activated MDEA and Selexol. Figure 98 gives
the vapor-liquid equilibrium curves for the Carbon Clean Solutions solvent, activated MDEA
(aMDEA), and DEPG (used to represent Selexol). The Carbon Clean Solutions solvent
compared favorably to the commercial solvents, achieving significantly higher CO₂ loading
values. Using the vapor-liquid equilibrium data, Carbon Clean Solutions estimated that the heat
of reaction for the Carbon Clean Solutions solvent was 39.2 kJ/mol CO₂, 20% lower than that of
MDEA (48.9 kJ/mol CO₂).

Although it was found during syngas testing that the Carbon Clean Solutions solvent could be
completely regenerated for both CO₂ and H₂S, the vapor-liquid equilibrium values for the
solvent/H₂S system were not determined, as were the values for the solvent and CO₂ system.
Carbon Clean Solutions planned to perform H₂S vapor-liquid equilibrium experiments at another
facility.

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

4.15 SRI Solvent and Bechtel Pressure Swing Claus Processes

SRI developed an innovative, low-cost CO₂ capture technology based on absorption with a high
capacity and low-cost aqueous ammoniated solution with a high-pressure absorber and stripper.
The AC-ABC process relies on the ability of the aqueous ammoniated solution to absorb CO₂
and H₂S at near ambient temperatures and to release it as a high-pressure gas at a moderately
elevated temperature. SRI, in collaboration with EIG Inc., and Bechtel Hydrocarbon Technology
Solutions Inc., led the effort to develop the AC-ABC solvent process, as well as to develop the
Bechtel Pressure Swing Claus (BPSC) process for converting H₂S to elemental sulfur.

The 500-lb/hr pilot-scale AC-ABC reactor system was installed at the NCCC with absorber and
regenerator columns and associated pumps and instrumentation for simultaneous capture of CO₂
and H₂S and for regeneration of CO₂ and H₂S at high pressure. The BPSC process was installed
downstream of SRI’s with a liquid SO₂ supply, catalytic reactors, and heat exchangers to convert
H₂S to elemental sulfur. The AC-ABC process was operated with syngas produced from
gasification of lignite, which contained about 2,000 ppmv H₂S, and which was pre-treated with
WGS and hydrocarbon removal. The syngas feed was composed of 14 – 15% CO₂, 1% CO,
12% hydrogen, 0.5% methane, and the balance of nitrogen.

During G-2, more than 300 hours of data was collected from the integrated operation of the
AC-ABC system, including a continuous run of 100 hours. The operation demonstrated
simultaneous capture of CO₂ and H₂S, with capture efficiencies of each greater than 99%,
simultaneous stripping of CO₂ and H₂S at high pressure up to 350 psig, and low ammonia losses
from the system. The BPSC system operated for 90 hours and converted the H₂S in the
AC-ABC outlet CO₂ stream to sulfur, producing an H₂S-free CO₂ stream. Based on the G-2
operation, several modifications were made, several of which are described below.

 • The absorber top stage initially contained bubble cap trays to control ammonia emissions,
 although flooding and solvent carryover occurred during G-2, so the bubble cap trays
 were replaced by structural packing. However, due to low liquid loads, the structural
 packing was not effective. The third stage of the absorber was bypassed, and the
 absorber was operated with two working stages. Test results showed that capture
 efficiency was still very high with only two operating stages. Ammonia emission from
 the absorber was measured at 500 to 1,000 ppm under steady operating conditions. In a
 commercial system, ammonia would be recovered in a sour-water stripper.
 • Steam-cleaning valves were added to the piping in which clogging was experienced due
 to process upset conditions during the first test campaign. This enabled faster turnaround
 between test runs and resulted in greater than 80% availability for the pilot plant during
 the second test campaign.
 • Viton diaphragms on the solvent circulation pump were replaced with ethylene propylene
 diene monomer material, which is more chemically compatible with sulfides in the
 solvent.
 • The SO₂ delivery system was modified, and a pulsation dampener was added to the SO₂
 line to reduce flow fluctuations due to the diaphragm pump. A new SO₂ vaporizer was
 installed to convert liquid SO₂ to vapor. Under the modified arrangement for SO₂

 166

National Carbon Capture Center Final Report: June 6, 2014 – March 31, 2026

 delivery, a recirculation loop was added to the pump and a Coriolis-based flow control
 was designed to withdraw the desired amount of liquid from the loop. The performance
 of SO₂ delivery system improved, but further improvements were needed.
The modifications resulted in significantly improved operation during the second test campaign
in G-4, with longer continuous operation (175 hours of 400-hour total testing time) and higher
CO₂ loading in the solution (120 g CO₂/L solution compared to 100 g CO₂/L solution in G-2).
Figure 99 plots data from the period of continuous operation. With two stages operating, the
absorber achieved greater than 99.5% CO₂ capture and H₂S capture in a single vessel. During a
period from 30 to 84 hours, the capture efficiencies for both CO₂ and H₂S were marginally lower
due to low ammonia concentration in the solvent. Fresh ammonia solution was added to the
system, which resulted in higher capture efficiencies.

 Figure 99. Capture Efficiency and CO₂ Loading in Solvent for SRI AC-ABC Process

The BPSC process operated for 131 hours during G-4. The regenerated gas stream, CO₂, and
H₂S from the AC-ABC system, was fed to the BPSC skid at 350 psig, and bottle SO₂ was
injected into the feed stream in a stoichiometric ratio. The operation showed that the BPSC
technology can convert the gas stream of 99.5 vol% H₂S to elemental sulfur with H₂S in the
product gas at ppm levels. Sulfur formed on the catalyst at sub-dewpoint conditions according to
the thermodynamic concept during the absorption step. Although no long-term data was
collected, the unit recovered the sulfur as a high-purity liquid, validating the underlying design
concepts.
